<meta>This page begins the Biographical Preface to the works of Thomas Vaughan, also known by the pseudonym Eugenius Philalethes. It follows a blank page in the original volume.</meta>

# BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap "T" featuring intricate floral and vine patterns with two small bird-like figures at the top corners.</image-desc>

THE long confusion of Eugenius Philalethes—otherwise known as Thomas Vaughan—with the anonymous, world-traveling expert <note>original: "cosmopolite adept"</note>, Eirenæus Philalethes, has cast much doubt upon the history and identity of the Welsh initiate. Eirenæus is said to have accomplished the Great Work <note>original: "Magnum Opus"</note> at the age of twenty-two and to have subsequently wandered over a large portion of the habitable globe, performing astounding transmutations under various names and disguises. To clear this up, it is best to present the reader with certain exact citations from the chief authority concerning Vaughan, which is the *Athenae Oxonienses* <note>original: "Athenæ"</note> by Anthony à Wood.

> “Thomas Vaughan, who styles himself in all or most of the writings which he published as Eugenius Philalethes, was the son of Thomas Vaughan of Llansantffraed <note>original: "Llansomfreid"</note>. He was born at Newton, in the parish of St. Bridget, near Brecknock in Brecknockshire, in 1621. He was educated in grammar under one Matthew Herbert and entered Jesus College in the Michaelmas term of 1638. He was placed under the instruction of a notable tutor; benefiting greatly from his lectures, he earned a degree in the arts and was made a fellow of the college. Afterward, taking holy orders from Dr. Manwaring, the Bishop of St. David’s, he had the rectory of the aforementioned St. Bridget conferred upon him around that time by his kinsman, Sir George Vaughan. But the instability of the times <note>Vaughan's career was interrupted by the English Civil War; he was eventually evicted from his rectory by Parliamentarians.</note> prevented him from peacefully holding the position. He left it and retired to Oxford where, in quiet seclusion, he pursued his medical inclination <note>original: "medicinal geny"</note>, which was natural to him. He eventually became eminent in the chemical <term>In the 17th century, "chymical" or "chemical" often referred specifically to alchemical medicine.</term> branch of that field at Oxford, and afterwards in London, under the protection and patronage of the noted chemist Sir Robert Murray (or Moray), a knight and secretary of state for the kingdom of Scotland. . . . He was a great chemist, a noted alchemist <note>original: "son of the fire"</note>, an experimental philosopher, and a zealous brother of the Rosy Cross— <meta>catchword: Rosie-</meta>

<summary>This page begins a biographical sketch of Thomas Vaughan, distinguishing him from the alchemist Eirenæus Philalethes and citing Anthony à Wood's account of his early life, education, and career.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Eugenius Philalethes, Eirenæus Philalethes, Anthony à Wood, alchemy, Rosicrucianism, 17th-century biography, Oxford University</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Thomas Vaughan, drawing from the writings of Anthony à Wood and later historians, detailing his death and his expulsion from his parish.</meta>

# BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.

Rosicrucian <note>original: "Crucian"</note> fraternity,\* a student <note>original: "understander"</note> of some of the Oriental languages, and a reasonably good English and Latin poet. He was neither a Catholic <note>original: "papist"</note> nor a member of a non-conforming sect <note>original: "sectary"</note>, but a true, resolute Protestant in the best sense of the Church of England. . . . He accompanied the previously mentioned Sir Robert Murrey to Oxford <note>original: "Oxon"</note> at the time the Great Plague in London drove their Majesties and their respective courts to that place, where he stayed for a time. Soon after, taking up residence <note>original: "taking up his quarters"</note> in the house of Samuel Kem, rector of Albury, near Thame and Ricot in Oxfordshire, [he] died there quite suddenly while experimenting with <note>original: "operating"</note> strong mercury. Some of it accidentally went up into his nose and killed him,† on the 27th of February in 1665, and he was buried on the first of March following in the church belonging to the said village of Albury, otherwise known as Oldbury (about 8 miles distant from Oxford), through the care and expense of the said Sir Robert Murrey.”‡

The history of Brecknockshire, written by Theophilus Jones in the year 1809, speaks of a farmhouse at Newton which was once quite famous, being “occupied by two brothers named Vaughan, who were very eccentric characters,” but for the facts of the life of Eugenius, this work is mainly indebted to Wood. It lists, however, the alleged reasons for the expulsion of Thomas Vaughan from his church position.

> “He was ousted by the propagators of the gospel in Wales for drunkenness, swearing, sexual immorality <note>original: "incontinency"</note>, and carrying arms for the king.”§

This information, together with a few scraps which may be gathered from Elias Ashmole, has been the total sum of our knowledge concerning the Welsh Royalist, who is now counted among the first of British mystics and Hermetic <term>adepts</term> <note>An "adept" is a person who is highly skilled or proficient in a particular subject, specifically here referring to a master of alchemy and esoteric philosophy.</note>. After much research, I am fortunately able to supplement it from an unexpected source. I have discovered a genuine original manuscript, which bears on its flyleaf the inscription, “From the books of Thomas and Rebecca Vaughan, September 28, 1651. What God has joined together, let no man separate? <note>original: "Ex Libris Thomas et Rebecca Vaughan, 1651, Sept. 28. Quos Deus conjunxit quis separabit?"</note>” It is entitled “*Water of Life, Not of the Vine* <note>original: "AQUA VITÆ NON VITIS"</note>, or the Radical Moisture <note>original: "Humiditie"</note> of Nature Mechanically and Magically Dissected by the conduct of Fire."

---

\* This statement is twice expressly contradicted by Vaughan himself, who says in his Preface to the “*Fame and Confession of the Fraternity of R. C.*”: “As for that Fraternity, whose History and Confession I have here ventured to publish, I have, for my own part, no relation to them, nor do I much desire their acquaintance.” And again, “I have no acquaintance with this Fraternity as to their persons.”
† According to Mr. Harris of Jesus College. Wood, manuscript note in Ashmole.
‡ *Athenae Oxonienses* <note>original: "Athenæ Oxoniens."</note>, Edited by Philip Bliss, 4to, 1817, iii. 722.
§ “History of the County of Brecknock,” ii. part ii. p. 540.

<meta>This page continues the "Biographical Preface" of Thomas Vaughan (Eugenius Philalethes). It transitions from his life history to a description of his private notebooks, which include alchemical experiments and personal reflections.</meta>

<page-num>ix</page-num>
<header>BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.</header>

and Ferment;" but it is not a consecutive treatise. It is an occasional diary and a notebook of personal experiments in several classes of mystic physics <note>original: "mystic physics"; likely referring to alchemy and occult science.</note>. The daily memoranda are in English, and the thaumaturgic <note>original: "thaumaturgic"; miracle-working or magical.</note> recipes are in Latin and somewhat hard to understand. The latter, being numerous enough to form a small volume and presenting many obvious difficulties of interpretation, are necessarily withheld for the present. However, the fragmentary memorials are curious enough to demand their entire transcription, and I shall give them as far as possible in their chronological order, without any further preface. The manuscript is ostensibly written on one side of the paper only, but the reverse pages are covered with notes, additions, and with the memoranda which I am about to reproduce.

## SACRED TO MEMORY <note>original: "MEMORIÆ SACRUM"</note>.

> On the same day my dear wife fell ill—it being a Friday—and at the same time of day, namely in the evening, my gracious God put into my heart the secret of extracting the oil of <term>Halcali</term> <note>original: "Halcali"; likely a variant of "alkali," a key alchemical salt.</note>, which I had once accidentally found at the Pinner of Wakefield <note>A well-known inn or tavern where Vaughan and his wife stayed.</note> in the days of my most dear wife. But it was again taken from me by a most wonderful judgment of God, for I could never remember how I did it, though I made a hundred attempts in vain. And now my glorious God (whose name be praised forever) has brought it again into my mind, and on the same day my dear wife fell ill; and on the Saturday following, which was the day she died on, I extracted it by the former practice. So it was that on the same days, which proved the most sorrowful to me that can ever be, God was pleased to confer upon me the greatest joy I can ever have in this world after her death.
>
> ->The Lord gives, and the Lord takes away: blessed be the<-
> ->name of the Lord. Amen! T. R. V.<-
> <note>The initials T.R.V. stand for Thomas and Rebecca Vaughan, reflecting their partnership in alchemical work.</note>

## NOTE WELL—NOTE WELL—NOTE WELL <note>original: "N. B.—N. B.—N. B."</note>.

When my dear wife and I lived at the Pinner of Wakefield, I remember I melted down equal parts of talc and <term>the Eagle</term> <note>original: "ye Eagle"; an alchemical symbol usually representing sal ammoniac or a volatile substance.</note> with sulfur <note>original: "Brimstone"</note>, repeating the fusion twice. And after that, going to draw Spirit of Salt <note>Hydrochloric acid.</note> with <term>Oil of Glass</term> <note>original: "Oyle of Glass"; likely concentrated sulfuric acid.</note>, I happened (as I think) to mingle some Bay-Salt, or that of <term>Glue of the Sea</term> <note>original: "Colla Maris"; likely a specific marine salt or bitumen.</note>, with the former composition, and I had an oil with which I performed miracles. But attempting to make more of it, I never could achieve it, having forgotten the Com-

<meta>Text ends abruptly at bottom of page with hyphenated word "Com-".</meta>

<summary>This page describes Thomas Vaughan’s personal notebooks, which mix intimate diary entries about his wife Rebecca’s death with alchemical recipes. Vaughan records the "miraculous" rediscovery of a lost alchemical secret (the oil of Halcali) at the exact moment of his wife’s final illness and passing.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Rebecca Vaughan, alchemy, Halcali, Pinner of Wakefield, the Eagle, Spirit of Salt, Oil of Glass, alchemical experiments, mourning</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s personal diary of alchemical experiments and spiritual reflections following the death of his wife, Rebecca. The text transitions from technical alchemical observations to more personal, prophetic dreams.</meta>

<page-num>x</page-num>
<header>BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.</header>

position; but now I am confident the <term>Eagle</term> <note>In alchemy, the "Eagle" often refers to a volatile substance like sal ammoniac or a specific purified mercury used as a solvent.</note> was in it. I clearly remember the appearance of the first vapor that came out, and I could never see anything like it again except when I worked on the Eagle—though I never afterwards worked on her prepared in the same way as I did then. I also know from experience that <term>talc</term> and <term>bay salt</term> <note>Sea salt gathered from salt marshes.</note> together will yield six times more spirit than either will yield on its own. That passage from <term>Rhazes</term> <note>original: "Rhasis"; Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (c. 865–925), a famous Persian physician and alchemist.</note> confirms my finding where he mentions the "Water of salt of three kinds" <note>original: "Aqua Salis trium generum"</note>. But above all, I am convinced by that one phrase from <term>Lully</term> <note>original: "Lullie"; Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and writer on alchemy.</note>, namely "Salt of the Stone" <note>original: "Petra Salis"</note>, and especially that list of materials he makes in his book *The Intellective Art* <note>original: "Ars Intellectiva"</note>: "Niter, Salt, Sulfur, vapor." Nothing could have been said more clearly. And yet, I suspect I shall be much troubled before I find what I have lost; there is so little difference between forgetfulness and ignorance. 
T. R. V. <note>Thomas and Rebecca Vaughan. Thomas frequently combined his initials with his late wife's to symbolize their spiritual union.</note> 1658.

->Whom God has joined, who shall separate? <note>original: "Quos Deus conjunxit quis separabit?"</note><-

### 1658.

[This happened on a Sunday night toward daybreak, and indeed I think it was morning light.]

On the 13th of June, I dreamed that someone appeared to me and cleared herself of the scandalous contents of certain letters that had been put into my hands by a false friend. Then she told me that her father had informed her that she should die again about a quarter of a year from the time she appeared to me. This would be exactly the 14th of September next; we were married on the 28th of that same month. It may be that my merciful God has given me this notice of the time of my own death <note>original: "dissolution"</note> through one who is so dear to me. Her person representing my own signified my death, not hers, for she can die no more. Great is the love and goodness of my God, and I shall be most happy in this interpretation if I may meet her again so soon and begin the heavenly and eternal life with her in the very same month in which we began our earthly life. I beseech my good God to grant us this for the sake of his dear Son and our Savior, Jesus Christ. Amen!

[Written on the 14th of June, the day after I dreamed it. 1658.]

### N.B.—N.B.—N.B. 1658.

On Friday the 18th of July, I myself fell ill at Wapping <note>A district in London along the north bank of the Thames.</note>, and that night I dreamed I was pursued by a <term>stone horse</term> <note>An uncastrated male horse or stallion.</note>, just as my dear wife dreamed before she fell ill. I was grievously troubled all night with a feeling of suffocation at the heart, which continued all through the next day.

<summary>Thomas Vaughan records alchemical observations regarding "the Eagle" and cites historical authorities like Rhazes and Lully, followed by a prophetic dream about his own death involving the spirit of his late wife.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Alchemy, The Eagle, Rhazes, Ramon Llull, Dream prophecy, Rebecca Vaughan, Wapping, Stone horse</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s personal diary. The opening sentence completes a thought from the previous page regarding a physical or spiritual affliction following the death of his wife, Rebecca.</meta>

<page-num>xi</page-num>
<header>BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.</header>

...most violently, and it still remains, though with some slight relief. On the following Saturday, July 17th, because of some secret spiritual instinct, I could no longer stay at Wapping but came that very night to Sir John Underhill’s. On the Sunday following that night, I learned that Mr. Highgate was dead, just as my heart had told me in Wapping a few days before. The will of my God be done: Amen and Amen!

That night I arrived at Sir John’s, I dreamed I had lent 20 pounds to my cousin J. Wakebross, and that his mother had stolen the money, and I was likely to lose it. But my cousin advised me to claim that I had received it, and he would secure it for me. I pray to God that my dear wife’s belongings do not go astray! <note>original: "miscarrie"</note>

My most dear wife fell ill on Friday evening, April 16th, and died the following Saturday evening, the 17th. She was buried on the 26th of the same month, a Monday afternoon, at Meppershall <note>original: "Mappersall"</note> in Bedfordshire, 1658. We were married in the year 1651 on September 28th, by a minister whose name I have forgotten.

> May God, in His infinite and certain mercies in Christ Jesus, bring us together again in Heaven, where she has gone before me—and with her goes my heart and my faith, never to be broken; and this You know, oh my God! Amen!

->*Left at Mrs. Highgate’s.*<-

1.  One flat trunk belonging to my dear wife, with her maiden name upon it.
2.  Another cabinet trunk <note>A smaller, more ornate chest for valuables.</note> belonging to my dear wife, in which is her small distaff <note>original: "rock"; a tool used for spinning wool or flax.</note> and Bible; her childhood Bible I have with me.
3.  One great wooden box belonging to my dear wife, containing all her best clothing <note>original: "Apparell"</note>; inside that is her great Bible, along with her copy of *The Practice of Piety* <note>A popular 17th-century devotional book by Lewis Bayly.</note> and her other books of devotion.
4.  Another wooden box with pillows in it, and a small decorative basket belonging to my dear wife.
5.  One large trunk belonging to my dear wife, with my name upon it, in which are the silver spoons. In the drawers are two small boxes: one contains a lock of my dear wife’s hair, arranged by her own hands, and another contains several small locks of hair.
6.  One pair of great andirons <note>original: "grate Irons"; metal supports for logs in a fireplace.</note> with brass knobs, and a single pair with brass knobs, a fire shovel, tongs, and bellows; my dear wife’s little chair, a round table, a joined stool <note>A sturdy stool made by a professional joiner using mortise and tenon joints.</note>, and a close stool <note>A portable toilet enclosed in a wooden cabinet or box.</note>, with...

<summary>Thomas Vaughan records the details of his wife Rebecca's death and burial in 1658, followed by a meticulous inventory of her personal effects and household goods left in the care of Mrs. Highgate.</summary>
<keywords>Rebecca Vaughan, Meppershall, Bedfordshire, 1658, mourning, dreams, household inventory, devotional books, andirons, close stool</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s personal diary and inventory of his late wife Rebecca’s belongings, transitioning into his reflections on her spiritual state and his alchemical studies.</meta>

<page-num>xii</page-num>
<header>BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.</header>

3. [continued] ...a large glass bottle full of eye-water <note>A medicinal wash used to treat eye ailments.</note>, made at the Pinner of Wakefield <note>The name of the inn where the Vaughans lived in London.</note> by my dear wife and my sister Vaughan, who are both now with God.

So that we might live well and practice our charity—which neither of us lacked, according to our means—I spent my time during her life studying natural secrets <note>original: "Acquisition of some naturall secrets"; this refers to Vaughan’s work in alchemy and natural philosophy.</note>. I had been inclined toward these from my youth; and everything I now write and know through practice, I achieved during her lifetime—truly not before or after, but during the time we lived together at the Pinner of Wakefield. Although I did not bring them to perfection in those precious days, the gates were opened to me then, and everything I have done since is merely the result of those principles. I did not find them through my own wit or labor, but by God’s blessing and the encouragement I received from a most loving, obedient wife, whom I pray God rewards in Heaven for all the happiness and contentment she gave me. I shall set them down here in order, declaring earnestly and with a clear conscience that they are the absolute truth; and I leave them for the use and benefit of whomever God, in His providence, shall guide them to.

On Saturday morning, the 28th of August, after daybreak, it pleased Almighty God to reveal to me in a wonderful way the most blessed state of my dear wife. This was partly through her herself and partly through His own Holy Spirit in a clear revelation, which opened up the meaning of those mysterious words of St. Paul: “For we know that if our earthly house, this tabernacle, were dissolved...” <note>2 Corinthians 5:1. Paul compares the human body to a "tabernacle" or tent that is replaced by an eternal body in heaven.</note>

Bless the Lord, O my soul! And all that is within me, bless His holy name!
T. R. V. <note>Thomas and Rebecca Vaughan. Thomas often combined their initials as a sign of their spiritual union.</note>

->Whom God has joined, who shall separate? <note>original: "Quos Deus conjunxit, quis separabit?"</note><-

->1658.<-

The dream I wrote on the previous page should not be ignored, for a few nights earlier my dear wife appeared to me in my sleep and foretold the death of my dear father. Since then, it has actually come to pass; he is dead and gone to my merciful God, as I have been informed by letters arriving from the country. It is necessary, therefore, for me to prepare myself and make proper use of this warning received from my merciful and most loving God. He does not usually grant such mercies to all men, yet He was pleased to share it with me through my dear wife—to assure me she was a saint in His holy Heavens, being employed as an angel and a messenger of the God of my salvation. To Him be all praise and glory attributed in Jesus Christ forever! Amen!
T. R. V.

## 1658.

The month and the day I have forgotten, but having prayed earnestly for the remission of sins <note>original: "Remission of sinns"; the theological concept of forgiveness and the removal of the guilt of sin.</note>, I went to bed. I dreamed that I lay with sores covering my feet, clothed in certain rags, under the shelter of the great oak that grows before the courtyard of my father's house, and it rained all around me. My feet, which were sore with boils and corrupt matter, troubled me extremely, so that being unable to stand up, I was laid out flat. I dreamed that my father and my brother William <note>original: "W."; likely Thomas’s brother William Vaughan.</note>, who were both dead, came to me, and my father sucked the corruption out of my feet, so that I was immediately well and stood up with great joy; and looking at my feet, they appeared very white and clean, and the sores were completely gone!

Blessed be my good God! Amen!

## 1659. April 8th, Friday <note>original: "Die ♀"; the astronomical symbol for Venus, representing Friday in the Latin and astrological calendars of the period.</note>

In the evening I was overcome by a sudden heaviness of spirit, but without any obvious cause whatsoever. But, I thank God, a great tenderness of heart came along with it, so that I prayed most earnestly with many tears and sorrow for sin. I fervently entreated my gracious God for pardon for myself and my most dear wife, and besought Him to bring us together again in His Heavenly Kingdom, and that He would show me His mercy and answer my prayers by such means and in such a way as might quicken <note>To revive, energize, or bring to life.</note> my spirit, so that I might serve Him cheerfully and praise His name with joy.

I went to bed that night after earnest prayers and tears, and toward daybreak, or just at that time, I had the following dream. I thought that I was again newly married to my dear wife and brought her along with me to show her to some of my friends, which I did in these words: "Here is a wife whom I have not chosen for myself, but *my father chose her for me*," and he asked me if I would not marry her, for she was a beautiful wife. No sooner had he shown her to me than I was extremely in love with her, and I married her immediately. When I had said this, I thought we were both left alone, and calling her to me, I took her into my arms, and she immediately embraced me and kissed me; nor had I...

\* This was not true of our earthly <note>original: "temporall"; referring to life in the physical world as opposed to the eternal or spiritual.</note> marriage, nor of our natural parents, and therefore it signifies some greater mercy.

...in all this vision any sinful desire, but such a love for her as I had for her very soul in my prayers, to which this dream was an answer. At this point I awoke immediately, with exceeding great inward joy. Blessed be my God! Amen!

### April 9, 1659. Saturday <note>original: "Die ♄"; the astronomical symbol for Saturn, representing Saturday.</note>

I went to bed after prayers and hearty tears, and had this dream toward daybreak. I dreamed I was in some obscure, large house where there was a tumultuous, raging crowd of people, among whom I knew no one but my brother H. <note>original: "H."; almost certainly Thomas's twin brother, the poet Henry Vaughan.</note> My dear wife was there with me, but having become uncomfortable at their disorder, I quitted the place and went out, leaving my dear wife behind me. 

As I went out, I reflected and called to mind some small—or at least seemingly small—unkindnesses I had shown toward my dear wife during her lifetime. The remembrance of them being hateful to me, I wondered why I should leave her behind and neglect her company, especially since I now had the opportunity to converse with her after death. These were my thoughts, whereupon I turned back inside to take her along with me. We were followed by a certain person with whom I had, in former times, wasted many years in drunken revelry. 

I had a very long cane in my hand, and at last we came to a churchyard. It was the brightest daylight I ever beheld. When we were about the middle of the churchyard, I struck the ground with the full length of my cane, and it produced a most shrill, reverberating echo. I turned back to look at my wife, and she appeared to me in green silk that reached down to the ground. She was much taller and slenderer than she had been in her lifetime, but in her face there was so much glory and beauty that no angel in Heaven can have more. 

She told me the noise of the cane had frightened her a little, but as she said so, she smiled at me and looked most divine. At this, I looked up toward Heaven and—having quite forgotten my first realization (which was true) that she appeared to me after her death—I was much troubled in mind lest I should die before her. I feared this for spiritual reasons <note>original: "spirituall Accompt"; meaning he was concerned for her spiritual welfare and salvation if he were not there to guide or support her.</note>, lest after my death she might be tempted to do wrong and to live otherwise than she did at present. 

While I was so troubled, the cane that was in my hand suddenly broke. Once it was broken, it no longer looked like a cane, but was a brittle, weak reed. This reminded me of her death again, which ended my fear and the doubts I had felt about dying before her. When the reed was broken,

<summary>Thomas Vaughan recounts a vivid dream from April 1659 in which he reunites with his deceased wife in a churchyard; the dream explores his lingering guilt over past unkindnesses and his anxieties about her spiritual state.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Rebecca Vaughan, Henry Vaughan, 1659, dream, vision, grief, churchyard, symbolism, reed, spiritual anxiety</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s spiritual journal entries from 1659, concluding a dream about his wife and recording a vision of his own impending illness and death.</meta>

<page-num>XV</page-num>
<header>BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.</header>

She came close to me, and I gave her the longer half of the reed, and the furthest end, and the shortest I kept for myself: but looking on the broken end of it, and finding it ragged and somewhat rough, she gave me a knife to polish it, which I did. Then we both passed out of the churchyard, and turning to the gentleman that followed me, I asked him if he would go along with us, but he utterly refused; and the truth is, he still follows the world too much. Then I turned to my dear wife, to go along with her, and having done so, I awoke.

By this dream, and the shortest part of the reed left in my hand, I guess I shall not live as long after her as I have lived with her. Praised be my God! Amen!

->*April the 16th, at night. 1659.*<-

I dreamed that a flame of a whitish color would break out at the toes of my left foot, and this was told me in my dream by a strange person of a dark countenance. It is to be noted that this was the very night on which my dear wife died in 1658: it being a Saturday night, and but one short of the number, or true account <note>original: "Accompt"; a 17th-century spelling of 'account,' likely referring here to his calculation of the exact date or the anniversary of her death.</note>. It may be that the disease which shall cause my death was shown to me on the night she died, for it is true that in my left foot there has now fallen down a dangerous humor <term>humor</term> <note>In early medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids thought to control health. A "dangerous humor" refers to a harmful accumulation of fluid or a morbid condition.</note>, and it lodges under my very heel; upon the lifting of my leg upward, it pains me strangely. It fell first into my knee, and what it may come to I know not, unless it will end in a gout: but it first of all troubled me in the sinews and caused a contraction <note>original: "contracture"</note> of them, and then I had a dull pain, and still have, in the uppermost joint of the thigh. T. R. V. <note>The initials T. R. V. represent Thomas and Rebecca Vaughan, signifying their spiritual union.</note>

Many years ago, at Paddington, before my liver ailment <note>original: "distemper in the Liver"</note> seized me, there appeared to me twice in the same night, in two separate dreams, a young, strange person, not unlike to him who appeared in a strange manner to me at St. Edmund Hall in Oxford. His countenance was dark, and I believe it is the evil genius <term>evil genius</term> <note>In this context, a "genius" refers to a guiding spirit or supernatural entity; here, a malevolent or warning one.</note>. But in this last dream, I saw him not so clearly, my life—I bless God for it—being much improved <note>original: "amended"</note>. The evil he so gladly signifies to me does not frighten me, for I am ready for death, and with all my heart shall I welcome it; for I desire to be dissolved <note>original: "dissolved"; a biblical reference (Philippians 1:23) meaning to die and pass from the physical body into the spiritual realm.</note> and to be with Christ, which is far better for me than to live and sin in this sinful body. T. R. V. 1659.
God is. T. R. V. Amen and Amen!

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.</header>

These private records <note>original: "memoranda"</note> serve as a touching testimony to the tenderness and humanity of their writer, and, as a mystic, they provide conclusive proof of his sincerity. They confirm the fact of his marriage to a lady whose family history is unknown; they offer evidence of his profound religious convictions and the intensity of his devotional fervor. Moreover, they enable us to reconcile the way he died, as reported by Ashmole and Wood <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692) and Anthony à Wood (1632–1695) were famous 17th-century antiquarians and biographers who recorded the lives of English scholars and alchemists.</note>, with the statements in his books against the purely physical side of alchemy. We no longer have to wonder how an expert in the spiritual side of the *Great Work* <note>original: *Magnum Opus*; the alchemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone, which Vaughan viewed as a spiritual rather than just a physical pursuit.</note> came to die during an experiment in ordinary chemistry. He admits in these records that he turned to the lesser science with the hope of increasing his wealth so he could be more charitable, and he claims a success that his motives seem to have deserved, at least from the perspective of poetic justice. It is unlikely that future research will uncover more facts about the life of this interesting mystic, but I believe I am right in saying that the fragments I have luckily found bring us closer to the man himself than any of his books on magic, and that our respect for his character must grow the closer we look at him.

I should only add that in this edition of some of his most famous works, several long Latin passages have been translated into English. In doing so, I have followed his own method from when he provided English versions of his obscure <term>recondite</term> <note>original: "recondite"; meaning obscure, little known, or difficult to understand.</note> citations—that is to say, I have attempted, where necessary, an illustrative and interpretive translation to clarify as much as possible the unclear meanings used by highly mystical writers.

The editor also describes their approach to translating the difficult Latin and mystical passages in this collection.</summary>

# INTRODUCTORY ESSAY

### ON THE ESOTERIC LITERATURE OF THE MIDDLE AGES, AND ON THE UNDERLYING PRINCIPLES OF THEURGIC ART AND PRACTICE IN WESTERN CHRISTENDOM.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'T' decorated with elaborate foliate scrollwork and botanical motifs.</image-desc>
THE magical writings of Thomas Vaughan represent an introductory explanation <note>original: "prolegomena"; a set of preliminary remarks or an introduction to a subject.</note> not only for the general history of practical transcendentalism <note>A philosophy or spiritual practice that seeks to understand or reach a reality beyond the physical world.</note> and the philosophy of transcendental art—written from the perspective of a Christian initiate—but they are specifically focused on interpreting alchemical symbolism. They claim to provide the intelligent reader with a substantially new revelation of that mysterious *First Matter* of the *Great Work* <note>original: *Magnum Opus*; the ultimate goal of alchemy, traditionally the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. This substance grants those who know it and can use its many powers a full and perfect ability to successfully perform all types of theurgic <term>theurgic</term> <note>Theurgy refers to "god-working," or rituals intended to invoke the presence of or unite with divine beings to achieve spiritual transformation.</note> experiments. 

Adopting the terminology of Hermetic experts <note>original: "adepts"; masters of the occult arts associated with the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, Thomas Vaughan expands the theoretical range of alchemical processes. He outlines the spiritual evolution of humanity, completely and scornfully rejecting work that only deals with physical minerals. Instead, he claims that the true high priests <note>original: "hierophants"</note> of mystic science have a personal interest and role in the entire creative work <note>original: *opus*</note>. 

In offering a reprint of these curious and truly important treatises to the modern student of ancient mysteries for the first time, it seems necessary to clearly state the reasons for their reproduction and the exact nature of the interest they now hold. Until recently, the vast literature of ancient occultism held little more than archaeological interest—as the remains of once-respected high thinking from past ages—and a certain value for book collectors due to the extreme rarity of all esoteric works. This interest and value would be an insufficient reason for their revival in an age of modern, empirical science <note>original: "positive science"; a reference to Positivism, a 19th-century philosophy that recognizes only that which can be scientifically verified.</note>

<summary>This page introduces an essay on Thomas Vaughan's magical writings, explaining that he viewed alchemy as a spiritual process rather than a purely physical or "mineral" pursuit. The editor notes that while these texts were once seen only as archaeological curiosities, they now hold renewed value for students of the "Great Work."</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, introductory essay, transcendentalism, alchemy, First Matter, Magnum Opus, theurgy, Hermeticism, esoteric literature, occultism</keywords>

->INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

or, in the absence of any message of vital importance to this truth-seeking <note>original: "veridic"</note> age, they would have remained merely archaeological monuments and treasures for book-lovers alone, on philosophical, practical, and even commercial grounds. However, the sudden revival of psychic research among us, and certain discoveries made in the psychic world—many of which are now well known, while others remain hidden <note>original: *in abscondito*</note>—have placed the position of the ancient mystics in an entirely different light. They have created a strong likelihood that earlier seekers also have a right to be heard. It suggests that the practitioners of early psychic experiments in ages of singular purpose may have progressed beyond ourselves and may be qualified to be our teachers and guides. These are the broad theoretical <note>original: *à priori*</note> grounds for the revived interest in the entire field of esoteric <term>esoteric</term> <note>Intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge.</note> literature. 

But when the full power of an impartial and sympathetic mind, already aware of the established facts I have just mentioned, is applied to the proper study of "the philosophers" (as they were collectively called), this general interest is quickly swallowed up by a far more absorbing feeling. We find ourselves in the presence of a massive <note>original: "titanic"</note> claim, put forward in many cases by intellectual giants. This claim is joined to a height of aspiration, a wealth of spiritual insight, a cosmic breadth of view, and a degree of apparent personal holiness that are sufficient to deeply impress even the most unemotional minds. Meanwhile, vistas of vast possibilities unveil to the prepared imagination at the magic word of the high priests <note>original: "hierophants"; interpreters of sacred mysteries or esoteric principles</note>. 

These are the more specific reasons for the new interest in ancient esoteric literature. Here, once more, the accepted facts of modern psychology serve as evidence for the truth of these claims. In my opinion, this evidence is strong enough not only to justify a thorough and practical investigation following the methods of the mystics, but to make such investigation essential for those who, like myself, are unconditionally devoted to the progress of psychic science. The reason for this reprint <note>original: *raison d’être*</note> is to place within the reach of like-minded students the works of an acknowledged master <term>adept</term> <note>A person who is skilled or proficient in a particular art or science, especially one of the occult sciences.</note>—which are otherwise almost impossible to find <note>original: *introuvable*</note>—and thereby to set new, sympathetic investigators on the path of the grand mysteries. 

Out of respect for the work of modern theosophists <note>Theosophists are members of a movement, particularly the Theosophical Society founded in 1875, that seeks to understand the nature of divinity and the universe through mystical insight.</note>, it must be stated that they were essentially the first to draw attention to the connecting link between modern psychic phenomena and ancient wonder-working <note>original: "thaumaturgy"</note>. The current revival of magic and Magian thought <note>Referring to the wisdom of the Magi or ancient Persian astrologer-priests.</note> in England is directly due to their influence.

Having paid this deserved tribute, it becomes necessary to explain the nature of the psychic facts to which I have several times alluded, as

<summary>The author explains that the revival of psychic research and theosophy has made ancient mystical texts relevant again, suggesting that early "adepts" may have possessed spiritual knowledge superior to modern science.</summary>

->INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

much to prevent misconception as for the use of inquirers at the early stages of their progress.

The proven facts which I consider—along with many other qualified people—to be probable evidence for the truth of the magical claim, when viewed broadly in its entirety, are quite independent of any mystical theory. They are not restricted to any special circle of secret <term>esoteric</term> <note>Intended for or understood by only a small group of people with specialized knowledge.</note> students and are completely unrelated to the results that may or may not have been achieved by those colleges of Eastern masters <note>original: "adepts"</note> that Theosophists <note>Followers of a religious and philosophical system, popularized in the late 19th century, which seeks to understand the nature of divinity and the universe through mystical insight.</note> claim live in the politically unreachable strongholds <note>original: "fastnesses"</note> of the Tibetan highlands. I speak with all respect for an opinion held by several intellectual people, and with proper appreciation for what evidence exists on the subject; personally, I am not committed to any specific opinion on the matter, except on the theoretical <note>original: *à priori*</note> ground of magical possibility. These psychic facts can be verified by any intelligent person who possesses enough persistence and is capable of appreciating the issues at stake in a way that allows them to conduct their research with those issues in mind. They involve the higher classes of hypnotic phenomena, ecstatic and trance-state clairvoyance, thought-transference, the action of the mind upon matter from a distance <note>original: "transcorporeal action of mind upon matter"</note>, and those phenomena of modern spiritualism that unbiased researchers agree are well-supported. These facts offer a rational basis for the belief in a form of existence other than human physical life on earth. They naturally end the age of spiritual faith and begin the first dawn <note>original: "auroral light"</note> of an age of spiritual knowledge. This reveals for the first time, openly to all humanity, that it is possible—even in earthly life—to enter into another form of perception and to establish communication with levels of intelligent existence that are normally beyond our range.

This communication is currently very restricted, and progress, which has been slow at best, seems at the present moment to be almost completely stopped. This is partly due to the insincerity of experiments, which are attempted on the lowest levels of physical existence with coarse, degraded, and sometimes unhealthy <note>original: "diseased"</note> instruments <note>In this context, "instruments" refers to spiritualist mediums or the human subjects of psychic experiments.</note>, yet are concerned with spiritual heights. Meanwhile, the ignorance of the proper method of procedure—the "true process" of alchemical allegory—creates another seemingly impossible barrier. In this difficulty, the earnest student who turns for enlightenment to the sanctuaries of ancient mystic wisdom and for counsel to its grand high priests <note>original: "hierophants"</note> finds himself face to face with the departed but still powerful representatives of a priestly <note>original: "sacerdotal"</note> and royal science. This science claims to be exclusively acquainted with the One Way of Rectitude <note>The "One Way of Rectitude" refers to the specific, disciplined path of spiritual and moral development prescribed by ancient mystical traditions.</note>

<summary>The author argues that modern psychic phenomena like clairvoyance and thought-transference provide a rational, verifiable basis for believing in a spiritual existence beyond the physical world. He suggests that while modern experiments are often flawed, ancient mystical traditions hold the "true process" for navigating these higher levels of consciousness.</summary>

->INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

distance, and the Unerring Path of Light. He discovers that the wonders of the ancient world are essentially the same as those modern phenomena with which he is already familiar—events he is convinced are real and which have inspired him to continue his search. The hypnotic trance, as we know it today, is clearly and frequently mentioned in ancient writings. Modern clairvoyance is matched by the magical "vision at a distance." In place of thought-transference, the ancients had mystical methods of communicating with people no matter how far away, even without any physical means. Furthermore, a direct fundamental connection can easily be drawn between many wonders of American Spiritualism and the ghostly mysteries of <term>necromantic</term> <note>The practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead.</note> and magical summoning. 

However, while our modern phenomena seem like only the beginning of an experience, the miraculous <note>original: "thaumaturgic"</note> results achieved by the initiates of old are of a higher and more noble level; they are the fruit of refined methods and a long series of experimental research. There is no doubt or hesitation in the teachings or claims of the <term>hierophants</term> <note>High priests or official interpreters of sacred mysteries.</note>; they are always certain, unwavering, and practically in agreement. They write with the weight of a vast and limitless subject behind them—one that explores both the depths and the heights of existence—and they are strengthened by a majestic awareness of eternity.

As they are currently conducted, our modern experiments lack practical results. Our lines of investigation reach a certain point and then stop, but the ancient pioneers of mysticism seem to have discovered some previously mysterious way to cross the barriers that block our path. In doing so, they tell us they gained possession of a massive secret, which they claim is divine in nature. They dared not reveal it publicly, for immense penalties were associated with dishonoring the Grand Mysteries. In their books, they protected their knowledge from the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> by using allegorical language and symbols, leaving their true meaning to be discovered by the sincere student through insight granted by the spiritual world. They also preserved their secrets by initiating tested disciples of proven discretion, to whom they seem to have freely shared the precious treasures of their knowledge. In this way, some of the <term>secret colleges of magic</term> <note>Private organizations or esoteric schools where occult knowledge was preserved and taught to initiates.</note>—which were apparently once numerous—were established in the West.

Therefore, studying the mystics presents obvious difficulties that seem impossible to overcome at first. However, speaking from personal experience, I do not hesitate to say that a pure, patient, and active mind has no reason to be discouraged. The basic phenomena are identical, and thus the modern...

He encourages students that these ancient mysteries can still be understood because the underlying spiritual phenomena remain the same.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of ancient mystical traditions, moving from a comparison of modern and ancient phenomena to a warning about how to interpret historical alchemical and Hermetic texts.</meta>

->INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

The psychologist has already reached the outer doors of the sanctuary; but he must carefully bear in mind that a large proportion of <term>Hermetic literature</term> <note>A body of philosophical and religious writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, blending Egyptian and Greek wisdom.</note> is concerned with a physical process for converting "base metals" <note>Common metals like lead or iron, which alchemists sought to transform into "noble" gold.</note> into gold. It is just as misguided and unproductive to force a psychological meaning onto purely alchemical writings as it is to ignore their historical context. Such meanings are often completely at odds with the lives and clear goals of the authors. The historical literature of inner psychology is already vast enough without the reckless, though perhaps understandable, attempts by several critics to claim every ancient text for their field.

The literature concerning <term>theurgy</term> <note>From the Greek 'theourgia,' meaning 'god-working'—a type of magic intended to invoke the presence of deities or spirits to achieve a state of union with the divine.</note> and mysticism in Western Christianity, which is my sole focus in this essay, is only one branch of universal occultism. However, it can be divided into several distinct categories, all of which require consideration. This study will be much easier if we first briefly look at the history of Christian magic—or rather, magic as it was practiced in the Christian countries of Europe.

The philosophical principles behind the theory and practice of the magical arts are mainly derived from the Platonic School of Alexandria—the school of Ammonius Saccas, Proclus, and Hypatia; the school of Synesius, who interpreted theosophical dreams; and the angelic mysticism of the <term>pseudo-Dionysius</term> <note>A mysterious Christian theologian and philosopher of the late 5th to early 6th century whose writings on the "celestial hierarchy" of angels were immensely influential.</note>. The Neoplatonists were essentially the heirs of the ancient wisdom of the <term>Magi</term> <note>original: "Magian wisdom"; referring to the priestly caste of ancient Persia known for their knowledge of astronomy and magic.</note> from Egypt, Greece, and Rome. The mystical works of Hermes Trismegistus, produced during this period of intellectual light in Alexandria, were not simply the inventions of a semi-Christian sage. Instead, they likely contained traditional secrets and theories about the universe from a very ancient time. 

The core belief of this high magical faith, practiced by the great masters of Alexandrian philosophy, was that through specific prayers and rituals—performed solemnly by men who were pure, sober, self-controlled, and mentally enlightened—it was possible to communicate directly with those invisible powers that inhabit the immense gap between humanity and God. A divine state of joy followed this communication with the universe's higher intelligences. In this state, man briefly shared in god-like qualities, and the power and wisdom he gained gave him authority over many levels of spiritual beings.

The outlawing <note>original: "proscription"</note> of the old pagan religion <note>original: "cultus"</note> and the bitter, constant persecution of all who practiced secret and magical arts began during the reign of the notorious Emperor Constantine. This was continued by Valentinian, Theodosius, and other prominent figures...

<summary>This page discusses the origins of Western Christian magic and mysticism, tracing its philosophical roots to the Neoplatonic School of Alexandria and the Hermetic tradition. The author warns against misinterpreting physical alchemy as purely psychological while explaining the core goal of "theurgy": direct communication with divine spirits.</summary>

<keywords>Hermetic literature, alchemy, theurgy, Neoplatonism, Alexandria, Hermes Trismegistus, pseudo-Dionysius, Christian magic, occult history</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory history of Western magic, transitioning from the philosophical origins in Alexandria to how these traditions survived and evolved through the Middle Ages by blending with Jewish, Arabic, and folk influences under the umbrella of Christianity.</meta>

<page-num>xxii</page-num>

->INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

The rise of imperial Christianity did not eliminate polytheism or destroy the <term>adepts</term> <note>Highly skilled practitioners of occult arts who have reached a high level of initiation.</note>. The old religion and the old <term>theurgic art</term> <note>Rituals intended to invoke divine presence or spirits.</note> took refuge in remote places; they were practiced in secret and silence, and in this way, many of those mysterious secret societies were presumably born. These groups carried on the traditions of the <term>Magi</term> <note>Originally Persian priest-sages; here, it refers to masters of ancient wisdom and magic.</note> through the entire period of the Middle Ages, and numerous magical rituals reveal their connection with <term>Neo-Platonism</term> <note>A philosophical system originating in the 3rd century AD that blended Plato's ideas with Eastern mysticism.</note>.

The banning <note>original: "proscription"</note> of magic and paganism was eventually followed by the banning and persecution of the Jews. In the same way, they were forced to practice their religious rites in secret. Their Eastern <note>original: "oriental"</note> resentment was frequently driven to a frenzy by their intolerable sufferings and humiliations. As practitioners <note>original: "professors"</note> of <term>Kabbalistic arts</term> <note>Jewish mystical traditions involving the study of the divine and the hidden meanings of sacred texts.</note>, they were firm believers in the power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of invocations and spoken formulas, and had been dedicated since ancient times to every kind of superstitious practice. They used their mystical methods to harm their enemies, and the "infernal magic" of the Middle Ages—with its mockery of Christian rituals, its black masses, and its unholy prayers—is, at least in part, their creation.

Thus, medieval occultism was essentially made up of many different parts. It borrowed, on one hand, from the rabbinical wisdom of Israel and, on the other, from pagan sources. The Crusades brought it under Arabic influence, which was significantly increased by the spread of <term>alchemical</term> <note>Relating to alchemy, the forerunner of chemistry focused on transforming matter and finding the elixir of life.</note> ideas from the East to the West. Meanwhile, from the remains <note>original: "debris"</note> of every vanished <term>cultus</term> <note>original: Latin for "cult" or "system of worship."</note> that had flourished in ancient times among the <term>Teutonic</term> <note>Germanic peoples.</note> and <term>Celtic</term> <note>Peoples of Western Europe, including the Gauls and Britons.</note> nations, the mythology of nature-spirits, the elfin world, and the strange doctrines concerning <term>elementary intelligences</term> <note>Beings or spirits believed to inhabit the four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note> was built up.

Over this colorful variety of beliefs, the <term>sacerdotal cope</term> <note>A liturgical cloak worn by priests; used here metaphorically to describe the outward appearance of the Christian Church.</note> of Christianity was always spread. This may have been used as a disguise at first, but in most cases, it eventually became—without question—the official religious belief of most European adepts. The writings of <term>esoteric literature</term> <note>Works intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge.</note> are completely in agreement on this point. Whatever secret teachings were part of the traditional "science of the Magi," they did not conflict with the sincere Christian faith of later initiates, or they were adapted to fit with orthodox Christian teaching. If we accept the claims of magic, it is likely that the secret knowledge passed down was more about hidden power than hidden doctrine. This view is entirely consistent with the universal history of magic; in every age and nation, we find the same claims to supernatural <note>original: "preternatural"</note> powers made on behalf of many different religious systems.

<summary>This page describes how ancient magical traditions survived the rise of Christianity by merging with Jewish Kabbalah, Arabic alchemy, and European folklore to form medieval occultism. It argues that while these practices were diverse, they eventually operated under a Christian framework, focusing more on secret powers than conflicting doctrines.</summary>
<keywords>medieval occultism, Neo-Platonism, Kabbalah, alchemy, Magi, nature-spirits, Christian magic, theurgy</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the history of Western magic, shifting from the secret survival of ancient traditions to their integration into the Christian framework of the Middle Ages and the Renaissance.</meta>

<page-num>xxiii</page-num>

->INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

The magical literature of Europe is full of powerful formulas for the summoning and control of all classes of spiritual beings. To a large extent, these are taken directly from the hierarchical worship rituals <note>original: "hierarchic liturgies"</note> of the Christian world. The rituals explain the miraculous powers inherent in the names of Jehovah, Jesus, Mary, and similar sacred names. Additionally, the number mysticism of Pythagoras is interpreted to support the doctrine of the Trinity.

There is, therefore, no reason to assume that Western initiates <note>Individuals who have been admitted into secret or esoteric knowledge.</note> had discovered a "religion behind all religions." They were simply Christian mystics who never dreamed of looking further than Christianity for enlightenment. What they claimed to possess was the key to performing miracles, not a key to universal religious symbolism. Those who practiced <term>goetic magic</term> <note>A form of magic involving the invocation of spirits or demons, often for selfish or dark purposes.</note> and the infamous attendees of the Witches' Sabbath may have denied and rejected Christ, but they did not deny his power. If they served another master, it was in search of an immediate reward and with a full awareness of the spiritual penalties they were risking.

The amount of misunderstanding that exists on this point—even among sympathetic researchers—requires a firm statement to correct the general tendency to project modern ideas onto these texts. Many modern readers try to strip away the positive elements of the authors' faith, but these Christian adepts generally held their beliefs with sincerity, and often in the face of persecution from their fellow believers.

On the surface, the sincere Christian faith of these medieval and later experts seems to contradict the massive claims of <term>magian art</term> <note>The high art or science of the Magi, referring to the mastery of occult wisdom and natural forces.</note> and science. I recognize, along with the general consensus of modern mystical thought, that the Christian system (at least in its outward presentation) does not provide us with a fully intelligible theory of the universe. We might reasonably expect that enlightened people, who supposedly "enjoy clear and open thought within a universal consciousness," would have possessed a more complete theory of how the universe began <note>original: "cosmogonical doctrine"</note>.

As a person who does not take sides in these opinions, and as someone who regrets the extreme intellectual foolishness of rushing toward unstable and useless convictions on the most important problems using half-baked theories, I have no desire to downplay the importance of such difficulties. However, it is just as easy to exaggerate their significance. Our modern discoveries in psychology have not yet helped us reach a definite theory of the universe, and in their current state, it is impossible that they ever will. Even if the ancient mystics developed these psychological insights indefinitely, they may have similarly failed to find total enlightenment; for the power of—

<summary>This page argues that Western magical traditions were deeply rooted in sincere Christian belief rather than a hidden "universal religion." The author notes that even practitioners of dark magic acknowledged the power of Christian names and symbols, though they chose to serve other masters for immediate gain.</summary>

<keywords>Christian mysticism, goetic magic, magian art, sacred names, Pythagoras, cosmology, psychology, medieval magic, religious symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Western esoteric knowledge, transitioning from the Christian context of these practices to the specific disciplines of Natural Magic, Transcendental Magic, and Alchemy, as well as the foundational texts like the Kabbalah and the works of Hermes Trismegistus.</meta>

<page-num>xxiv</page-num>

<header>INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.</header>

Performing wonders within the domain of natural law and elevating one's intuitive faculties to expand the scope of perception within the universe <note>original: "Cosmos"</note> may not necessarily put the observer in a position to make successful philosophical generalizations. On the other hand, if the "Great Secret" claimed by the Magi <note>Wise men or magicians, traditionally referring to the Zoroastrian priests of ancient Persia who were said to possess hidden knowledge.</note> involves a truly universal science, and if it takes the observer outside the laws of nature, he may be carried beyond the reach of theory altogether. The temporary experience of a transcendent and god-like state of being <note>original: "deific form of subsistence"</note> might temporarily remove from the mind all awareness of normal thoughts and typical intellectual limits.

Medieval secret knowledge <note>original: "esoteric knowledge"</note> is divided into three broad categories. The first is described as Natural Magic; the second as Spiritual or Transcendental Magic; and the third, under the broad title of Alchemy, includes a philosophy and a physical practice that are of the highest importance to the modern student. The philosophy of this entire subject is found in two invaluable collections: the works attributed to Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure said to be the author of the Hermetic Corpus, a series of sacred texts that form the basis of Hermeticism.</note> and the Jewish Kabbalah <note>An ancient Jewish mystical tradition providing an interpretation of the Bible and the universe.</note>. For all practical purposes, this is contained in Baron de Rosenroth’s *The Kabbalah Unveiled* <note>original: "Kabbala Denudata"</note>, a portion of which has recently been translated into English. Explanations of these philosophical textbooks are numerous, and they vary considerably in quality. Much interesting and important material can be found in Cornelius Agrippa’s *Three Books of Occult Philosophy*, although this author—who was so highly praised by Thomas Vaughan <note>A 17th-century Welsh philosopher and alchemist.</note>—is not ranked among the highest level of masters <term>adepts</term>.^1 Both the Hermetic and Kabbalistic writings focus largely on the mystical history of creation. They view the evolution of humanity as strictly parallel to this creation through the magical "doctrine of correspondence" <note>The principle that things in the spiritual world have a direct relationship with things in the physical world; often summarized by the phrase "as above, so below."</note>. Consequently, a secret significance is assigned to those parts of the texts that deal with the development of the physical universe out of the chaotic clash of elemental forces.

In addition to this, the Kabbalistic books deal extensively with <term>pneumatology</term> <note>The study or theory of spiritual beings, souls, or the breath of life.</note>, the hierarchy and classification of spirits, the cyclical journey of the soul, its nature, origin, and destiny, and the divine progress of—

---

1 "Cornelius Agrippa, who was a seeker all his life and who found neither knowledge nor peace, belongs to a different category. His books are full of learning and boldness; his personal character was eccentric and independent, which earned him the reputation of a terrible sorcerer and led to his persecution by priests and princes. He later wrote a work condemning the very studies that had brought him no happiness, and he died in loneliness and misery." — Eliphas Lévi, *The History of Magic* <note>original: "Histoire de la Magie"</note>, pp. 346, 347.

<summary>This page outlines the three main branches of medieval esoteric knowledge—Natural Magic, Transcendental Magic, and Alchemy—and identifies the works of Hermes Trismegistus and the Kabbalah as their primary philosophical foundations.</summary>

->### INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

the Divine Intellectual Essence from star to star and from sun to sun through the endless chain of existence, and the highest problems of transcendental psychology. Their philosophical interest in our current stage of public <term>exoteric</term> <note>original: "exoteric"; intended for or likely to be understood by the general public</note> spiritual investigation is hardly lessened by the uncertainty of their origin, or the occasionally fraudulent way specific books have been released to the world. This is because they undoubtedly represent an ancient tradition and are entirely consistent with the solemn grandeur of Jewish genius.

A significant branch of the <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>original: "Kabbalah"; a tradition of Jewish mysticism</note> is dedicated to practical magic. This can be described as both the source and the summary of all existing rituals since the days of the *Enchiridion* <note>original: "Enchyridion"; likely referring to the *Enchiridion of Pope Leo III*, a famous book of magical prayers</note>, including those of the *Black Art* <note>original: "Black Art"; sorcery or dark magic</note>, which are simply hellish distortions of normal and lawful magic.

The nature, methods, and results of *Natural Magic* have been described in various ways by its practitioners. Its scope is often expanded until it includes much of the spiritual or transcendental branch—for example, predicting future events that are beyond the reach of normal probability. Such predictions can only be made by the ecstatic shifting of the intellectual faculties into another state of being. Properly defined, it is the revealing of the secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> of physical nature through art. In simpler and more specific terms, it is the production of seemingly miracle-working <term>thaumaturgic</term> <note>original: "thaumaturgic"; from the Greek "thauma" (wonder) and "ergon" (work), meaning the performance of miracles or wonders</note> effects by using physical laws that are not widely known; therefore, it has no connection to psychology. 

Today, experimental chemistry produces countless phenomena that appear clearly miraculous to the uninformed mind. *"We call that most secret and hidden department of physical science, which develops the mystical properties of natural substances, Natural Magic,"* says Robert Fludd <note>Robert Fludd (1574–1637) was a prominent English physician and mystical philosopher</note> in his "Brief Defense for the Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross." He identifies the three Magi—who were led by the Star in the East to the cradle of the Great Christian Initiator—and the legendary King Solomon as being among the most famous masters <note>original: "adepts"</note> of this basic branch of secret <note>original: "esoteric"</note> wisdom. This field reaches its peak in the heavenly science of astrology, which is the calculation of future events based on the traditional and observed facts of how stars influence human life. It is impressive because of its antiquity and important because of the respect it has earned from great minds of the past. However, neither astrology nor any other form of *Natural Magic* is useful to the student of the psyche.

Spiritual or Transcendental Magic includes several distinct subdivisions of secret art and science. Considered in its—

<meta>This page continues the introductory essay's exploration of Spiritual Magic, transitioning from the definition of Natural Magic to the specific disciplines and psychic processes required for transcendental communication and the "great work."</meta>

->### INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

In its totality, it is the synthesis of those methods and processes by which the ancient mystics claimed to have developed their psychic powers <note>original: "potencies"</note> so as to establish communication with forms of intelligent, finite beings <note>original: "subsistence"</note> that exist beyond the physical horizon and are therefore normally invisible. Their goal was to form a connection with the underlying principles of nature and thus develop the possibilities that are hidden like seeds in the heart of all material substances. By doing so, they aimed to perform actions on the physical plane that are beyond the scope of common physical science. Lastly, as the crowning aim and **great work** <note>original: *magnum opus*</note> of experimental mysticism, they sought to enable the "highest aspect of humanity" to hold "immediate intercourse with the highest aspect of the universe."

The wonders of Spiritual Magic are said to be accomplished by means of a certain lifestyle and a specific sequence of ceremonies. While these are all of symbolic significance, the highest experts <note>original: "adepts"</note> unanimously consider them to be devoid of inherent power. They are simply adopted to direct and develop the psychic faculties of will and imagination, which are the primary agents in every magical process. Éliphas Lévi <note>a 19th-century French occultist and author</note> recommends that the seeker in the **entrance hall** <note>original: *pronaos*; the porch or vestibule of an ancient temple</note> of the Spiritual Temple should "rise daily at the same hour, and at an early hour; bathe summer and winter before daybreak in spring water; never wear soiled clothes, washing them himself if necessary; and exercise himself through voluntary self-denial <note>original: "privations"</note> so that he may be better able to bear involuntary ones; finally, to silence all desires except that of achieving the **great work** <note>original: *magnum opus*</note>." 

But this is simply the preliminary discipline. The preparation of the mystic "sulfur of the wise" is of another and higher kind. The student of Thomas Vaughan <note>a 17th-century Welsh philosopher and alchemist</note> will find it described in various parts of his writings, and especially in *The Secret Magical Soul* <note>original: *Anima Magica Abscondita*</note>. It is a process of psychic chemistry of a threefold <note>original: "triadic"</note> and absolutely supernatural character. Indeed, the attacks of modern mystics against the use of the term "supernatural" are founded on a fundamental misunderstanding of occultism and are due to the influence of materialistic philosophy. It is a doctrine of magical science that there is an inherent imperfection in Nature, and that there is an absolute perfection which transcends Nature; now, the testimony of the visible universe and the unceasing aspiration of humanity’s higher consciousness are in harmony with this doctrine.

The threefold process of which I have spoken is the transformation <note>original: "transmutation"</note> of the physical body by the soul within it, the elevation and transfiguration of the soul by the overshadowing spirit, and the illumination and divinization <note>original: "deification"</note> of the spirit by contact with the Universal Consciousness. This process accomplishes that rebirth <note>original: "regeneration"</note> of the...

<summary>This page defines Spiritual Magic as a method for developing psychic powers to communicate with invisible entities and achieve the "great work." It describes the necessary mental and physical disciplines, citing figures like Éliphas Lévi and Thomas Vaughan, and outlines a "triadic" process of spiritual transformation.</summary>

->### INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

The whole person is the true subject of transcendental philosophy and the only safe basis for practical magic. All operations attempted by the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> and the uninitiated—in other words, by spiritually unrefined <note>original: "unregenerate"</note> persons—are either dangerous, unsuccessful, or, as in the case of Black Magic, of a dark and hateful nature.

Contemplation and quietism <note>A system of religious mysticism that emphasizes passive contemplation and the suppression of self-will to achieve union with the Divine.</note> are the keys to this mysterious process, which seems to have reached its highest point among Eastern nations. It is described by Roger Bacon <note>A 13th-century English philosopher and friar who was an early advocate of the scientific method and was often associated with alchemy.</note> as the modification of the body by alchemy, which presents much of Hermetic allegory <note>Symbolic stories and metaphors related to the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus and the alchemical tradition.</note> in a new and more understandable light.

When this modification, or New Birth <note>A spiritual transformation or regeneration of the soul.</note>, has been accomplished, the Magus <note>A practitioner of magic or a wise person initiated into secret knowledge.</note> is placed in communication with the creative forces of the universe. The pathways of spiritual perception—which are narrow, difficult, and full of barriers to the psychologist of today—are thrown wide open for unlimited exploration. Such, at least, is the claim of the magical textbooks. The initiated epopt <note>original: "epopt"; an initiate into religious mysteries, from the Greek word for "eyewitness."</note> may then proceed to the invocation of celestial beings <note>original: "intelligences"</note>, the souls of the great departed, and to the assertion of intellectual control over the hierarchies of elemental beings <note>Spirits associated with the four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note>. The depths and heights of his own immortal nature are also revealed to him, and from the peaks of his spiritual life, he may soar into ecstatic yet conscious communion with God Himself. On the physical plane, he may perform many wonders by the adaptation of natural laws, which seem to the uninitiated observer to defy all law. He may fill inert substances with the power <note>original: "potency"</note> of his individual will—which is the philosophical principle of talismanic magic <note>Magic involving objects, or talismans, believed to possess supernatural powers.</note>. He can search all hearts and read all destinies, perceive events happening at a great distance, and can impart a portion of his own powers to suitable subjects, inducing trances, clairvoyance, prophetic foresight, and so on.

Such is the great claim of Spiritual Magic, and it involves at least an aspiration of the highest imaginable kind. Its opposite exists in the counter-claim of the Black or Infernal Art, with all its bizarre horrors and barbaric, twisted processes. Through these, the initiates of forbidden knowledge employed their developed physical faculties in operations of darkness and destruction.

The third division of medieval esoteric science is, in some respects, the most important of all. Alchemy is not only the foundation of the experimental method that has transformed the face of the earth; it is not only the historical root <note>original: "radix"</note> of modern physics, including chemistry; it is not only a secret <note>original: "arcane"</note> process for the manufacture of material gold, but it has originated a theory...
