<meta>This page continues the introductory essay's exploration of the links between ancient mysticism and modern spiritual practices, specifically focusing on the connection between alchemy and magic.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

This is of the utmost importance to all present students of psychology.

I have traced the connection between ancient wonder-working <term>original: "thaumaturgic"</term> mysticism and modern mystic action. I have shown that the high priests <term>original: "hierophants"</term> of old were familiar with the spiritual phenomena of today. They claim to have made such progress in the paths where we are now slowly and painfully traveling that they had entered into the permanent possession of a power and knowledge which was dangerous or impossible for them to reveal. Consequently, they spoke of this knowledge in veiled language, but they nevertheless endeavored to extend it to others so that it might be preserved. To this end, they invented their symbols and allegories in the hope that a divine light would illuminate deserving seekers and enable them to penetrate to their inner significance. 

Now, in a large number of cases, the great initiates of ancient magic were also the leaders of alchemy, and these two branches of secret <note>original: "esoteric"</note> wisdom are intimately and curiously connected in both principle and practice. The doctrines of mystical and magical regeneration were explained by alchemical philosophers, and in return, the magicians taught the psychic manufacture of gold. Astrology lent to both the assistance of its traditional observations and the resources of its ancient <note>original: "archaic"</note> symbolism. Alchemists and magicians claim to possess the same tremendous secret and the same unspeakable <note>original: "indicible"</note> power. They worked with the same weapons following strictly identical methods, but in different fields of achievement: the material world was the territory of the followers of Geber <note>Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) was an 8th-century Arab alchemist whose writings formed the foundation for medieval European alchemy.</note>, while the realms of the mind were delivered to the disciples of the Magi. The highest inspirations of both schools appear to have been derived from the Hermetic books <note>A collection of ancient texts attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, combining philosophy, magic, and alchemy.</note>. Although the practical alchemy of the Christian age originated with the Arabian Geber, its sources must therefore be sought in the theosophy of the later Platonists <note>Philosophers who followed the teachings of Plato but added mystical and religious elements, particularly in the 3rd to 5th centuries AD.</note>.

Now, whether from hints contained in the Hermetic books, or from some adaptation of Kabbalism <note>A tradition of Jewish mysticism focused on the nature of the divine and the universe.</note>, or from whatever source the seeds came that grew in the minds of the alchemists, a theory of universal development—capable of being applied in almost any direction—was expressed in this branch of secret literature. This theory constitutes the general and explainable principle of the Secret Doctrine of Mysticism. I have described this theory at considerable length and, I believe, with a certain precision, in an account of the true principles of the Great Work <note>original: "magnum opus"</note> included in the "Lives of Alchemical Philosophers," recently published in London. This biographical work endeavors, through a consideration of the careers—

<summary>The author argues that ancient magic and alchemy are two sides of the same secret wisdom, both derived from Hermetic and Platonic sources, aimed at universal development and spiritual regeneration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Introductory Essay, specifically completing a sentence about master philosophers and their role in defining the nature of practical alchemy.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

...of master <note>original: "adept"</note> philosophers, to determine the true nature and object of practical alchemy. So far as this plan is fulfilled, it constitutes a suitable and necessary introduction to the study of historical <term>Hermetics (the study of ancient occult teachings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus)</term>. I do not propose to repeat here anything substantial that I have already stated elsewhere. The alchemical theory of universal development includes a philosophical prediction of the modern evolutionary hypothesis. Considering the scientific ignorance and darkness of the Middle Ages, this is simply bewildering. It justifies the indignant demand of one <note>original: "theosophical" — relating to mystical insight into the nature of God</note> writer for the return of its rightful property to "a plundered <note>original: "spoliated"</note> past." But the alchemical theory was created by thinkers who believed in the absolute reality of spiritual things. Their doctrine of evolution was extended to the soul and spirit of man, though alchemists rarely attempted to apply it outside the realm of metals.

I would direct the particular attention of earnest psychological researchers to this grand and important doctrine—the highest outcome of Hermetic speculation that has been openly passed down from antiquity. It is founded on a general assumption that the philosophers claim to have demonstrated as experimentally true in at least one kingdom of Nature. Namely, they claim that all existing substances—the substance of spirit and soul, of animal and vegetable life, of metals and of stones—contain elements or seeds of a higher perfection than they can normally show. There is no practical limit to their progress towards perfection, and man is the agent and distributor of Divine power for the development of his own latent energies and those of all earthly things. The union of individual consciousness with the universal consciousness of God was the high point of this theory as it applied to man. Similarly, the creation of a <term>tincture (a substance, often called the Philosopher's Stone, capable of transforming base metals into gold)</term> which would transform a million times its own weight into gold was its final goal regarding metals.

The unity and connection of Nature, despite its infinite variety of forms, was the first logical outcome of this assumption; the universal potential for improvement formed the bond of union. From this dual doctrine of fundamental unity and hidden power, a practical conclusion was drawn: the processes for developing inherent energies in the various kingdoms of Nature should be strictly parallel, while still respecting their formal differences. **That which is above is like that which is below, and that which is below is like that which is above, to perform the miracles of the one thing.** <note>original: "Quod superius sicut quod inferius, et quod inferius sicut quod superius ad perpetranda miracula rei unius." — This is the famous central maxim of the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

The alchemical doctrine of evolution is the philosophical basis of

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical theory of evolution, arguing that ancient master philosophers understood the potential for universal perfection in both the spiritual and material realms long before modern science. It emphasizes the Hermetic maxim of "as above, so below," linking the refinement of the human soul to the transmutation of metals.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Hermetics, evolution, theosophy, spiritual development, tincture, Emerald Tablet, transmutation, latent energy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Introductory Essay, elaborating on the connection between the "great work" of alchemy and the spiritual transformation of humanity.</meta>

### INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.

...the sublime claim of transcendental or spiritual magic, which I have already considered at such length, and the full resulting psychic importance of the literature of alchemy may be shown in a few words. Although it conceals the primary substance <note>original: "first matter"</note> of the "great work" <note>original: "magnum opus"</note>, it describes the processes which, given the primary substance, will ultimately eliminate the imperfections of metals. According to the theory, these processes have parallels in every department of Nature. Thus, the magical evolution, transformation <note>original: "transfiguration"</note>, or reconstruction of man is to be accomplished in a manner that is strictly similar to the reconstruction in the mineral world. 

As man is the subject of spiritual chemistry, the primary substance does not need to be sought in this division of the art. Likewise, because man is that mystical "philosophers' vessel" <note>original: "vas philosophorum"</note> which has always been a puzzle <term>crux (a central or difficult point)</term> for seekers from the days of Geber <note>Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) was an 8th-century polymath and is often considered the father of early chemistry.</note> onward, it is a plain case that the development of his hidden spiritual energies may be accomplished along the lines of the declared Hermetic processes as they are described in alchemical works—provided the assumptions contained in the general Hermetic theory have a basis in fact, as claimed. 

Now, the processes in question are described with a reasonable amount of clarity <note>original: "perspicuity"</note>. I suggest to those numerous students of psychology who are turning for light to the writings and to the alleged achievements of the old mystics that here is an adequate reason for their earnest and exhaustive study. There is some ground for believing that we may strike upon an untapped <note>original: "unwrought"</note> mine of spiritual possibilities in the hidden but not unreachable mysteries of alchemy.

The practical outcome of my own studies in this direction must be reserved for the present, as it would be unwise in the limited space of the current essay to reveal beforehand what I later hope to treat in a comprehensive and complete manner. My present object is to draw the attention of other investigators to the only lines of research which are likely to produce a definite and desired result, and if possible, to gain their collaboration in the first serious attempt at the mystical reconstruction of humanity. 

From the practical magic of the Middle Ages, we may learn that new and old psychological phenomena are identical. From <term>theurgic (referring to divine or supernatural magic)</term> philosophers, we may ascertain the true nature of the psychic achievements which transcendental magic claims to have accomplished. For the actual processes by which it attained its grand results, we must study the "assembly of philosophers" <note>original: "turba philosophorum"</note>—the long line of alchemists—and do so in a sequential and exhaustive manner. But we must carefully bear in mind that we are in search of the psycho-chemical process, which is connected to, but not identical with, the metallic process of the assembly <note>original: "turba"</note>.

<summary>This page argues that alchemical processes for refining metals are spiritual metaphors for the evolution and "reconstruction" of the human soul. The author suggests that by studying the history of alchemy, researchers can discover practical methods for unlocking latent spiritual energies in humanity.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, spiritual chemistry, magnum opus, Geber, Hermetic theory, transcendental magic, theurgic philosophy, turba philosophorum, psychology</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument that the alchemical transformation of metals serves as a perfect symbolic parallel for the spiritual evolution of the human soul. It encourages students to look past outdated scientific theories to find deeper psychological truths.</meta>

### INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.

...that the transformation <note>original: "transmutation"</note> of physical substances into actual gold was the goal of alchemy itself, and that it can only provide us with a comparison <note>original: "parallel"</note>. But the accuracy of this comparison is guaranteed by the theory: "From the greater to the lesser, from the lesser to the greater, the consequences are identically connected, and the proportions are increasingly exact <note>original: "progressively rigorous"</note>."

The student would also be wise to avoid getting discouraged by the outdated forms of reasoning and the debunked <note>original: "exploded"</note> physical ideas of all the Hermetic philosophers. Their concepts are quite crude and sometimes seem hardly consistent with sanity. However, their psychological <note>original: "psychic"</note> knowledge should not be judged by their lack of progress in physics; even a real process for transforming metals is not incompatible with a troubling ignorance of many laws of nature.

Everyone involved in the current revival of mysticism should strive—through logical and consistent study using an unbiased historical method—to recover some definite <note>original: "positive"</note> knowledge from its hidden retreats <note>original: "secluded sanctuaries"</note>. Interest that only shows itself through occasional <note>original: "spasmodic"</note> investigation is inconsistent; reckless and narrow-minded <note>original: "illiberal"</note> theories are its usual results. The secrets of hidden <note>original: "esoteric"</note> literature will only yield to the deep analysis of sympathetic minds. These minds must be properly prepared for the task by an understanding of modern psychological progress and must possess a level of ambition <note>original: "aspiration"</note> that matches the ambition of the high priests <term>hierophants (ancient Greek priests who interpreted sacred mysteries)</term>.

<summary>The author argues that while the scientific ideas of old alchemists are outdated, their spiritual and psychological insights remain valid and require serious, unbiased study. He emphasizes that understanding esoteric secrets requires a mind that is both intellectually prepared and spiritually dedicated.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Hermetic philosophy, mysticism, spiritual evolution, esoteric literature, hierophants, transmutation</keywords>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND TRULY
# REGENERATED BRETHREN
## R.C., <note>The "R.C." refers to the "Rosy Cross" or Rosicrucians, a mysterious and legendary secret society of scholars and mystics popular in the 17th century.</note>
## TO THE PEACE-LOVING APOSTLES OF THE CHURCH
## IN THIS CONTENTIOUS AGE,
### SALUTATION FROM THE CENTER OF PEACE.

<image-desc>A large ornamental initial letter 'I' within a square frame, decorated with intricate vine and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

Inasmuch as this dedicated gift <note>original: "votive offering"</note> addresses the high priest alone at the high altar, it might seem like a sacrilege to thrust it upon you. Even devotion has its limits; whoever approaches uninvited is guilty of audacity, not of service. That ancient *battle of the giants* <note>original: "gigantomachia"—a reference to the Greek myth where giants tried to overthrow the gods by storming heaven</note> of the poets concerned those who endeavored to take Heaven by storm; nor are fatuous and wicked "sparklers" <note>likely referring to superficial or false intellectuals</note> lacking in our own day, who dream themselves stars and believe that they are equal to the sun. Far be such arrogance and the peak of ambition from Eugenius! <note>The author’s pseudonym, Eugenius Philalethes.</note> This would be "to pile Pelion upon Ossa." <note>A classical idiom for an enormous but futile labor, referencing giants who stacked two mountains to reach the gods.</note>

I, most noble Brethren, am in the entrance hall <note>original: "vestibule"</note> of the Sanctuary <note>original: "Sacrarium"</note>, and I do not set my offering upon the altar, but, more modestly, upon the threshold. If admitted into your ranks, I would offer such things to you:

> Which future ages and posterity might 
> compare even to the writings of Arpinum. <note>original: "Quæ secula posterique possint Arpinis quoque comparare chartis." This quotes the Roman poet Martial; the "writings of Arpinum" refers to the works of the famous orator Cicero.</note>

But there is no reason why I should despair. There shall come those in the final times who will prefer my small torch to the "sun of Tuscany." <note>Likely referring to the great intellectual light of the Italian Renaissance or specifically to Galileo.</note> And indeed I am a colleague by that demonstration of Marcus Tullius Cicero, "because our leadership strives toward the same immortality." <note>original: "quod in eandem immortalem tendit noster consulatus."</note> I have wandered like the bees (not those of Quintilian <note>The Roman rhetorician Quintilian once wrote of bees in a poisoned garden to warn against learning from corrupt sources.</note> in poisoned gardens), touching lightly the Celestial Flowers, which derive their scents from the Aromatic Mountains. If there is any honey to be found here, I offer it to...

# DEDICATION.

...you this honeycomb and beehive. Roses, however, are usually stained upon the breasts of most people; perhaps this handful is also sullied, for I gathered it myself. Whatever is in error belongs to Eugenius; what remains belongs to Truth. Yet for what purpose is this testimony of Truth offered to you liberated souls, who enjoy the unveiled manifestation of the triple witness <note>original: "triplex Martyrium"—likely a reference to 1 John 5:8, "the Spirit, the water, and the blood," often interpreted alchemically as the three essential principles of life and matter.</note> of Spirit, of Water, and of Blood? This is no voice of help, however small, but a thing unnecessary. Wise is he who remains silent in the presence of Heaven! 

Receive therefore, most illustrious Brethren, this small offering <note>original: "mite"—a reference to the "widow’s mite" from the Bible (Mark 12:41–44), signifying a gift that is small in value but represents everything the giver has.</note>, not as something I should be offering to you, but as all that I am able to give. My good will is found in my willing service. This also my poverty declares: do not judge the gift itself, but the obedience of

->Your humble petitioner,<-

# THE AUTHOR TO THE READER.

<image-desc>A large, ornate woodcut initial letter 'L' decorated with intricate scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

Look on this life as the journey of a Royal Essence <note>original: "Essence Royall"—a reference to the soul as a noble or divine substance descending from heaven.</note>: the soul only leaves her court to see the country. Heaven contains within it a scene of Earth; and had she been contented with pure ideas, she would not have traveled beyond the map. But excellent patterns inspire their imitations: Nature, which was so beautiful in its original model <note>original: "type"—a theological or philosophical archetype or blueprint.</note>, could not be a mess in its physical manifestation <note>original: "Anaglyph"—literally a low-relief carving; here used to mean the physical, "embossed" world that reflects the spiritual model.</note>. This causes her to wander here to examine the medal by the mold <note>original: "flask"—a frame used in casting metal; the soul compares the finished product (the "medal") to the mold it came from.</note>, but while she examines their symmetry, she gives it form. Thus, her descent reveals her origin. God, in love with His own beauty, creates a glass <note>A mirror.</note> to view it through reflection; but because the fragility of matter excludes eternity, the composition was subject to breaking apart.

Ignorance gave this release the name of Death, but properly it is the soul's birth, and a charter that grants her liberty. She has several ways to leave her home, but her best departure is one not caused by disease. This is her mystical walk, an exit intended only for a return. <margin>Hebrews</margin> When she takes the air at this door, it is without harm to her dwelling. The magicians tell me, "The soul of one being goes out, and another enters" <note>original Latin: "Anima unius entis egreditur, et aliud ingreditur."</note>. Some have examined this and state it is an expenditure of influences, as if the soul exercised her royalty only through the eye, or held some blind authority at the pores. 

But this is to judge magical principles by the slight, superficial rules of common philosophy. This is an age of intellectual slavery; if people encounter anything extraordinary, they usually trim it with logical distinctions or smear it with false interpretations until it looks like the traditions of Aristotle <note>The author is criticizing the rigid "Scholastic" followers of the Greek philosopher Aristotle, who dominated university education at the time.</note>. His followers are so confident in his principles that they do not seek to understand what others are saying, but rather try to make others say only what they themselves understand. In Nature, it is just as it is in Religion: we are constantly reworking old elements but do not seek the "America" <note>A metaphor for a "New World" of undiscovered knowledge or spiritual truth.</note> that lies beyond them. The Apostle tells us of leaving the first principles of the doctrine of Christ and going on to perfection: not laying again the founda—

# THE AUTHOR TO THE READER.

...tion of repentance from dead works, and of faith toward God, of the doctrine of baptism, and of laying on of hands, of resurrection, and of eternal judgment. Then he speaks of illumination, of tasting the heavenly gift, of being made partakers of the Holy Spirit, of tasting the good word of God, and the powers of the world to come <note>These are references to Hebrews 6:1-5, describing the progression from basic religious instruction to deep spiritual experience.</note>. Now, if I were to ask any sect (for there is currently no true unified communion in Christendom) where these later spiritual experiences lead, they could only point me back to the first basic principles or offer some empty pretense of spirituality. 

Our natural philosophers are much like those who claim the privilege of prophets and try to predict events based on planetary alignments and motions. This is as logical as if I saw a Swede performing military drills and tried to discover his secret strategies just by looking at his postures. <margin>Lord Verulam <note>Francis Bacon</note> in his *Natural History*.</margin> Friar Bacon <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), the English philosopher and scientist famous for his experimental methods.</note> walked in Oxford between two steeples, but anyone who thought they could discover his thoughts by watching his footsteps would have been more of a fool than his companion. 

The Peripatetics <note>original: "Peripateticks"—followers of Aristotle's philosophy.</note>, when they define the Soul or some lower principle, describe it only by outward circumstances—which even a child can do—but they explain nothing of its true essence. Thus, they dwell entirely on the surface; their efforts are mere superficial tickling, and their acquaintance with Nature does not reach the heart. 

Nevertheless, I acknowledge that the Schoolmen <note>The medieval scholars who used logic and philosophy to support Christian theology.</note> are clever. They conceive irregular principles and prescribe rules for method, even though they lack actual substance. Their philosophy is like a church that is all discipline and no doctrine. For if you take away their introductions, their forms of arguing, and their reciting of different opinions along with several other digressions, the actual substance of these "Tostados" <note>original: "Tostati"—referring to Alfonso Tostado, a 15th-century theologian famous for writing incredibly long, commentary-heavy works; here used to mean long-winded writers who produce a vast volume of text with little core truth.</note> would scarcely amount to a single message <note>original: "Mercury"—possibly referring to the messenger of the gods or a small amount of alchemical "spirit."</note>. 

Furthermore, their Aristotle is a poet in his writing; his principles are mere fantasies, and they rely more on our willingness to agree with them than on his own solid foundation. This is why his followers, despite having the help of so many centuries, can get nothing out of him but abstract notions. Indeed, they use these notions as he says Lycophron <note>A Greek poet known for his obscure and difficult language.</note> used his descriptions: not as seasoning, but as the main food. 

Their writings are a mere swelling <note>original: "tympanie"—a medical term for a distended or bloated abdomen; here meaning "empty, inflated language."</note> of technical terms. It is more entertaining than a battle in *Don Quixote* to watch the duels and disputes <note>original: "digladiations"—literally "sword-fights."</note> they have over him. One will make him speak sense, another nonsense, and a third both. Thomas Aquinas handles him gently, Duns Scotus makes him flinch, and he is taught like a performing ape to show various tricks. 

If we look at his opponents, even the least of them has defeated him; Telesio <note>Bernardino Telesio (1509–1588), an Italian philosopher who attacked Aristotelian physics.</note> has dealt him a fatal blow, and Campanella <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), who argued for a philosophy based on sensory experience rather than Aristotle's logic.</note> has completely unsettled him. But just as that bald regular at the circus had a skull so hardened by use that it shattered all the tiles thrown at it, so this Aristotle thrives on conflict, and the world continues to praise him.

<summary>The author argues that contemporary scholars and religious sects focus on superficial rules and abstract logic rather than the true, essential nature of the soul and the world. He specifically critiques the "Schoolmen" and the enduring, though flawed, influence of Aristotle's philosophy.</summary>

### THE AUTHOR TO THE READER.

...when Truth cries him down. The Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy.</note> look on God as they do on carpenters who build with stone and timber, without any infusion of life. But the world, which is God's building, is full of spirit, quick <note>Archaic term for living or animated.</note> and living. This spirit is the cause of multiplication, of several perpetual productions of minerals, vegetables, and creatures engendered by putrefaction <note>The early scientific belief that certain organisms arose spontaneously from decaying matter.</note>, all of which are manifest, infallible arguments of life. Besides, the texture of the universe clearly discovers <note>reveals</note> its animation. The earth, which is the visible natural basis of it, represents the gross, carnal parts. The element of water corresponds to the blood, for in it the pulse of the Great World <note>The Macrocosm, or the universe conceived as a single living organism.</note> beats; this most men call the flux and reflux <note>The tides.</note>, but they do not know the true cause of it. The air is the outward refreshing spirit where this vast creature breathes—though invisibly, yet not altogether insensibly. The interstellar skies are his vital, ethereal waters, and the stars his animal, sensual fire. 

You will tell me perhaps that this is new philosophy, and that of Aristotle is old. It is indeed, but in the same sense as religion is at Rome <note>Likely a Protestant critique suggesting that just as Roman Catholicism was seen as a departure from primitive Christianity, Aristotelianism is a departure from original truth.</note>. It is not the primitive truth of the creation, nor the ancient, real Theosophy <note>Divine wisdom or mystical knowledge of God and nature.</note> of the Hebrews and Egyptians, but a certain supernatural upstart, a vomit of Aristotle which his followers lick up and swallow with such diligence. I do not present you here with any clamorous opposition to their patron, but a positive expression of principles as I find them in Nature. I may say of them as Moses said of the **FIAT** <note>The divine command "Let there be" from Genesis.</note>: "These are the generations of the heavens and of the earth, in the day that the Lord God made the heavens and the earth." They are things "beyond reasoning," sensible, practical truths, not mere vagaries and rambles of the brain. 

I would not have you look on my endeavors as a design for captivity; I intend not the conquest but the exercise of your reason—not that you should swear allegiance to my dictates, but compare my conclusions with Nature and examine their correspondence. Please consider that obstinacy enslaves the Soul and clips the wings which God gave her for flight and discovery. If you will not quit your Aristotle, let no prejudice hinder your further search. Great is the number of those who perhaps would have attained perfection had they not already thought themselves perfect. This is my advice, but how welcome it is to you, I do not know. If you will kick and fling, I shall say with the Cardinal, "My ass also kicks up his heels," for I value no man's censure. It is an age wherein truth is near a miscarriage, and it is enough for me that I have appeared thus far for it in a day of necessity.

E. S.

He encourages readers to use their reason to compare his ideas with Nature rather than blindly following established academic traditions.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the author's critique of Aristotelian philosophy to his personal philosophical journey, exploring the relationship between man, God, and the natural world following the Fall.</meta>

# ANTHROPOSOPHIA THEOMAGICA.

<image-desc>A large decorative square woodcut initial 'W'. The background consists of intricate scrolling foliate patterns and vines. At the bottom center of the square, there is a small, grotesque mask or face.</image-desc>

WHEN I discovered this truth, that man in his original state was a branch planted in God and that there was a continual influx from the stock to the scion, I was much troubled by his corruptions and wondered why his fruits did not correspond to his root. But when I was told he had tasted of another tree, my wonder quickly vanished, it being my chief care to restore him to his first simplicity and separate his mixtures of good and evil. But his Fall had so bruised him in his best part that his soul had no knowledge left to devise a cure for him; his punishment immediately followed his trespass: <margin>Cornelius Agrippa, *On the Vanity of the Sciences*.</margin>

> "All things became hidden, and oblivion, the mother of ignorance, entered in."

This Lethe <note>In Greek mythology, the river of forgetfulness; here used to represent the loss of divine memory or knowledge.</note> did not remain only in his body, but passing along with his nature, made his posterity its channel. Imperfection is an easy inheritance, but virtue seldom finds any heirs. Man had at the start, and so have all souls before their entrance into the body, an explicit, methodical knowledge; but they are no sooner vesselled <note>Contained within the "vessel" of the physical body.</note> than that liberty is lost, and nothing remains but a vast, confused notion of the creature. Thus I was left only with a capacity without power, and a will to do that which was far enough above me. In this perplexity, I studied several arts and rambled over all those inventions which the folly of man calls sciences; but these endeavors not suiting my purpose, I quitted this book-business and thought it a better course to study Nature rather than Opinion. Thereupon I considered with myself that man was not the primitive, immediate work of God, but the world was, out of which he was made. And to regulate my studies in point of method, I judged it convenient to examine its principles first, and not him. But the world in general being too large for investigation, I resolved to take a part for the whole and to guess at the structure by proportion. To perfect this my essay, I took to task the fruits of one spring. Here I observed a great many vegetables, fresh and beauteous in their time, but when I looked

<summary>Vaughan describes his realization that humanity's corruption stems from the Fall, which replaced divine knowledge with ignorance, prompting him to abandon traditional academic sciences to study Nature directly.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Anthroposophia Theomagica, The Fall of Man, Lethe, Cornelius Agrippa, Natural Philosophy, Macrocosm, Divine Knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical investigation into the origins of the natural world and the underlying "matter" of creation, following his realization that human knowledge was clouded by the Fall.</meta>

back on their origin, they were no such things as vegetables. I applied this observation to the world and gained from it this inference: that in the beginning, the world was no such thing as it is now,* but some other seed or matter out of which that fabric which I now behold arose. But, not resting here, I drove my conclusion further; I conceived that those seeds from which vegetables sprang must have been something else at first rather than seeds, having some pre-existent matter from which they were made, but what that matter might be, I could not guess. Here I was forced to leave off speculation and come to experience. While I sought the world, I went beyond it, and I was now in quest of a substance which I could not see without Art. Nature wraps this most strangely in her very bosom, nor does she expose it to anything but her own Vital Celestial Breath. But since God Almighty is the only proper immediate Agent who actuates this Matter, both in the work of generation and formerly in his creation, it will not be amiss to speak somewhat of him, so that we may know the Cause by his creatures, and the creatures by their Cause.

> My God, my Life, whose Essence man
> Is in no way fit to know or scan,
> But should approach Your Court a guest
> In thoughts more low than his request;
> When I consider how I stray,
> I think it is pride in me to pray.
> How dare I speak to Heaven, nor fear
> In all my sins to court Your ear?
> But as I look on moles that lurk
> In blind intrenchments, and there work
> Their own dark prisons to repair,
> Heaving the earth to take in air:
> So view my fettered Soul, that must
> Struggle with this, her load of dust,
> Meet her address, and add one ray
> To this caged portion of Your day.
> She would, though here imprisoned, see
> Through all her dirt Your Throne and You.
> Lord, guide her out of this sad night,
> And say once more, "Let there be Light."

<margin>Esdras.</margin>
It is God’s own positive truth: In the beginning, that is, in that dead silence, in that horrible and empty darkness when as yet nothing was fashioned, then (says the Lord) I considered those things, and they were all made through me alone, and through no other; by me also they shall be ended, and by no other.

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's transition from investigating physical matter to exploring the divine mind of the Creator, establishing that God conceived the world as an eternal idea before its material manifestation.</meta>

ANTHROPOSOPHIA THEOMAGICA. 11

Meditation precedes every solemn work; this is a thing so well known to man that he needs no further proof of it than his own practice. That there is also in God something analogous to it, from which man derived this customary notion of his, is as most agreeable to reason as it is also very suitable to Providence. “The Gods (says *Jamblichus*) conceived within themselves the whole design before they generated it.” And the Spirit here says to *Esdras*: "Then did I consider these things." He considered them first and made them afterward. God in his *Eternal Idea* foresaw that for which as yet there was no material copy. The goodness and beauty of the one moved him to create the other, and truly the image of this prototype being embraced in the second made Him so much in love with his creature that, when sin had defaced it, he restored it by the suffering of that pattern by which at first it was made. *Dionysius the Areopagite*, who lived in the primitive times and received the mysteries of Divinity immediately from the apostles, calls God the Father sometimes “the Arcanum of Divinity,” sometimes “that hidden supersubstantial Being,” and elsewhere he compares him to a root whose flowers are the *Second* and *Third Persons*. This is true; for God the Father is the basis or supernatural foundation of his creatures: God the Son is the Pattern in whose express image they were made: and God the Holy Spirit is the “Spirit-Fabricator,” or the Agent who framed the creature in a just symmetry to his Type. This consideration or type God has since used in the performance of inferior works. Thus in the institution of his Temple, he commands *Moses* to the Mount, where the Divine Spirit shows him the idea of the future structure: <margin>Exodus.</margin> "And let them make me a sanctuary that I may dwell among them, according to all that I show you, after the pattern of the Tabernacle, and the pattern of all its instruments; even so shall you make it." Thus the Divine Mind instructs us “by setting forth ideas by a certain self-extension beyond himself,” and sometimes more particularly in dreams. To *Nebuchadnezzar* he presents a tree strong and high, reaching to the heavens, and its sight to the ends of the earth. To *Pharaoh* he shows seven ears of corn. To *Joseph* he appears in sheaves, and then resembles the *Sun, Moon*, and *Stars*. To conclude, he may express himself by whatever he will, for in him are innumerable, eternal prototypes, and he is the true Fountain and Treasure of Forms. But so that we may come at last to the proposed purpose: *God the Father* is the *Metaphysical, Super-celestial Sun*,

---

<note>* The mystical writings attributed to Dionysius the Areopagite are now almost universally rejected as forgeries of the fifth century.</note>

<summary>The author argues that God conceived the universe as an internal "Eternal Idea" or prototype before creation, a process mirrored in human meditation and biblical visions. He describes the Trinity's roles in this process: the Father as the foundation, the Son as the pattern, and the Holy Spirit as the fabricator.</summary>

the Second Person is the Light, and the Third is “Fiery Love,” or a Divine Heat proceeding from both. Now, without the presence of this Heat there is no reception of the Light, and by consequence no influx from the Father of Lights. For this “Love” is the medium which unites the Lover to that which is beloved, and probably it is the Platonists’ “Chief Daemon, who does unite us to the Rulers of Spirits.” I could speak much more of the functions of this Loving Spirit, but these are “Grand Mysteries of God and of Nature,” and require not our discussion so much as our reverence. Here also I might speak of that supernatural generation, whereof Trismegistus says: “The one begets one, and does reflect upon itself its own brightness;” but I leave this to the Almighty God as his own essential, central mystery. It is my only intention in this place to handle exterior actions, or the process of the Trinity from the Center to the Circumference. And that I may the better do it, you are to understand that God before his work of creation was wrapped up and contracted in himself. In this state the Egyptians style him “Solitary Monad,” and the Cabalists “Dark Aleph;” but when the decreed instant of creation came, then appeared “Bright Aleph,” and the First Emanation was that of the Holy Spirit into the bosom of the matter. Thus we read that Darkness was upon the face of the deep, and the Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters. Here you are to observe that notwithstanding this process of the Third Person, yet was there no Light, but darkness on the face of the deep, illumination properly being the office of the second. Therefore God also, when the Matter was prepared by Love for Light, gives out his *Fiat Lux*, which was no creation as most think, but an Emanation of the Word, in whom was life, and that life is the light of men. This is that life whereof Saint John speaks, that it shines in the darkness and the darkness comprehended it not. But lest I seem to be unique in this point, I will give you more evidence. Pimander, informing Trismegistus in the work of the creation, tells him the self-same thing. “I am that Light, the Pure Intelligence, thy God, more ancient than the aqueous nature which shone forth out of the shadow.” And Georgius Venetus in his book *De Harmonia Mundi*: “Whatsoever lives does subsist by virtue of its inward heat; thence that substance of heat, indiscriminately distributed through the world, is held to contain within itself a vital strength; yea, Zoroaster testifies that all things were made out of fire, when he says: all things were produced by a single fire, that fire, to wit, which God, the inhabitant of essential flame (as Plato has it), did bid appear in the substance of Heaven,

He correlates these Christian concepts with the Hermetic, Platonic, and Cabalistic traditions to explain the transition from the "Solitary Monad" to the material world.</summary>

For this term, a false translation substitutes FIAT LUX, "let Light be made"; but the Light is in no way made, but rather communicated and admitted to things that were previously obscure, so that they may be clarified and made splendid in its beauty."

<margin>Genesis.</margin>
But to proceed: no sooner had the Divine Light pierced the bosom of the Matter than the Idea or Pattern of the whole material world appeared in those primitive waters like an image in a glass. It was by this pattern that the Holy Ghost framed and modeled the universal structure. This mystery or appearance of the Idea is excellently manifested in the magical analysis of bodies; for he who knows how to imitate the proto-chemistry of the Spirit by the separation of the principles in which life is imprisoned may see the impression of it experimentally in the outward natural garments. But lest you should think this is my own invention and not a practical truth, I will give you another man's testimony. "I ask," says one, "what such great philosophers would say if they beheld a plant born as if in a moment in a glass vial, with its colors as in life, and then die again, and be reborn, and that daily, and whenever they choose? But I believe they include the power to deceive human senses in the magical art of demons."* They are the words of Doctor Marci in his *Defensio Idearum Operatricium*. But you should be warned, there is a twofold Idea—Divine and Natural. The natural is a fiery, invisible, created spirit, and properly a mere enclosure or garment of the true one. Hence the Platonists called it "the Nimbus of the descending Divinity." Zoroaster and some other philosophers think it is "the Soul of the World," but, with their permission, they are mistaken; there is a wide difference between *Anima* and *Spiritus*. But the Idea I speak of here is the true, primitive, exemplary one, and a pure influence of the Almighty. Before the coagulation of the seminal principles into a gross, outward fabric—which is the end of generation—this Idea impresses a model or pattern into the vital ethereal principles, after which the body is to be framed; and this is the first inward production or draft of the creature. This is what the Divine Spirit suggests to us in that Scripture where He says that God created every plant of the field before it was in the ground, and every herb of the field before it grew. But despite this presence of the Idea in the matter, the creation was still not performed "by the projection of anything outside the essential

archetype," for it is God who comprehends His creature, and not the creature God.

Thus far I have handled this primitive, supernatural part of the creation. I must confess it is but short in respect to what might be said, but I am confident it is more than has been discovered formerly: some authors having not searched so deeply into the center of Nature, and others being unwilling to publish such spiritual mysteries. I have now come to the gross work or mechanics of the Spirit, namely, the separation of several substances from the same mass; but first, I shall examine that <term>Lymbus</term> or huddle of matter in which all things were so strangely contained. It is the opinion of some men, and learned ones at that, that this sluggish, empty rudiment of the creature was no created thing. I must confess the point is as obscure as the thing itself, and to state it with sobriety—unless a man were illuminated with the same Light that this <term>Chaos</term> was at first—is altogether impossible. For how can we judge a nature different from our own, whose species was also so remote from anything now existing that it is impossible for fancy to apprehend it, much less for reason to define it? If it is created, I conceive it to be the effect of the <term>Divine Imagination</term>, acting beyond itself in contemplation of that which was to come, and producing this passive darkness as a subject to work upon in the circumference. <term>Trismegistus</term>, having first expressed his Vision of Light, describes the matter in its primitive state thus:

> "And in a short time after (he says), the Darkness was thrust downwards, partly confused and dejected, and tortuously circumscribed, so that I appeared to behold it transformed into a certain humid substance, and afterwards, one might say, excited and vomiting forth smoke as from fire, and giving forth a mournful and inexpressible sound."

Certainly these *Tenebræ* he speaks of, or the smoky spawn of Nature, were the first created matter, for that Water we read of in Genesis was a product or secondary substance. Here also he seems to agree further with the <term>Mosaicall tradition</term>; for this "Smoke" which ascended after the transmutation can be nothing else but that Darkness which was upon the face of the Deep. But to express the particular mode or way of the Creation, you must understand that in the Matter there was a horrible confused qualm, or stupefying spirit of moisture, cold, and darkness. In the opposite principle of Light there was heat and its effect, <term>siccitie</term> <note>dryness</note>; for these two are no elemental qualities, as the <term>Galenists</term> and my <term>Peripateticks</term> suppose. But they are (if I may say so) the hands of the Divine Spirit by which He worked upon the Matter, applying every agent to its proper patient. These

two are active and masculine, while those of moisture and cold are passive and feminine. Now, as soon as the Holy Ghost and the Word (for it was not one or the other, but both—"the formative intelligence conjoined with the Word," as Trismegistus has it—I omit that speech, *Let us make man*, which effectively proves their union in the work) had applied themselves to the Matter, there was extracted from its bosom a third spiritual, celestial substance, which, receiving a tincture of heat and light proceeding from the Divine Treasures, became a pure, sincere, and harmless fire. The bodies of angels consist of this, as does the Empyreal Heaven, where intellectual essences have their residence. This was "the primeval marriage of God and Nature," the first and best of compositions. This extract, being thus settled above and separated from the mass, retained within it a vast portion of light and created the first day without a sun. But the splendor of the Word, expelling the darkness downwards, caused it to become more settled and compact toward the center, creating a horrible, thick night. Thus God (as the Hebrew text has it) was between the light and the darkness, for the Spirit still remained on the face of the inferior portion to extract more from it. In the second separation, "the nimble atmosphere" was drawn out, as Trismegistus calls it—a spirit not as refined as the former, but vital and in the next degree to it. This was extracted in such abundance that it filled all the space from the mass to the Empyreal Heaven, beneath which it was condensed into a water—though of a different constitution than the elemental kind—and this is the body of the interstellar sky. But my Peripatetics, following the principles of Aristotle and Ptolemy, have imagined so many wheels there, with their final diminutive epicycles, that they have turned that regular fabric into a rumbling, confused labyrinth. The inferior portion of this second extract, from the Moon to the Earth, remained air, partly to divide the inferior and superior waters, but chiefly for the respiration and nourishment of the creatures. This is what is properly called the Firmament, as is plain from Esdras: "On the second day you created the spirit of the Firmament"; for it is "the bond of all Nature," and in the outward geometrical composition, it corresponds to "the Middle Nature," for it is spread through all things, prevents a vacuum, and keeps all parts of Nature in a firm, invincible union.

<margin>Author of *Philosophia Restituta*</margin>
This is "the sieve of Nature," as one wittily calls it—a thing appointed for most secret and mysterious duties—but we shall speak further of it when we come to handle the elements particularly. Nothing now remained but the two inferior principles, as we

...commonly call them—Earth and Water. The Earth was an impure, sulfurous subsidence, or *caput mortuum* <note>The "dead head" or useless residue remaining after a chemical process</note> of the creation. The water was also phlegmatic, crude, and raw, not as vital as the former extractions. But the Divine Spirit, to make his work perfect, moving also upon these, imparted to them life and heat, and made them fit for future productions. <margin>Job.</margin> The Earth was so overcast and mantled with the Water that no part of it was to be seen; but so that it might be more immediately exposed to the Celestial Influences which are the cause of vegetation, the Spirit orders a retreat of the Waters, “breaks up for them his decreed place, and sets them bars and doors.” <note>A reference to Job 38:10</note> The Light as yet was not confined, but retaining its vast flux and primitive liberty, equally possessed the whole creature. On the fourth day it was collected into a sun, and taught to know its fountain. The Darkness, from which proceed the corruptions and, consequently, the death of the creature, was imprisoned in the center, but still breaks out when the day gives it leave, and like a defeated giant thrusts his head out of doors in the absence of his adversary. Thus Nature is a lady whose face is beautiful but not without a black mask. However, when it shall please God more perfectly to refine his creatures, this tincture shall be expelled quite beyond them, and thus it will be an outward darkness—from which Good Lord deliver us!

Thus I have given you a cursory and short account of the creation in general. I shall now descend to a more particular examination of Nature and especially her inferior, elemental parts, through which man passes daily, and from which he cannot be separated. I was about to stop here to prevent all future outcries, for when a Peripatetic <note>A follower of Aristotle's philosophy</note> finds here only three—no, only two—genuine elements (Earth and Water, for the Air is something more), will he not cry out that I have committed sacrilege against Nature and stolen the fire from her altar? This is noise indeed, but until they take a coach in a cloud and discover that idol they prefer next to the moon, I am resolved to continue my heresy. I am not only of the opinion, but I am certain there is no such principle in Nature. The Fire which she uses is “the physical and incorporeal horizon, the interbinding of both worlds, and the Seal of the Holy Ghost.” It is no chimerical, fictitious quirk like that of the schoolmen. I shall therefore request my friends the Peripatetics to return their fourth element to Aristotle, so that he may present it to Alexander the Great as the first part of a new world, for there is no such thing in the old.

To proceed then: the Earth (as you were told before) being the

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>ANTHROPOSOPHIA THEOMAGICA.</header>

The residue or remains of that primitive mass which God formed out of Darkness must necessarily be a feculent, impure body; for the extractions which the Divine Spirit made were pure, oily, ethereal substances, not the crude, phlegmatic, undigested humors settled like dregs toward the center. The Earth is spongy, porous, and magnetic; its composition is loose, the better to take in the several influences of heat, rains, and dews for the nourishment and conservation of her products. In her is the principal residence of that <term>Matrix</term> which attracts and receives the sperm from the Masculine part of the World. She is Nature’s Etna; here Vulcan exercises himself—not that limping, poetic one who halted after his fall, but a pure, celestial, plastic Fire <note>Formative or creative fire</note>. We have astronomy here under our feet; the stars are resident with us, along with an abundance of jewels and <term>pantauras</term> <note>Legendary stones or universal medicines</note>; she is the nurse and receptacle of all things, for the Superior Natures engulf themselves into her. What she receives in this age, she reveals to the next, and, like a faithful treasurer, conceals no part of her accounts. Her proper, congenial quality is cold.

I am now to speak of the Water. This is the first element we read of in Scripture, the most ancient of principles and the Mother of all things among visible objects. Without the mediation of this, the Earth can receive no blessing at all, for moisture is the proper cause of mixture and fusion. The Water has several complexions according to the several parts of the creature. Here below, and in the circumference of all things, it is volatile, crude, and raw. For this very reason, Nature makes it no part of her provision until she rectifies it first, exhaling it upward with her heat and thus condensing it into rains and dews, in which state she uses it for nourishment. Somewhere it is interior, vital, and celestial, exposed to the breath of the First Agent, and stirred with spiritual, eternal winds. In this condition it is Nature’s favorite—*Fœmina Fatacissima* <note>Most fertile woman</note>, as one calls it. This is that Psyche of Apuleius, and the Fire of Nature is her Cupid. He that has seen them both in the same bed will confess that love rules all. But to speak something of our common, elemental Water: it is not altogether contemptible; there are hidden treasures in it, but so enchanted we cannot see them, even though the chest is transparent. “The congealed spirit of the Invisible Water is better than the whole earth,” says the noble and learned *Sendivogius*. I do not advise the reader to take this phlegm to task as if he would extract a *Venus* from the sea, but I wish him to study Water so that he may know the Fire.

I have now handled the two Elements, and more I cannot find:

<page-num>18</page-num>

I know the *Peripatetics* claim there are four elements, and, with the help of their master's quintessence, a fifth principle. I shall reduce their stock at my leisure, but the subject to be spoken of now is *Air*. This is no element, but a certain miraculous hermaphrodite, the cement of two worlds, and a mixture of extremes. It is *Nature's* common place, her index, where you may find all that she ever did or intends to do. This is the *World's* panegyric; the travels of both globes meet here, and I may call it the rendezvous. Within it are innumerable magical forms of men and beasts, fish and fowls, trees, herbs, and all creeping things. This is "the sea of things invisible," for all the conceptions "in the bosom of the superior Nature" wrap themselves in this gauze before they embark in the shell. It retains the species of all things whatsoever and is the immediate receptacle of spirits after death, from where they pass to a *Superior Limbus*. I would amaze the reader if I related the several duties of this body, but it is the *Magician's* back door, and none but friends enter through it. I shall say nothing more—only this I would have you know: the *Air* is "the envelope of the life of our sensitive spirit," our animal oil, the fuel of the vital, sensual fire, without which we cannot survive for a minute.

<margin>Cornelius Agrippa.</margin>
I have now come to the fourth and last substance, the highest in "the *Scale of Nature*." There is no fifth principle, no quintessence, as Aristotle dreamed, but God Almighty. This Fourth Essence is a moist, silent *Fire*. This *Fire* passes through all things in the world, and it is *Nature's* chariot; she rides in this; when she moves, this moves; and when she stands still, this stands still, like the wheels in *Ezekiel* whose motion depended on that of the *Spirit*. This is the mask and screen of the *Almighty*; wherever He is, this train of *Fire* attends Him. Thus, He appears to *Moses* in the bush, but it was in *Fire*; the prophet sees Him break out from the north, but like a *Fire* enfolding itself. At *Horeb* He is attended by a mighty strong wind tearing the rocks to pieces, but after this comes the *Fire*, and with it a still small voice. *Esdras* also defines Him as a God whose service is performed in *Wind* and *Fire*. This *Fire* is the garment of the *Divine Majesty*—His "back parts" which He showed to *Moses*, but His naked, royal essence no one can see and live—the glory of His presence would swallow up the natural man and make him entirely spiritual. Thus Moses' face, after speaking with Him, shines, and from this small tincture we may guess at our future state in the *Regeneration*. But I have touched the veil and must return to the outer court of the *Sanctuary*.

I have now in some measure performed what I first promised: an exposition of the World and its parts; but out of my affection for Truth and the dominion I wish for her, I shall be somewhat more specific in the examination of Nature and proceed to a further discovery of her riches. I advise the reader to be diligent and curious in this following part of the discourse, so that having once attained the fundamentals of science, he may better understand its superstructures.

Know then that every Element is threefold, this triplicity being the express image of their Author, and a seal He has laid upon His Creature. There is nothing on earth, no matter how simple, how vile or abject in the sight of man, but it bears witness of God—even to that abstruse mystery, His Unity and Trinity. Every compound whatsoever is three in one and one in three. The basest reptile, even in its outward symmetry, testifies of its Author, its several proportions answering to their eternal, superior Prototype. Now man has the use of all these creatures, God having furnished him with a Living Library in which to employ himself. But he, neglecting the works of his Creator, pursues the inventions of the creature, laps up the vomit of Aristotle and other illiterate heathens—men who, concerning the faith, are reprobate, and in the Law of Nature altogether unskilled, scribbling blasphemous atheists "whose souls listen to be distracted and torn asunder, and who behold hell" (as Agrippa has it). He is much troubled by those mysteries of the Trinity and the Incarnation; one denies, another grants them; but if they did once see the Light of Nature, they might find those mysteries by reason which are now above their faith. When I speak of a natural triplicity, I speak not of kitchen-stuff—those three pet principles, Water, Oil, and Earth—but I speak of celestial, hidden natures, known only to absolute magicians, whose eyes are at the center, not on the circumference; and in this sense, every element is threefold. For example, there is a threefold earth: first there is "the elementary earth," then there is "the celestial earth," and, lastly, "the spiritual earth." The influences of the spiritual earth, by the mediation of the celestial, are united to the terrestrial and are the true cause of life and vegetation. These three are the fundamentals of Art and Nature. The first is a visible, tangible substance—pure, fixed, and incorruptible—cold in quality, but by the application of a superior agent, dry, and consequently a fit receptacle for moisture. This is "Created Aleph," the true "Adamic Earth," the basis of every building in Heaven and Earth. It answers to God the Father, being the natural foundation of the creature, as He is

the supernatural. Without this, nothing can be perfected in Magic. The second principle is the infallible Magnet, the Mystery of Union. By this, all things may be attracted, whether physical or metaphysical, no matter how great the distance. This is Jacob’s ladder; without this, there is no ascent or descent, whether through influence or in person. I believe the absence of this is that Gulf between Abraham and Dives. This corresponds to God the Son, for it is that which mediates between extremes and allows the lower and the higher to communicate. But there is not one in ten thousand who knows either the substance or the use of this nature. The third principle is not properly a principle. It is not “from whom,” but “by which all things exist.” This can do everything, and its faculties cannot be expressed. It corresponds to the Holy Ghost, for among natural things, it is the only agent and craftsman. Now, whoever knows these three perfectly, along with their various levels or connected links—which do not differ in substance but in quality—and whoever can reduce their impurities to one pure consistency, and their many parts to a spiritual, essential simplicity, he is an absolute, complete magician, and fully capable of all strange and miraculous feats. Secondly, you must learn that every Element is twofold. This duality or confusion is that *Binarius*, mentioned by Agrippa in *Scalis Numerorum*, as well as by both himself and Trithemius in their letters. Other authors who dealt with this science were meddlesome scribblers and did not understand this “Secret of the Shades.” This is where the creature goes astray and falls from its original Harmonic Unity. You must therefore subtract the Duad, and then the magicians’ Triad may be reduced “by the Tetrad into the extreme simplicity of Unity,” and consequently, “into a metaphysical union with the Supreme Monad.”

The sun and moon are two magical principles, the one active, the other passive; the first masculine, the second feminine. As they move, so move the wheels of decay and creation. They naturally dissolve and combine, but the moon is specifically “the instrument for the transformation of lower Nature.” These two luminaries are multiplied and bear fruit in every individual creation. There is no compound in all of nature that does not have in it a little sun and a little moon. The little sun is “the Son of the celestial Sun,” and the little moon is “the Daughter of the celestial Moon.” Whatever duties the two great luminaries perform for the preservation of the great world in general, these two little luminaries perform the same for the preservation of their small cask, or Microcosm, in particular. They are “Miniatures of the greater Living Being”—Heaven and Earth in a smaller script. God, like a wise Architect, sits in the center of all,

repairs the ruins of his building, composes all disorders, and maintains his creature in his first, primitive harmony. The moon is “that well-watered and many-founted moist principle,” at whose top sit Jove and Juno on a throne of gold. Juno is an incombustible, eternal oil, and therefore a fit receptacle of Fire. This Fire is her Jove, the little sun we spoke of formerly; these are the philosophers' *Sol* and *Luna*, not gold and silver, as some mountebanks and charlatans would have it. But because I have proceeded this far, I will give you a true recipe of the Medicine—

> “Ten parts of celestial slime, separate the male from the female, and each afterwards from its earth, physically, take note, and with no violence. Conjoin after separation in due, harmonic, vital proportion; and, straightway, the Soul descending from the pyroplastic sphere, shall restore, by a miraculous embrace, its dead and deserted body. The conjoined substances shall be warmed by a natural fire in a perfect marriage of spirit and body. Proceed according to the Vulcanico-Magical theory, till they are exalted into the Fifth Metaphysical Rota. This is that world-renowned medicine, of which so many have scribbled and which so few have known.” *

<margin>Revelation.</margin>
It is a strange thing to consider that there are in Nature incorruptible, immortal principles. Our ordinary kitchen fire, which in some measure is an enemy to all compositions, nevertheless does not so much destroy as purify some parts. This is clear from the ashes of vegetables, for although their weaker exterior elements expire by the violence of Fire, yet their Earth cannot be destroyed, but vitrified. The fusion and transparency of this substance is caused by the radical moisture or seminal water of the compound. This water resists the fury of the fire, and cannot possibly be vanquished. “The rose lies hidden through the winter in this water” (says the learned Severine). These two principles are never separated, for Nature proceeds no further in her dissolutions. When death has done its worst, there is a union between these two, and out of them shall God raise us at the last day, and restore us to a spiritual condition. Besides, there remains in them that primitive universal tincture of the Fire; this is still busy after death, brings Nature again into play, produces worms, and other inferior generations. I do not conceive there shall be a Resurrection of every species, but rather their terrestrial parts together with the element of water (for “there shall be no more sea”) shall be united in one mixture with the Earth, and fixed to a pure, transparent substance. This is St. John’s Crystal Gold, a fundamental of the

These enduring elements, purified and vitrified, are presented as the physical basis for the final Resurrection and St. John's "Crystal Gold."</summary>

Thus, Reader, I have made a full but short inquiry into the Mysteries of Nature. It is more than has been discovered until now, and therefore I expect more opposition. I know my reward is calumny, but he who has already condemned the vanity of opinion is not likely to respect that of censure. I shall now put the creatures to their proper use and, from this shallow contemplation, ascend to mine and their Author.

It is necessary now, if we follow that method of which God Himself is the Author, to examine the nature and composition of Man, having already described those Elements or principles of which he was made and consists. Man, if we look at his material parts, was taken out of the great world as woman was taken out of man. I shall therefore, to avoid repetitions, refer the reader to the former part of this discourse; where, if things are rightly understood, he cannot be ignorant of his material frame or composure. We read in Genesis that God made him out of the Earth: this is a Great Mystery. For it was not the common pot-clay, but another, and that of a far better nature. He who knows this knows the subject of the philosophical medicine and, by consequence, what destroys or preserves the temperament of Man. In this are principles homogeneous with his life, such as can restore his decays and reduce his disorders to a harmony. Those who are ignorant on this point are competent

If these words be well examined, you may possibly find it out, and so much for his body. His Soul is an essence not to be found in the fabric of the great world, and therefore purely divine and supernatural. Montanus calls it "the Wind of the Divine Spirit and the Breath of the Life Divine." He seems also to make the creation of man a little Incarnation, as if God in this work had multiplied Himself. Adam (says he) received his Soul "by a wonderful and unparalleled inspiration and fructification of God (if it be lawful so to speak)." St. Luke also tells us the same thing, for he makes Adam the son of God, not in respect of the exterior act of creation, but by way of descent, and <margin>Acts.</margin> this St. Paul confirms in the words of Aratus: "For we also are his generation." The soul of man consists chiefly of two portions—*Ruach* and *Nephes*—inferior and superior. The superior is masculine and eternal, the inferior feminine and mortal. In these two <margin>Arias Montanus.</margin> consists our spiritual generation. "As, however, in the rest of the animal world, and also in man himself, the conjunction of male and female tends towards a fruit and propagation worthy of the nature of each; so in man that interior and secret association of male and female, to wit, the copulation of the rational soul and the animal life, is appointed for the production of fitting fruit of Divine Life. And unto this does that arcane benediction and endowed fecundity, that revealed faculty, and warning, refer—Increase, and multiply, and replenish the earth, and subdue it, and have dominion." Out of this, and some former passages, the understanding reader may learn that marriage is a commentary on life, a mere hieroglyphic, or outward representation of our inward vital composition. For life is nothing else but a union of male and female principles, and he who perfectly knows this secret knows the Mysteries of Marriage, both spiritual and natural, and how he ought to use a wife. Matrimony is no ordinary trivial business, but in a moderate sense sacramental. It is a visible sign of an invisible union to Christ, which St. Paul calls a Great Mystery, and if the thing signified be so revered, the signature is no offhand, contemptible agent. But of this elsewhere. When God had thus finished his last and most excellent creature, he appointed his residence in Eden, made him his viceroy, and gave him a full jurisdiction over all his works, so that as the whole man consisted of body and spirit, the inferior earthly creatures might be subject to the one, and the superior Intellectual Essences might minister to the other. <meta>This page continues the discussion of man’s origins, moving from the creation of his dual-natured soul to the celestial rebellion and the subsequent fall of man in Eden.</meta>

Immediately upon his promotion, there was a faction in the Heavenly Court, and the Angels, scorning to serve this piece of clay, conspired how to overthrow him. The first in this plot was *Lucifer*—*Montanus* tells me his name was *Hilel*. He plotted to nullify what God had enacted, so that he might simultaneously overreach Him and His creature. He shared this strategy with others of the Hierarchy and strengthened himself with conspirators. But there is no wisdom against God: the mischief was no sooner hatched than he and his confederates were expelled from Light into Darkness; and thus rebellion is like the sin of witchcraft—a witch is a rebel in physics, and a rebel is a witch in politics. The one acts against nature, the other against order, which is the rule of nature; but both are in league with the devil, as the primary father of discord and sorcery. *Satan*, having been thus ejected, as is the condition of the damned, became more hardened in his resolutions; and to carry out his malice, he arrived by permission at Eden. Here he made Woman his instrument to tempt Man, and overthrew him by the same means that God had made as a help for him. Adam, having thus transgressed the commandment, was exposed to punishment, and his posterity along with him. But here lies the problem: how can we possibly learn about his disease if we do not know its immediate cause? If I ask our theologians what the Forbidden Fruit was, I may wait a long time without an answer. Search all the schoolmen from *Ramus* to *Peter of Spain*, and they have no logic on this point. What shall we do in this case? To speak anything contrary to the authority of *Aristotle* (though perhaps we hit the mark) is to expose ourselves to public outcry; but because I prefer a private truth to a public error, I will proceed. And now, reader, "prick up your ears," approach without prejudice, and I will tell you that which has never before been discovered.

What I am writing now must necessarily appear very strange and incredible to the common man, whose knowledge remains stuck on the surface of allegories and mystical speeches, never grasping what is signified by them to us. This, I say, must inevitably sound strange to those who understand the Scriptures in a literal, plain sense, failing to consider the scope and intention of the Divine Spirit by whom they were first written and delivered. Nevertheless, *Origen*, being "one chosen out of many" and, in the judgment of many wise men, the most learned of the Church Fathers, never dared to trust his own judgment on this point; instead, in those Scriptures where his reason could not find satisfaction, he always concluded there was a mystery.

Certainly, if it is once granted (as some do not hesitate to affirm) that the Tree of Knowledge was a vegetable and Eden a garden, it may

it could be very well affirmed that the Tree of Life, being described in the same category (as the schoolmen express it), was also a vegetable. But let any impartial Christian judge how derogatory this is to the power of God and to the merits and passion of Jesus Christ, whose gift is eternal life. Here then we have a certain entrance into Paradise, where we may search for this Tree of Knowledge and (perhaps) learn what it is. For since it must be granted that by the Tree of Life is figured the Divine Spirit (for it is the Spirit that quickens, and shall one day translate us from corruption to incorruption), it will be no indiscreet inference on the contrary that by the Tree of Knowledge is signified some sensual nature, repugnant to the spiritual, wherein our worldly sinful affections—such as lust, anger, and the rest—have their seat and predominate.

I will now digress a while, but not much from the purpose, by which it may appear to the reader that the letter is no sufficient expositor of Scripture, and that there is a great deal of difference between the sound and the sense of the text. Dionysius the Areopagite in his Epistle to Titus gives him this *caveat*: “And to know this is, nonetheless, the value of the business—that the tradition of theologians is twofold—the one mystical and secret, the other manifest and more known.” And in his book of the *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*, written to Timothy, he affirms that in the primitive, Apostolical times, the mysteries of Divinity were delivered “partly in written and partly in unwritten canons.” Some things, he confesses, were written in the theological books, and such are the common doctrines of the Church now; in which, nonetheless (as St. Peter says), “there are many things hard to be understood.” Some things again, “which wholly transcended carnal understanding, were transmitted without writing from mind to mind, being concealed between the lines of the visible word.” And certainly this oral tradition was the cause that in the subsequent ages of the Church, all the mysteries of Divinity were lost. Indeed, to this very day there is not one among all our school-doctors, or late extemporaries, that knows what is represented to us by the outward element of Water in Baptism. True indeed, they tell us it signifies the washing away of sin, which we grant them, but this is not the full meaning for which it was ordained. It has been the common error of all times to mistake *signum* for *signatum*, the shell for the kernel; yet to prevent this, it was that Dionysius wrote his book of the *Celestial Hierarchy*, and especially his *Theologia Significativa*, of which there is such frequent mention made in his works. Verily, our Saviour Himself, who is blessed for evermore, did sometimes

Supposing then (as is most true) that, among other mystical speeches contained in Scripture, this of the Garden of Eden and the trees in it is one, I shall proceed to the exposition of it in some measure, concealing the particulars notwithstanding.

<margin>*Genesis.*</margin>
<margin>*Genesis.*</margin>
Man in the beginning (I mean the substantial inward man), both in and after his creation, for some short time, was a pure intellectual *essence*, free from all fleshly, sensual affections. In this state the *Anima*, or *sensitive nature*, did not prevail over the spiritual as it does now in us. For the superior mental part of man was united to God "by an essential contact," and the Divine Light being received in, and conveyed to, the inferior portions of the soul, did mortify all carnal desires, to such an extent that in Adam the sensitive faculties were scarcely at all employed, the spiritual prevailing over them in him, as they do over the spiritual now in us. Hence we read in Scripture, that during the state of innocence he did not know that he was naked, but no sooner does he eat of the Tree of Knowledge than he saw his nakedness, and was ashamed of it; why also he hides himself among the trees of the garden, and when God calls to him, he replies: "I heard your voice in the garden, and I was afraid because I was naked, and I hid myself." But God, knowing his former state, answers him with a question: "Who told you that you were naked? Have you eaten of the tree, of which I commanded you that you should not eat?" Here we see a twofold state of man—his first and best in the spiritual substantial union of his intellectual parts to God, and the mortification of his ethereal sensitive nature, wherein the fleshly, sinful affections had their residence—his second, or his fall, in the eating of the forbidden fruit, which did cast asleep his intellectual faculties, but did stir up and exalt the sensual. For (says the serpent) "God does know that in the day you eat thereof, then your eyes shall be opened, and you shall be as gods, knowing good and evil. And when the woman saw that the tree was good for food, and that it was pleasant to the eyes and a tree to be desired to make one wise; she took of the fruit thereof, and did eat, and gave also to her husband, with her, and he did eat; and the eyes of both were opened, and they knew that they were naked." Thus we see the sensual faculties revived in our first parents and brought "*from potentiality into activity*," as the schoolmen speak, by virtue of this forbidden fruit. Neither did this eating suppress the intellectual powers in Adam
