only, but in all his generations after him, for the influence of this fruit passed together with his nature into his posterity. We are all born like Moses with a veil over the face; this is what hinders the prospect of that intellectual, shining light which God has placed in us. And to tell a truth that concerns all mankind, the greatest mystery both in divinity and philosophy is how to remove it.

It will not be amiss to speak somewhat in this place of the nature and constitution of man, to make more plain what has already been spoken.

As the <term>Great World</term> consists of three parts—the <term>Elemental</term>, the <term>Celestial</term>, and the <term>Spiritual</term>, above all which God himself is seated in that infinite, inaccessible Light which streams from his own nature, even so man has in him his earthly, elemental parts, together with the celestial and angelical natures, in the center of all which moves and shines the <term>Divine Spirit</term>. The sensual, celestial, <term>ethereal nature</term> of man is that by which we move, see, feel, taste, and smell, and have a commerce with all material objects whatsoever. It is the same in us as in beasts, and it is derived from Heaven, where it is predominant, to all the inferior earthly creatures. In plain terms, it is part of the <term>Soul of the World</term>, commonly called the <term>Medial Soul</term>, because the influences of the Divine Nature are conveyed through it to the more material parts of the creature, with which of themselves they have no proportion. By means of this Medial Soul, or Ethereal Nature, man is made subject to the influence of stars, and is partly disposed of by the <term>Celestial Harmony</term>. For this middle part (middle I mean between both extremes, and not that which actually unites the whole together), as well that which is in the outward heaven as that which is in man, is of a fruitful, insinuating nature, and carried with a strong desire to multiply itself, so that the Celestial Form stirs up and excites the Elemental. For this spirit is in man, in beasts, in vegetables, in minerals, and in everything it is the mediate cause of composition and multiplication. Neither should any wonder that I affirm this spirit to be in minerals because the operations of it are not discerned there. For shall we conclude therefore that there is no inward agent that actuates and specifies those passive, indefinite principles whereof they are compounded? Tell me not now of blind <term>Peripatetical</term> forms and qualities! A form is that which Aristotle could not define substantially, nor any of his followers after him, and therefore they are not competent judges of it. But, I beseech you, are not the faculties of this spirit suppressed in man also when the organs are corrupted, as it appears in those that are blind? But, notwithstanding the

eye only is destroyed and not the visual power, for that remains, as it is plain in their dreams. Now this vision is performed by a reflection of the <term>visual radii</term> in their inward, proper cell. For Nature employs her gifts only where she finds a convenience and fit disposition of organs; since these are not in minerals, we may not expect so clear an expression of the natural powers in them. Notwithstanding, in the flowers of several vegetables (which in some sort represent the eyes), there is a more subtle, acute perception of heat and cold, and other celestial influences than in any other part. This is manifest in those herbs which open at sunrise and shut towards sunset, which motion is caused by the spirit being sensible of the approach and departure of the sun. For indeed the flowers are (as it were) the spring of the spirit, where it breaks forth and streams, as it appears by the odors that are more celestial and comforting there. Again, this is more evident in the plant-animals, as the <term>Vegetable Lamb</term>, the *<term>Arbor Casta</term>*, and several others. But this will not be understood by any except those who have seen this spirit separated from its elements, where I leave it for now.

Next to this sensual nature of man is the angelical or rational spirit. This spirit adheres sometimes to the *<term>mens</term>*, or superior portion of the Soul, and then it is filled with the Divine Light; but most commonly it descends into the ethereal inferior portion, which St. Paul calls the natural man, where it is altered by the celestial influences and diversely distracted by the irregular affections and passions of the sensual nature.

Lastly, above the Rational Spirit is the *<term>Mens</term>*, or Concealed Intelligence, commonly called *<term>Intellectus Illustratus</term>*, and by Moses the <term>Breath of Life</term>. This is that spirit which God himself breathed into man, and by which man is united again to God. Now, as the Divine Light, flowing into the *<term>mens</term>*, did assimilate and convert the inferior portions of the Soul to God, so, on the contrary, the Tree of Knowledge did obscure and darken the superior portions, but awakened and stirred up the animal, sinful nature. The sum of all is this: man, as long as he continued in his union with God, knew the good only, that is, the things that were of God; but as soon as he stretched forth his hand and ate of the <term>Forbidden Fruit</term>—that is, the <term>Medial Spirit</term>, or <term>Spirit of the Greater World</term>—presently upon his disobedience and transgression of the commandment, his union with the Divine Nature was dissolved, and his spirit being united to the spirit of the world, he knew the evil only, that is, the things that were of the world. It is true that he knew both the good and the evil, but the evil in a far greater measure than the good.

Some sparks of grace were left, and though the perfection of innocence was lost upon his fall from the Divine Light, yet conscience remained still *with him*, partly to direct, partly to punish. Thus you see that this *Anima Media*, or Middle Spirit, is figured by the Tree of Knowledge, but he who knows why the Tree of Life is said to be in the middle of the Garden, and to grow out of the ground, will more fully understand what we have spoken.*

We see, moreover, that the faculties which we have ascribed to the Tree of Knowledge are to be found only in the Middle Nature. First, it is said to be a tree "desired to make one wise," but it was fleshly, sensual wisdom—the wisdom of this world and not of God. Secondly, it is said to be "good for food and pleasant to the eyes"; so is the Middle Nature also, for it is the only medicine to repair the decays of the natural man, and to maintain our bodies in their original strength and integrity.

Lastly, so that I may speak something for myself: this is no new, unheard-of fancy, as the discerning reader may gather from Trismegistus. Indeed, I am truly of the opinion that the Egyptians received this knowledge from the Hebrews, who lived a long time among them, as it appears from Scripture, and that they passed it over to the Greeks. This is plain from Iamblichus in his book *De Mysteriis*, where he has these words:

> "The intellectual man, reflecting within himself, was formerly joined to the contemplation of the gods; afterwards, however, there entered in another soul, coexistent with the human form, and on that account he was bound by the same bond of fate and of necessity."

And what else, I ask you, is signified to us in that poetic fable of Prometheus?—that he should steal a certain fire from Heaven, for which transgression afterward, God punished the world with many diseases and mortality.

But someone may reply: Seeing that God made all things good, as it appears in His review of the creatures on the sixth day, how could it be a sin in Adam to eat that which in itself was good? Truly, the sin was not grounded in the nature of what he ate, but it was the consequence of the commandment, insofar as he was forbidden to eat it. And this is what St. Paul tells us, that he would not have known sin had it not been for the law; and again, in another place, "the strength of sin is the law." But immediately upon the disobedience of the first man, and his transgression of the commandment, the creature was made subject to vanity, for the curse followed, and the impure seeds were joined with the pure, and

<margin>Job.</margin>
They reign to this hour in our bodies, and not in us alone, but in every other natural thing. This is why we read in Scripture that "the Heavens themselves are not clean in his sight," and to this the apostle alludes in his speech to the Colossians, saying that "it pleased the Father to reconcile all things to himself by Christ, whether they be things in earth or things in Heaven." And here you should observe that Cornelius Agrippa mistook the act of generation for original sin—which was indeed its effect—and this is the only point in which he has erred.

I have now finished—only a word more concerning the location of Paradise, especially because of the diversity of opinions concerning that place and their absurdity. St. Paul in his second Epistle to the Colossians <note>The author likely refers to 2 Corinthians 12:2-4, as the passage quoted is found there; St. Paul only wrote one Epistle to the Colossians.</note> reveals it in these words: "I knew a man in Christ over fourteen years ago (whether in the body or out of the body, I cannot tell, God knows) such a one caught up to the third Heaven. And I knew such a man (whether in the body or out of the body, I cannot tell, God knows) how he was caught up into Paradise." Here you see that Paradise and the Third Heaven are interchangeable terms, so that the one reveals the other. I could have said much more concerning the Tree of Knowledge, as it is in itself a large and very mystical subject, but for my part, I rest content with my own particular understanding and do not desire to enlarge upon it any further. Nor would I have committed even this much to paper except out of my love for the Truth, and because I did not want these thoughts to perish altogether.

You see now, if you are not men of a most obtuse mind, how man fell, and, consequently, you may guess by what means he is to rise. He must be united to the Divine Light, from which he was separated by disobedience. A flash or <term>tincture</term> of this must come, or he can no more discern things spiritually than he can distinguish colors naturally without the light of the sun. This Light descends and is united to him by the same means as his Soul was at first. I speak not here of the symbolic, exterior descent from the prototypical planets to the created spheres and from there "into the night of the body," but I speak of that most secret and silent descent of the Spirit "through the sequence of natural forms," and this is a mystery not easily understood. It is a Cabalistical maxim—*Nulla res spiritualis descendens inferius operatur sine indumento*—"No spiritual entity descending into our inferior plane can manifest within it without a garment." Consider this well for yourselves, and take care that you do not wander in the circumference. The Soul of man, while she is in the body, is like a candle shut up in a

He argues that man's spiritual restoration requires a reunion with the Divine Light through a secret descent of the Spirit into the body.</summary>

dark lantern, or a fire that is almost stifled for want of air. Spirits
<margin>Proclus— *On the Soul*</margin> (say the Platonists) when they are "in their own country," are like the inhabitants of green fields, who live perpetually among flowers in a spicy, odorous air, but here below, "in the circle of generation," they mourn because of darkness and solitude, like people locked up in a pest-house. "Here they do fear, desire and grieve." This is what makes the soul subject to so many passions, to such a Proteus of humors. Now she flourishes, now she withers, now a smile, now a tear, and when she has played out her stock, then comes a repetition of the same fancies, until at last she cries out with Seneca, "How long shall these things continue?" This is occasioned by her vast and infinite capacity, which is satisfied with nothing but God, from whom at first she descended. It is miraculous to consider how she struggles with her chains when man is in extremity, how she falsifies with fortune, what pomp, what pleasure, what a paradise she proposes to herself! She spans kingdoms in a thought, and enjoys all that inwardly which she misses outwardly. In her are patterns and notions of all things in the world. If she but fancies herself in the midst of the sea, presently she is there, and hears the rushing of the billows. She makes an invisible voyage from one place to another, and presents to herself things absent as if they were present. The dead live to her; there is no grave can hide them from her thoughts. Now she is here in dirt and mire, and in a trice above the moon:

->Above the region of the storms she soars,<-
->Beneath her feet she hears devolving clouds,<-
->And under foot she thrusts the thunders blind.<-

But this is nothing. If she were once out of the body, she could act all that which she imagined. "In a moment (says Agrippa) whatever she desires shall follow." In this state she can "act on the fluids of the Macrocosm," make general commotions in the two spheres of air and water, and alter the complexions of times. Neither is this a fable, but the unanimous tenet of the Arabians, with the two Princes Avicebron and Avicenna. She has then an absolute power in miraculous and more than natural transmutations. She can in an instant transfer her own vessel from one place to another. She can (by a union with universal force) infuse and communicate her thoughts to the absent, be the distance never so great. Neither is there anything under the sun but she may know it, and remaining only in one place, she can acquaint herself with the actions of all places whatsoever. He it is to whom we must be united by “an essential contact,” and then we shall know all things, “manifested face to face through a clear sight into the Divine Light.” This influx from Him is the true, proper cause of our regeneration, that *sperma* of St. John, the seed of God which remains in us. If this is once obtained, we do not need to serve under Aristotle or Galen, nor trouble ourselves with foolish *Utrums* <note>Latin for "whether," used in scholastic debates to pose a question.</note> and *Ergos* <note>Latin for "therefore," used to conclude a logical syllogism.</note>, for His anointing will instruct us in all things. But indeed the doctrine of the Schoolmen, which in a manner makes God and nature opposites, has so weakened our confidence toward Heaven that we look upon all receptions from there as impossibilities. But if things were well weighed, and this cloud of tradition removed, we would quickly find that God is more ready to give than we are to receive; for He made man (as it were) for His playfellow, so that he might survey and examine His works. The inferior creatures He made not for themselves but for His own glory, which glory He could not receive from anything as perfectly as from man, who, having the spirit of discernment within him, might judge the beauty of the creation and consequently praise the Creator. <margin>Genesis.</margin> Therefore God also gave him the use of all His works, and in Paradise, how familiar He is—or rather, how He plays with Adam! “Out of the ground (says the Scripture) the Lord God formed every beast of the field, and every bird of the air, and brought them to Adam to see what he would call them; and whatever Adam called every living creature, that was its name.” These were the books which God ordained for Adam, and for us his descendants, not the quintessence of Aristotle, nor the temperament of Galen the Antichrist. with a *tradatur Satanæ* <note>Latin: "handed over to Satan."</note>. I know I shall be hated by most for my efforts, and perhaps mocked like *Pythagoras* in *Lucian*.
> “Who buys *Eugenius*? Who wishes to be above the best of men? Or to know the Harmony of the World and to live anew?”
But because, according to their own master, an oath is most honorable, and since an affirmation of this nature cannot fail for a Christian, I will come to my oath. I do therefore protest before my glorious God, I have not written this out of malice, but out of zeal and affection for the truth of my Creator. Let them take heed then, lest while they condemn Mysteries, they violate the Majesty of God in His creatures, and trample the Blood of the Covenant underfoot. But shall I not be counted a conjurer, seeing I follow the principles of Cornelius Agrippa, that grand Arch-mage, as the anti-Christian *Jesuits* call him? He indeed is my author, and next to God I owe all that I have to him. Why should I be ashamed to confess it? He was, reader, noble by birth, a *Protestant* by religion, as it appears from his own writings, besides the late but malicious testimony of *Promondus*, a 
<margin>*Promondus—In his critique of the desperate cause of Gisbertus Voetius.*</margin>
learned *Papist*. Regarding his course of life, he was a man famous in his person for both actions of war and peace, a favorite to the greatest princes of his time, and the just wonder of all learned men. Lastly, he was one who carried himself above the miseries he was born to, and made fortune know that man might be her master. This is answer enough to a few sophists, and, in defiance of all slanders, I salute his memory thus.

> Great glorious penman! whom I should not name,
> Lest I might seem to measure you by fame,
> Nature’s apostle and her choice high-priest,
> Her mystical and bright Evangelist,
> How am I raptured when I contemplate you,
> And wind myself above all that I see?
> The spirits of your lines infuse a fire
> Like the World’s Soul, which makes me thus aspire.
> I am unbodied by your books and you,
> And in your papers find my ecstasy;
> Or if I please but to descend a level,
> Your elements do screen my Soul again.
> I can undress myself by your bright glass,
> And then resume the enclosure as I was.
> Now I am earth, and now a star, and then
> A spirit; now a star and earth again.
> Or if I will but ransack all that exist,
> In the least moment I encompass all three.

> And with no arguments but noise and pride—
> You that damn all but what you yourselves invent,
> And yet find nothing by experiment;
> Your fate is written by an unseen hand,
> But his *Three Books* with the *Three Worlds* shall stand.

<margin>Genesis.</margin>
Thus far, reader, *I* have handled the composition and royalty of man; *I* shall now speak something of his dissolution, and close up my discourse, as he does his life, with death. Death is "the recession of life into the *Unknown*," not the annihilation of any single particle, but a retreat of hidden natures to the same state they were in before they were manifested. This is caused by the disproportion and inequality of the matter, for when the harmony is broken by the excess of any one principle, the vital twist (without a timely reduction to unity) disbands and unravels. In this recession, the several ingredients of man return to those several elements from which they first came during their entry into a compound; for to think that God creates anything from nothing in the work of generation is a pure metaphysical whimsy. Thus the Earthly parts, as we see by experience, return to the Earth, the celestial to a superior, heavenly *Limbus*, and the Spirit to God who gave it. Nor should anyone wonder that *I* affirm the Spirit of the living God to be in man, when God himself does acknowledge it for his own. "My spirit (he says) shall not always be sheathed (for so the Hebrew signifies) in man, for he also is flesh, yet his days shall be one hundred and twenty years." Besides, the breathing of it into Adam

proves it proceeded from God, and therefore is the Spirit of God. Thus Christ breathed on his Apostles, and they received the Holy Ghost. In Ezekiel, the Spirit comes from the four winds and breathes upon the slain, that they might live. Now this Spirit was the Spirit of Life, the same as that Breath of Life which was breathed into the first man, and he became a living soul. But, without doubt, the Breath or Spirit of Life is the Spirit of God. Neither is this Spirit in man alone but in all the Great World, though in another manner. For God breathes continually, and passes through all things like an air that refreshes—which is why he is also called by Pythagoras “the animating principle of all.” Hence it is that God in Scripture has several names according to those several offices he performs in the preservation of the creature. “Nay also (says the Areopagite) they declare him to be present in our minds, and in our souls, and in our bodies, and to be in Heaven equally with earth, and in himself at the same time; the same also they declare to be in the world, around the world, above the world, above the Heaven, the superior Essence, sun, star, fire, water, spirit, dew, cloud, the very stone, and rock, to be in all things which are, and himself to be nothing which they are.” And it is most certain because of his secret passage and penetration through all, that the other simile in Dionysius was given him. “He joins himself to the nature of Adam (says he), and to that which is most lowly and irrational, to the worm itself—so has it come down to us from those who in former times were versed in things Divine.”

Now this figurative kind of speech, with its variety of names, is not only proper to Holy Writ, but the *Egyptians* also (as *Plutarch* tells me) called *Iris*, or the most secret part of Nature, *myrionymos*, and certainly that the same thing should have a thousand names is no news to such as have studied the *Philosophers’ Stone*. But to return to the point from which we have digressed. I told you the several principles of man in his dissolution part, as sometimes friends do, in several ways—earth to earth, as our liturgy has it, and Heaven to Heaven, according to that of *Lucretius*.

> And that which first did issue from the earth
> Does now disintegrate to earth again;
> And what was missioned from ethereal shores,
> That Heaven’s resplendent temples welcome back.

But more expressly the Divine *Virgil*, speaking of his Bees—

> And by these signs, by this example set,
> Within the bees a part of *Mind Divine*
> And the ethereal sources they discern.

> For through the length and breadth of every land,
> Dim ocean’s depths and Heaven’s effulgent heights;

> The beasts of burden, and the race of man,

This vanishing or ascent of the inward ethereal principles does not immediately follow their separation. For that part of man which Paracelsus calls the Sidereal Man, and more appropriately “the Brute part of man,” but Agrippa calls “the spectre” and Virgil:

->“The ethereal sense, and heat of simple breath,”<-

this part, I say, which is the Astral Man,<note>Note 8.</note> sometimes hovers about the burial places of the dead, and that is because of the magnetism, or sympathy, which exists between it and the radical, vital moisture. In this “Spectre” is the seat of the Imagination, and it retains after death an impression of those passions and affections to which it was subject in the body. This makes it haunt those places where the whole man had been most familiar, and imitate the actions and gestures of life. This magnetism is excellently confirmed by that memorable incident at Paris which Dr. Fludd proves to be true by the testimonies of great and learned men. Agrippa also, speaking of the apparitions of the dead, has these words: “But what I myself have seen with my own eyes and touched with my own hands, I will not refer to in this place, lest it be my lot to be accused of falsehood by the ignorant on account of the stupendous strangeness of the occurrences.” But this scene does not exceed the span of one year, for when the body begins fully to corrupt, the Spirit returns to its original element. These apparitions have made a great noise in the world, not without some benefit to the pope. But I shall reserve all for my great work, where I shall more fully handle these mysteries.

I am now to speak of man as he is subject to a supernatural judgment. And, to be short, my sentiment is this: I conceive there are, besides the Empyreal Heaven, two inferior Mansions, or Receptacles of Spirits. The one is that which our Savior calls “the outer darkness,” and this is from where there is no redemption—

Those Seven Mighty Mountains, upon which there grow Roses and Lilies, or the outskirts of Paradise in Esdras. Such was that place where the oracle told Amelius the soul of Plotinus was—

> ->With sempiternal streams ambrosial,<-

<margin>1 Pet. 4:6.</margin>
Stellatus supposes there is a successive, gradual ascent of the Soul, according to the process of expiation, and he places her intermediate residence in the Moon. But, wherever it may be, my opinion is that this middlemost mansion is appointed for those souls whose whole person has not perfectly repented in this world, but who, nonetheless, are “of the number of the saved,” and are reserved in this place for further repentance in the spirit for those offenses they committed in the flesh. I do not here support that Will-o'-the-Wisp of Purgatory, or any such artificial, imaginary Tophet; but what I say (if I am not mistaken) I have strong Scripture for. It is that of St. Peter, where he speaks of Christ being “put to death in the flesh, but quickened by the Spirit; by which also he went, and preached unto the spirits that were in prison, which once were disobedient when the long-suffering of God waited in the days of Noah, while the ark was being prepared, wherein few, that is, eight, souls were saved by water.” These spirits were the souls of those who perished in the Flood, and were reserved in this place until Christ should have come and preached repentance unto them. I know Scaliger thinks to avoid this interpretation with his *Qui tunc* [Who then], arguing that they were then alive—namely, before the Flood—when they were preached to. But I shall overthrow this piece of nonsense with three solid reasons, drawn from the body of the text. First, it is not said that the Spirit itself precisely preached unto them, but He who went there by the Spirit, namely, Christ in the hypostatical union of his Soul and Godhead, which union did not exist before the Flood, when these dead people were living. Secondly, it is written that he preached unto spirits, not to men; to those who were in prison, not to those who were “in life,” which is quite contrary to Scaliger. And the apostle confirms this interpretation in another place—“to them that are dead,” the dead were preached to, not the living. Thirdly, the apostle says: these spirits were only once dis-

<meta>This page continues the author’s theological interpretation of 1 Peter, specifically regarding the state of souls who died during the Flood, and transitions into a personal defense of his religious identity and a critique of contemporary academic philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

obedient, and at the same time tells us when—namely, in the days of Noah—from which I gather they were not disobedient at this time of preaching; and this is plain from the following chapter.

> "For this cause (says the apostle) the gospel was preached also to those who are dead, that they might be judged according to men in the flesh, but live according to God in the Spirit." Now this judgment in the flesh was grounded on their disobedience in the days of Noah, for which they were also drowned, but salvation according to God in the Spirit proceeded from their repentance at the preaching of Christ, which was after death. I do not impose this on the reader as if I sat in the infallible chair, but I am confident the text itself will speak no other sense. As for the doctrine, it is in no way harmful, but, in my opinion, as it does not detract from the mercy of God, so it adds much to the comfort of man.

I shall now speak a word more concerning myself, and another concerning the common philosophy, and then I have done. It will be questioned, perhaps, what I am, and especially what my religion is. Take this short answer: I am neither Papist nor sectary, but a true, resolute Protestant in the best sense of the Church of England. As for philosophy, as it now stands, it is altogether imperfect, and at the same time false—a mere apothecary's drug, a mixture of inconsistent, contrary principles which in no way agree with the harmony and method of Nature. In a word, the whole <term>Encyclopædia</term> (as they call it), except for the demonstrative, mathematical part, is built on mere imagination without the least light of experience. I wish, therefore, all the true sons of my famous Oxford mother to look beyond Aristotle, and not confine their intellects to the narrow and cloudy horizon of his text, for he is as far short of Nature as the grammarians are of <term>steganography</term>. I expect no thanks for this my advice or discovery, though perhaps I deserve well for both; but, truly, the time will come when this truth shall be more perfectly manifested, and especially that great and glorious mystery of which there is little spoken in this book. "The King Messiah alone, the Word made flesh of the Father, reveals this Arcanum, hereafter to be more openly manifested in a certain fullness of time"—Cornelius Agrippa's own prediction, and I am confident it shall find enough patrons when nothing remains here of me but my memory.

<meta>This page concludes the author's work with a devotional poem and a final address to the reader, explaining the circumstances of the book's composition and the author's linguistic background.</meta>

> I know Your hand’s not short; but I’m unfit—
> A foul, unclean thing!—to take hold of it.
> I am all dirt, nor can I hope to please,
> Unless in mercy You love a disease.
> Diseases may be cured, but who would reprieve
> Him that is dead? Tell me, my God, I live.
> It is true I live, but I so sleep withal,
> I cannot move, and scarcely hear, when You call.
> Sin’s lullabies charm me when I would come,
> But draw me after You, and I will run.
> You know I’m sick; let me not be feasted,
> But keep a diet, and one prescribed by You.
> Should I carve for myself, I would exceed
> To surfeits soon, and by self-murder bleed.
> I ask for stones and scorpions, but am still crossed,
> And all for love—should You grant them, I were lost;
> Dear Lord, deny me still, and never sign
> My will but when that will agrees with Yours;
> And when this conflict’s past, and I appear
> To answer what a patient I was here—
> How I did weep when You did woo, and repine
> At Your best sweets, and in a childish whine
> Refuse Your offered love, yet cry and call
> For rattles of my own to play withal—
> Look on Your Cross and let Your Blood come in
> When mine shall blush as guilty of my sin;
> Then shall I live, being rescued in my fall—
> A text of mercy to Your creatures all,
> Who, having seen the worst of sins in me,
> Must needs confess the best of loves in You.

I have now finished, reader, but how much to my own prejudice I cannot tell. I am confident this shall not pass without noise, but I may do well enough if you grant me but one request. I would not have you look here for the decoration and polish of rhetoric, and all the more because English is a language the author was not born to. Besides, this piece was composed in haste, and in my days of mourning on the sad occurrence of a brother’s death. “And who knows how to write amidst a strife of tears and ink?”

To conclude—if I have erred in anything (and yet I followed the rules of creation), I do not expose it to the mercy of man, but of God, who as He is most able, so also is He most willing to forgive us in the day of our accounts.

# AN ADVERTISEMENT TO THE READER.

If the old itch of scribbling—a disease very common to Galenists <note>Followers of the traditional medical system of Galen</note>—surprises any of their tribe, I shall expect from them the following performances. First, a plain, positive exposition of all the passages in this book, without any injury to the meaning of their author; for if they interpret them otherwise than they ought, they only create errors of their own and then overthrow them.

Secondly, to prove their familiarity and knowledge in this art, let them give the reader a precise disclosure of all its secrets. If this is more than they can do, it is argument enough that they do not know what they oppose; and if they do not know, how can they judge? Or if they judge, where is their evidence to condemn?

Thirdly, let them not mangle and disarrange my book with a scattering of observations, but proceed methodically to the critique of each part, explaining what is obscure and revealing the very practice, so that the reader may find my positions to be false—not only in their theory but, if he will test it, by his own particular experience.

<margin>Proclus.</margin>
I have two more admonitions for the honest and well-disposed reader. First, that he would not slight my efforts because of my years, which are but few. It is the custom of most men to measure knowledge by the beard, but look instead upon the Soul—an essence of that nature “which requires not the passage of time for its perfection.” Secondly, that he would not conclude anything rashly concerning the subject of this art, for it is a principle not easily understood. It is neither earth nor water, air nor fire. It is not Gold, Silver, Saturn, Antimony, or Vitriol, nor any kind of mineral whatsoever. It is not blood, nor the seed of any individual, as some unnatural, obscene authors have imagined. In a word, it is no mineral, no vegetable, no animal, but a system, as it were, of all three. In plain terms, it is *Sperma Majoris Animalis* <note>The seed of the Greater Animal, referring to the Macrocosm or Universe</note>, the seed of Heaven and earth, our most secret, miraculous Hermaphrodite. If you know this, and, with it, the Hydro-pyro-magical art, you may with some security attempt the work—if not, practice is the way to poverty. Attempt nothing without science, but confine yourself to those bounds which Nature has prescribed for you.

->THE END.<-

### TO THE READER.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'N' featuring scrolled foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

NOW God defend! What will become of me? I have neither consulted with the stars, nor their urinals, the almanacs. A fine fellow, to neglect the prophets who are read in England every day! They shall pardon me for this oversight. There is a mystery in their profession they have not even heard of—“the Christian starry Heaven”—a new Heaven imagined over the whole earth. Here the twelve Apostles have overtaken the zodiac, and all the saints are arranged on their North and South sides. It would be a pretty vanity to preach when St. Paul is rising, and would not a papist smile to have his pope elected under St. Peter? Reader, if I studied these things, I should not think myself any worse employed than the Roman Chaucer was in *Troilus*. I come out as if there were no hours in the day, nor planets in the hours; neither do I care for anything but that interlude of Perrendenga in Miguel Cervantes:

> “Let the old man, my master, live, and Christ be with us all.”

You will wonder now where this is going, for I have neither a Count of Lemos nor a cardinal to pray for. I pray for the dead—that is, I wish him a fair remembrance whose labors have deserved it. It happened while exposing my former discourse to criticism (a custom that has strangled many truths in the cradle) that a learned man suggested to me some bad opinion he had of my author, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa. I have always understood it was not just one person, but many, in whose opinion that miracle suffered. It is the fate of profound writers to be misunderstood because of their obscurity; thus the spots on the moon, to some men, are earth, but it is more probable they are water. There is no day so clear that there are not dregs towards the horizon; so, inferior wits, when they reflect on higher intellects, leave a mist in their beams. Had he lived in ignorance, as most do, he might have passed from here like the last year’s clouds, without any further remembrance. But as I believe the truth is a main branch of that end for which I was born, so I hold it my duty to vindicate him from

from whom I have received it. Since the world was then unable to refute this man's principles by reason, it attempted to do so through scandal; the first argument they used was the same one the Jews used against the Savior: "You are a Samaritan and have a devil." The leader in this persecution is Cicognes, followed by Delrio in his legendary *Disquisitions*. But Paulus Jovius stirred in the filth, who, among his other *biographies*, has put my author to death. It is done very emphatically between him and his poet, whom he seemingly hired to attach verse to his prose, and thus patched together the legend. "Who would believe," he says, "that a monstrous disposition was hidden by the calm face of Henry Cornelius Agrippa!" In his following discourse, he presents his argument and turns my author's best qualities into a libel against his memory. 

But what troubles him most of all is that Agrippa would prove his doctrine using the Scriptures. Then he repeats the solemn, repetitive story of his "dog-devil," whose collar, symbolically decorated with nails, served as a ruff for his familiar spirit. To conclude the story, he kills him off at Lyons, where, being close to death, he renounced his magic with this desperate dismissal: "Begone, abandoned beast, you who have lost me everything!" This is a most blatant lie, and the least probable in every detail that has ever been told. Devils are not accustomed to leaving their conjurors on the day of death, nor will they be driven away at such times. This is the hour in which they wait for their prey, and change from being apparent servants into cruel masters. 

Besides, is it not completely absurd that anyone could track this devil from Agrippa’s lodging to the Saône <note>The text uses the Latin name Araria for the river Arar, now the Saône.</note>, where (says this prelate) he threw himself in? Certainly, spirits pass away invisibly, and with such speed that no mortal man can track them. If you believe this, you might as well believe all the fables of Purgatory. Now, reader, you have heard the worst; listen fairly and you shall hear the best. Johannes Wierus, an open opponent of ceremonial magic and formerly secretary to Cornelius Agrippa, speaks thus in his *Dæmonomania*. He wonders why some learned Germans and Italians were not ashamed to slander his master in their public writings. He admits that Agrippa had a dog named *Monsieur*, and while Wierus was in his service, Agrippa used to lead this spaniel on a hair chain when he walked outside. "And certainly the dog was a natural male animal," he says, to which Agrippa also paired a female dog of the same color named *Mademoiselle*. It is admitted that he was fond of this dog, as some men are, and having divorced his first wife, he would jokingly allow the dog to sleep with him under the sheets. In his study, too, this dog would lie by the table near his master, from which this great

The philosopher, “absolutely surrounded by his extraordinary manuscript treasures,” would sometimes not stir out for a whole week together. He was so dedicated to the benefit of posterity, who have only coldly rewarded him for his pains. I have also observed in his “Letters” that when he was resident at Malines, his servants used to give him an account in their letters of how his dogs fared, so fond was he of those creatures. But to come to the rest of the legend: Paulus Jovius tells you he died at Lyons “in a squalid and gloomy inn.” But Wierus, who had more reason to be inquisitive about his master’s death, tells me he died at Grenoble, and that he died “in the Lord,” not in despair as his enemies would have it. Here now was a noble stride from Gratianopolis to Lugdunum; surely this Paul was a poor geographer. But, reader, it is not my intention to conceal anything in this matter; know, therefore, that Agrippa had another dog, his Filioli, and this last one died with more respect than most of his master’s adversaries. Because my author, having uniquely trained him by some secret means, several learned men wrote epitaphs for him, some of which have been published and are still extant. From this fable of the Cerberus, Baptista Possevinus produced these verses:

> All you who, living, gaze upon this tomb,
> And think what lies within is worthy of rest,
> Know, here entombed, is the king of the abysmal Styx,
> Protected on earth by a guard from hell,
> But now surrendered to perdition
> As his warden’s prey. Oh! if only he had restrained his mind,
> He might have risen to the heights as far
> As he is now deeply plunged into perdition.

Thus they have thoroughly demonized him; but why may truth not be written in verse as well as scandal?

> Thus great Agrippa illuminated the twofold world,
> And in a weak frame
> Revealed his manifold abilities.
> By earth he conquered earth, by Heaven he gained,
> And mastered heavenly things; while alive, he wrote
> Amidst the praise of the wise,
> Attracting Nature’s self by natural things,
> And so for things celestial. Divine Life
> Recognized this spirit as its own.
> He taught in life, and teaches still in death,
> And while his course ascends up starry heights,
> His hands still dispense some magic power.

<meta>This page continues the defense of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, shifting from the circumstances of his death to an argument for his religious and intellectual integrity, portraying him as a man of reformed faith who rejected superstition and the abuses of the Roman Church.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

...against the German conjurer hosted in the French court. Indeed, he was so zealous and conscientious that, when asked by some theologians for a commentary on <term>Trismegistus</term>, he gave them a very sharp answer, referring all true knowledge to the Scripture. In a word, he did not only hate impious arts but also vain ones; for he lost the favor of the Queen Mother because he would not be employed by her in astrology. He was miraculously skilled in the true natural part of that science, which concerns generation and corruption, for he knew it was pointless to look for fatal events in the planets. Such things are not written in Nature, but in the higher tables of predestination. Having thus sufficiently proved his integrity, I will in a few words reveal the grounds of his persecution. He was a man of reformed religion, and if I had the leisure to cite his works, I could quickly prove he was not part of the Roman Church. For in his book *On the Vanity of All the Sciences*, he does not approve of monks and friars, but calls them sects "of which the Church at its best was devoid." And certainly that famous joke of his about the monk's cowl irritates the Papists to this day. He also rejects their images, their invocation of saints, their purgatory and pardons, and wanted the laity to receive communion in <term>both kinds</term> <note>referring to the Protestant practice of the laity receiving both the bread and the wine in the Eucharist</note>. He rebukes the Pope himself adequately and is utterly against the Office of the Inquisition. It is also not hard to guess what his opinion was of Luther from his letters; for in a letter to <term>Melanchthon</term> he has these words: "Greet for me that invincible heretic, Martin Luther, who (as Paul says in the Acts) serves God according to that sect which they call heretical." Lastly, he was entirely for the written word, preferring it to human laws, which is contrary to the Papists, who will not allow it to be the judge of controversies. This is the man, and such were his qualities at home, however the world has portrayed him abroad. Now, as for his more mysterious principles, you have their name in this discourse; if you can grasp them, I know you will call him specifically—as Trismegistus calls man in general—"the interpreter of God," or as Panaetius called his Plato, "the most divine, most holy, most wise man, and the Homer of philosophers." But this careless arrangement does not suit his memory, and these things come from me now as brief notes, not formal compositions. I shall say nothing more, but leave you to your studies while I translate this epitaph by Platina for his sixth volume.

->Do not seek to disturb the dead Agrippa,<-
->Nor what lies narrowly enshrined with him,<-
->Which only asks to be left alone at rest.<-

<meta>This page transitions from the defense of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa's character and religious views to a critical examination of the "Peripatetics" (followers of Aristotle) and their philosophical system, which the author views as detached from reality.</meta>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

<margin>Genesis.</margin>
<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial block containing the letter 'T', embellished with ornate floral and foliate scrolls.</image-desc>
To build castles in the air is a common proverb with all men, but a common practice with the Peripatetics only. I have sometimes marveled that the very end and result of their philosophy did not clearly reveal its falsity. It is a mere help to discourse. Mood and figure are their two pillars, their limit; their heptarchy ends in a syllogism, and the best professor among them is but a well-disciplined scold. Their seven years’ study are seven years of famine; they leave the soul "not satisfied," and are more a dream than that of Pharaoh. For truly, if the stage and reign of dreams is nowhere beyond fancy, then the fancies of these men, being nowhere beyond their authors, may rest on the same pillow. This sect, then, may be called "an assembly of dreamers;" their conceptions are not grounded on any reason existing in Nature, but they would base Nature on reasons framed and principled by their own conceptions. Their philosophy is built on general empty maxims—things of such stretch and latitude they may be applied to anything, but lead to the discovery of nothing. These are the first outlines of their monster, and in reference to them they have many subordinate errors, which claim a symmetry with their fundamentals, but in truth have none at all. These latter subtleties are so minced with divisions and distinctions that their very patrons are uncertain how to explain them. I could compare their physiology to a hunting scene in an Arras tapestry, where there is much resemblance but nothing of truth. It is the child of fancy, a romance in syllogisms, a texture of their own brain, like that cobweb landscape which Lucian’s spiders spun between the Moon and Venus. "Nature in general (they say) is the principle of motion and rest." A form is "the outward expression of an inward essence"—a definition they know not what to make of—and the soul is <term>ἐντελέχεια</term> <note>entelechy</note> or, the actuating principle of a mechanical body. These two last descriptions (for they are no

He likens their academic pursuits to a dream or a tapestry, lacking any grounding in physical reality.</summary>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

substantial definitions) are such riddles that I truly believe Aristotle used those words “form” and “actuality” because he did not want to reveal his ignorance on these points. For why should a form be called *Logos*, or in what other author do we find this “actuality”? But because Nature in general—that is, in her active and passive portions, namely, matter and form, together with the Soul of man—are the main foundations on which to build a philosophy, and because this Aristotle is so revered by his followers that the theologians of Cologne tell us he was a “precursor of Christ in natural things” as John the Baptist was in things of grace, I shall further examine these definitions of his and acknowledge the benefit when I find it.

In the first place, then, it might be thought that I am indebted to this man for telling me that Nature is a principle. In the same way, I might tell the reader that the Magician’s Passive Spirit is a principle; but if I do not tell him what kind of substance it is, I will grant him ten years of study, and even if the Sun went back ten degrees on his dial every day, he shall not know what or where it is without supernatural assistance. But you will reply: He tells me further that it is a principle that causes bodies to move and rest. I thank him for nothing. I do not desire to know what this principle does, for that is obvious to every eye; but I want to know what it is, and therefore he may keep his definition. Again you will object: He tells me not only that Nature is a principle, but that “Nature is form,” and, consequently, “form is Nature.” This is *idem per idem* <note>the same by the same</note>; he keeps me in a circle of notions but resolves nothing at all essentially. Besides, “form,” in the true scope of language, signifies the outward symmetry or shape of a compound. But the Peripatetics, who impose meanings on language as they do on Nature, interpret it otherwise in their books and mistake the effect for the cause. I shall therefore take it in their sense and be content for once to agree with their comments. Form, then, in their conception, is the same as *vis formatrix* or “Formative Power,” which Aristotle defines as “the outward expression of the inward essence.” I must confess I do not understand him, and therefore I shall take him on trust as his disciples explain him.
> “It is the *Logos* (says Magirus) insofar as it completes, improves, and informs natural substance, so that one thing may thereby be distinguished from another.”
This is an expression of the duty and effect of forms, but says nothing at all about their substance or essence.

Now let us see what he says regarding the Soul of man. The Soul (he says) is, in plain terms, the consummation, or—to use a barbarous term—

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Aristotelian definitions, transitioning from the definition of "form" to the definition of the "Soul."</meta>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

<page-num>49</page-num>

but truly *Finitatio*, though his own followers falsely translate it as “the actuating principle of an organized body.” But this definition is common to beasts and plants, and therefore he has stumbled upon another: “the Soul is that principle in which we live, feel, move, and comprehend.” Now, both of these descriptions concern only the operations and faculties which the Soul exercises upon the body, but they do not discover her nature or origin at all. It was sincerely done by Galen, who confessed his ignorance concerning the substance of the Soul; but this fellow [Aristotle], who did not have as much honesty, is proclaimed the “prince of philosophers,” and the positions of more glorious authors are examined by his dictates, as if by a touchstone. Indeed, the Scripture itself is often twisted and forced by his disciples to vote a *placet* <note>an expression of assent or approval</note> to his conclusions. It is a miserable task to dwell on this pagan, to gather his straw and stubble for most of our days, and after all to be no better acquainted with ourselves, except to know that the Soul is the cause of life, sense, motion, and understanding. I pity our customary follies, that we bind ourselves over to an apprenticeship of expense and study, only to encompass a few superficial truths which every plowman knows without a book. Truly, Nature is such a tutor that no one can be ignorant of these things, for who is so stupid as not to know the difference between life and death—the absence and presence of his Soul? Yet these very definitions, though looked upon as rare, profound, philosophical determinations, instruct us in nothing more than this. Away, then, with this Peripatetic philosophy, this “vain babbling” as St. Paul justly styles it; for, surely enough, he had some experience of it at Athens during his dispute about the resurrection. Let us no longer look upon this *Olla Podrida* <note>a jumbled collection or mishmash</note>, but upon that spirit which resides in the elements; for this produces real effects by the subsequent processes of corruption and generation, but the spirit of error, which is Aristotle’s, produces nothing but a multiplicity of notions. Observe, then, that this Stagirite <note>Aristotle</note> and Nature are at a great distance from one another; the one ends in works, the other in words. His followers refine the old notions, but not the old creatures. And, truly, the mystery of their profession consists only in their terms; if their speculations were exposed to the world in a plain dress, their meaning is so empty and shallow that no one would acknowledge them as philosophers. In some discourses, I confess they have Nature before them, but they do not go the right way to understand her. They are always in pursuit but never overtake their game; for who among them has knowledge so complete and orderly that he can justify his positions by practice? Again, in some things they are quite

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Peripatetic (Aristotelian) philosophers, arguing that their definitions of the soul and nature are superficial and fail to grasp the internal, spiritual realities of the world.</meta>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

<page-num>50</page-num>

...off the mark; they scold and squabble about whims and problems of their own which are no more in Nature than Lucian’s *Lachanopters* or *Hyppogypians*. Now the reason for their errors is this: they are experienced in nothing but outward accidents or qualities, and all the performance they can achieve in philosophy is to pronounce a body hot or cold, moist or dry. But if they consider the essential temperament, they are grossly mistaken in stating these qualifications; for it is not the touch or sight that can discern intrinsic, true complexions. A body that is outwardly cold to the sense may be hotter in the innermost part, where the genuine temperament lies, than the Sun himself is outwardly. 

But they do not know the providence of Nature—how she interposes a different resisting quality in the circumference of everything, lest the qualities of surrounding bodies should conspire in too great a measure with the center, and so bring about a dissolution of the compound. Thus she interposes a passive, refreshing spirit between the Central Fire and the Sulphur; again, she places the Sulphur between the Liquor of the Celestial Luna and her outward Mercury—a rare and admirable texture, infallibly proving that none but God only wise, who foresaw the conveniences and inconveniences of his creatures, could arrange them in that daring order and connection. 

But to go further with these Peripatetics: their philosophy is a kind of physiognomy; they will judge invisible, inward principles (forms, as they call them), which are shut up in the closet of matter, and all this while only examining the outside or crust of nature. It would be a foolish presumption if a lapidary should undertake to state the value or luster of a jewel that is locked up before he opens the cabinet. I advise them, therefore, to use their hands, not their fancies, and to change their abstractions into extractions; for, truly, as long as they lick the shell in this fashion, and do not pierce experimentally into the center of things, they can do no differently than they have done. They cannot know things substantially, but only describe them by their outward effects and motions, which are subject and obvious to every common eye. 

Let them consider, therefore, that there is in Nature a certain spirit which applies himself to matter and acts in every generation; that there is also a passive, intrinsic principle where he is more immediately resident than the rest, and by the mediation of which he communicates with the more gross, material parts. For there is in Nature a certain chain, or subordinate proximity of complexions between visibles and invisibles, and this is the means by which the superior, spiritual essences descend and converse here below with matter. But take care lest you

<summary>The author argues that Peripatetic philosophers fail to understand nature because they focus on outward appearances rather than internal, spiritual principles. He advocates for an experimental, alchemical approach ("extractions") to discover the "Central Fire" and the hidden chains connecting the spiritual and material realms.</summary>

<page-num>51</page-num>

...misunderstand me. I do not speak in this place of the Divine Spirit, but I speak of a certain art by which a particular Spirit may be united to the universal, and nature as a consequence may be strangely exalted and multiplied.* Now then, you who have your eyes in your hearts, and not your hearts in your eyes, attend to what is spoken; and, so that I may exhort you to Magic in the Magician's phrase—"Hear with the understanding of the heart."

It is obvious to all those whom Nature has enriched with sense, and convenient organs to exercise it, that every body in the world is subject to a certain species of motion. Animals have their progressive outward, and their vital inward motions. The Heavens are carried with that species which the Peripatetics call <term>Lation</term>, where, by the way, I must tell you, it proceeds from an intrinsic principle, for "intelligences" are fabled. The air moves variously, the sea has its flux and reflux. Vegetables have their growth and augmentation, which necessarily imply a concoction; and, finally, the earth with her minerals and all other treasures, are subject to alteration—that is, to generation and corruption. Now, the matter itself being merely passive, and furnished with no motive faculty at all, we must of necessity conclude that there is some other inward principle which acts and regulates it in every several species of motion. But, truly, it is not enough to call this inward principle a "Form," and so bury up the riches of Nature in this narrow and most absurd formality. We should rather abstain from scribbling, or study to publish that which may contribute something to the author's credit, but much more to the benefit of the readers. To be plain then, this principle is the Soul of the World, or the Universal Spirit of Nature. This Soul is retained in the matter by certain other proportionate natures, and lacking a vent, does organize the shapeless mass. She labors as much as she can to resume her former liberty, frames for herself a habitation here in the center, puts her prison into some good order, and branches into the several members, so that she may have more room to act and employ her faculties. But you are to observe that in every frame there are three leading principles. The first is this Soul of which we have spoken something already. The second is that which is called the Spirit of the World, and this Spirit is the medium by which the Soul is infused and does move its body. The third is a certain oily ethereal water; this is the <term>Menstruum</term> and <term>Matrix</term> of the World, for in it all things are framed and preserved. The Soul is a compound of an ether of extreme thinness and of the most

uncompounded form of light.” Hence that admirable Platonic poet styled it “the fire of purest ether.” Virgil.

Neither should you wonder that I say it is a compound, for there is no perfect specific Nature that is simple and void of composition except only that of God Almighty. Trust not then to Aristotle, who tells you that the elements are simple bodies, for the contrary has been manifested by absolute, infallible experience. The passive spirit is a thin aerial substance, the only immediate garment wherein the Soul wraps herself when she descends and applies herself to generation. The radical vital liquor is a pure celestial nature, answering in proportion and complexion to the superior interstellar waters. Now, as soon as the passive spirit attracts the Soul, which is done when the first link in the chain moves (of which we shall speak in its proper place), then the ethereal water in a moment attracts the passive spirit, for this is the first visible receptacle wherein the superior natures are concentrated. The Soul being thus confined and imprisoned by lawful magic in this liquid crystal, the light which is in her streams through the water, and then it is “the Light made openly visible to the eye,” in which state it is first made subject to the artist. Here now lies the mystery of the magician’s <term>denarius</term>, his most secret and miraculous pyramid, whose first unity or cone is always in the “horizon of eternity,” but its base or quadrate is here below in the “horizon of the temporal.” The Soul consists of three portions of light and one of matter. The passive spirit has two parts of matter and two of light, which is why it is called the “natural medium” and the “sphere of equilibrium.” The celestial water has but one portion of light to three of matter. Now the chain of descent which concerns the spiritual is grounded on a similarity, or symbol of natures, according to that principle of Osthanes: ἡ φύσις τῇ φύσει τέρπεται—“One Nature delights in another.” For there being three portions of light in the Soul and two in the passive spirit, the inferior attracts the superior. Then there being but one portion in the celestial nature, and two in the middle spirit, this solitary shining Unity attracts the other <term>Binarius</term> to fortify and augment itself, as light joins with light or flame with flame, and thus they hang in a vital, magnetic series. Again, the chain of ascent which concerns matter is performed thus: The celestial nature differs not in substance from the aerial spirit but only in degree and complexion, and the aerial spirit differs from the aura, or material part of the Soul, in constitution only, and not in nature, so that these three being but one substantially may admit of a perfect hypostatic union, and be carried by a certain intellectual light “into

...the plane of the super-celestial world, and so swallowed up by immortality.* But one thinks Nature complains of a prostitution—that I go about to diminish her majesty, having almost broken her seal and exposed her naked to the world. I must confess I have gone very far, and now I must restrain myself, for there is a necessity for reserving as well as publishing some things; and yet I will speak of greater matters. The Soul, though in some sense active, is not essentially so, but is a mere instrumental agent, for she is guided in her operations by a Spiritual Metaphysical Grain, a Seed or Glance of Light, simple and without any mixture, descending from the first Father of Lights. For though His full-eyed love shines on nothing but man, yet everything in the world is in some measure directed for its preservation by a spice or touch of the first Intellect. This is partly confirmed by the Habitation and Residence of God, for He is seated above all His creatures, to hatch, as it were, and cherish them with living eternal influences which daily and hourly proceed from Him. Hence He is called by the <term>Kabbalists</term> <term>Kether</term>, and it answers to Parmenides' "Burning Crown," which he placed above all the visible spheres. This flux of immaterial powers, Christ Himself, in whom the fullness of the Godhead resided, confirmed and acknowledged in the flesh; for when the diseased woman touched His garment, though the crowd was great, He questioned who it was, adding this reason—"I perceive," said He, "that virtue has gone out of me." But, laying aside such proofs, though the Scripture abounds in them, let us consider the exercise and practice of Nature here below, and we shall find her game such that she cannot play it without this tutor. In the first place, then, I would gladly know: who taught the spider his mathematics? How does he come to lodge in the center of his web, so that he may sally forth upon all occasions to any part of the circumference? How does he come to premeditate and forecast? For if he did not first know and imagine that there were flies, upon which he must feed, he would not watch for them, nor spin out his nets in that exquisite form and texture. Truly, we must confess that He who ordained flies for his sustenance gave him also some small light to know and execute His ordinance. Tell me, if you can, who taught the hare to countermarch, when she doubles her tracks in the pursuit to confound the scent and puzzle her persecutors? Who counsels her to stride from the double to her form <note>A "form" is the name for a hare's resting place or nest.</note>, so that her steps may be at a greater distance, and, consequently, the more difficult to find out? Certainly, this is a well-ordered policy, enough to prove that God is not absent from His creatures, but that "Wisdom reaches mightily..."

<page-num>54</page-num>

"from one end to another," and that "his Incorruptible Spirit fills all things." But to speak about something more directly relevant to our purpose, let us consider the several products that are in Nature, with their admirable features and symmetry. We know very well there is but one matter out of which so many different shapes and constitutions are found.* Now, if the agent that determines and shapes the matter were not a discerning spirit, it would be impossible for him to produce anything at all. For let me suppose *Hyliard* <note>Nicholas Hilliard, the famous Elizabethan miniature painter.</note> with his pencil and tablet ready to portray a rose; if he does not inwardly understand the very shape and proportion of that which he intends to paint, he might as well do it without his eyes as without his intellect. Let us now apply this to the Spirit that works in Nature. This moves in the center of all things, has the matter before him, as the potter has his clay, or the painter his colors, and first of all he exercises his chemistry in several transformations, producing sinews, veins, blood, flesh, and bones, which work also includes his arithmetic; for he makes the joints and all integral parts—indeed, as Christ tells us, the very hairs of our heads in a certain determinate number—which may contribute to the beauty and motion of the frame. Again, in the outward lineaments or symmetry of the compound, he proves himself a most regular mathematician, proportioning parts to parts, all of which operations can proceed from nothing but a Divine, Intellectual Spirit. For if he did not have several ideas or conceptions corresponding to his several intentions, he could not distinguish the one from the other; and if he were not sensible, if he did not foresee the work he intends, then the end could be no impulsive cause, as the *Peripatetics* <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy.</note> have it.

The consideration of these several duties which this spirit performs in generation made *Aristotle* himself grant, that in the seeds of all things there were "potencies similar to designs." We should therefore examine: who weaves the flowers of plants? who colors them without a brush? who shoots the branches upward, and threads (as it were) their roots downward? For all these actions include a certain artifice which cannot be done without judgment and discretion. Now our Savior tells us: "My Father works until now;" and in another place, it is "God clothes the lily of the field;" and again, "not one sparrow falls without your Father." Verily, this is the truth, and the testimony of truth, notwithstanding *Aristotle* and his problems. Neither should you think the Divine Spirit disparaged by presiding over every generation because some products seem poor and contemptible; for, verily, as

...as long as they contribute to the glory of their Author, they are noble enough. If you reflect upon Egypt, you will find the lowliest of His creatures forced a universal confession from the sorcerers: *Digitus Dei est hic*—"the Finger of God is here." To come to the point, then: these invisible, central Artists are Lights seeded by the First Light in that primitive emanation, or *Sit Lux*—"Let there be light"—which some falsely translate as *Fiat Lux*—"Let light be made." For Nature is "the Voice of God," not a mere sound or command, but a substantial, active breath proceeding from the Creator and penetrating all things. God Himself is a <term>spermatic form</term>; and this is the only sense in which a form may be defined as "the outward expression of an inward essence."

I know this will seem harsh to some men, whose ignorant zeal has made them adversaries to God; for they rob Him of His glory and give it to His creature—or sometimes even to fancies and inventions of their own. I wish such philosophers to consider whether in the beginning there was any life or wisdom beyond the Creator, and, if so, to tell us where. Truly (to use their own term), they can never find this <term>Ubi</term> <note>Latin for "where" or "location".</note>. For these are gracious concessions or talents which God of His free will has lent us, and if He should take them back, we would immediately return to our original nothingness.

<margin>Isaiah.</margin>
Let them take heed, therefore, while they attribute generation to qualities, lest the true Author of it should come against them with that charge which He once brought against the Assyrians:
> "Shall the axe boast itself against him who hews with it? Or shall the saw magnify itself against him who shakes it? As if the rod should shake itself against those who lift it up, or as if the staff should lift itself up as if it were not wood!"

<margin>Is. xlv.</margin>
Let them instead dismiss their Aristotle and the errors with which he has infatuated so many generations. Let them approach with confidence the Almighty God who made the world, for no one can give a better account of the work than the Architect. Let them not despair of attaining familiarity with Him, for He is a God who desires to be known and will reveal Himself both for the manifestation of His own glory and the benefit of His creature. There is no reason, then, why we should reject this great and glorious Schoolmaster, whose very invitation offers more than ordinary encouragement:
> "Thus says the Lord, the Holy One of Israel, and our Maker: Ask Me of things to come concerning My sons, and concerning the work of My hands, you command Me. I have made the Earth and created man upon it; I, even My hands, have stretched out the Heavens, and all their host I have commanded."

<page-num>56</page-num>

How shall we approach the Lord, and by what means may we find Him out? Surely, not with words, but with works; not by studying ignorant, heathen authors, but by examining and testing His creatures. For in them lies His secret path, which though it be shut up with thorns and briars, with outward, worldly corruptions, yet if we would take the pains to remove this baggage, we might enter the Terrestrial Paradise—that *Hortus conclusus*, that Enclosed Garden of Solomon—where God descends to walk and drink of the Sealed Fountain. But, truly, there is such a general prejudice, such a customary opposition to all principles which cross Aristotle, that truth can no sooner step abroad but some sophist or another flings dirt in her face. It is strange that none of these schoolmen consider how the several distinctions and divisions translated from logic to divinity have set all Christendom on fire, how they have violated the peace of many flourishing kingdoms, and occasioned more sects in religion than there are opinions in philosophy. Most timely then and Christian is that petition <margin>In Genesis.</margin> of St. Augustine: "*Deliver us from logic, O Lord!*" And here I must desire the reader not to mistake me. I do not condemn the use but the abuse of reason—the many subtleties and tricks of it, which man has so applied that truth and error are equally disputable. I am one who stands up for a true natural knowledge, grounded, as Nature is, on Jesus Christ, who is the true foundation of all things visible and invisible. I shall therefore in this discourse touch merely upon those mysteries which some few have delivered over to posterity in difficult, obscure terms, so that, if possible, the majesty of truth and the benefit they shall receive from it may settle men in a new way, and bring them at last from vain, empty fancies to a real, sensible fruition of Nature.

You may remember how in my former discourse on the nature of man I mentioned a certain triplicity of elements according to their several complexions in the several regions of the world. I shall now speak of another triplicity much more obscure and mystical, without which you can never attain to the former; for these three principles are the KEY of all magic, without whose perfect knowledge you can never truly understand the best idioms in Nature. The first principle is One in One, and One from One. It is a pure white Virgin, and next to that which is most pure and simple. This is the first created unity. By this all things were made, not actually, but mediately, and without this nothing can be made, either artificial or natural. This is "*the Bride of God and of the Stars.*" *

Through the mediation of this, there is a descent from the One into the Four, and an ascent from the Three through the Four to the invisible, supernatural <term>Monad</term>. He who does not know this can never attain to the Art, for he does not know what he is looking for. The second principle does not differ from the first in substance and dignity, but in appearance and order. This second was the first, and remains so essentially, but by adhering to matter it contracted an impurity and so fell from its original unity; for this reason, the magicians call it the <term>Binarius</term>. Separate, therefore, the circumference from the center through the line of the diameter, and there will appear to you the philosopher’s <term>Ternarius</term>, which is the third principle. This third is not strictly a principle, but a product of art. It is of a diverse nature, compounded in one sense and decomposed in another, consisting of both inferior and superior powers.

This is the Magician’s Fire. This is the Mercury of the philosophers, that most celebrated <term>Microcosm</term> and Adam. This is the labyrinth and wilderness of magic, where a world of students have lost themselves; it is a thing handled so confusedly and obscurely by those who knew it that it is altogether impossible to find it in their records. There is no recent writer who understands the full extent and universality of this principle, nor its genuine metaphysical use. It moves here below in shadows and gauzes, and above in white, ethereal garments; nor is there anything in nature exposed to such public misuse as this is, for it passes through all hands, and there is no creature that does not make use of it. This <term>Ternarius</term>, being reduced by the <term>Quaternary</term>, ascends to the magical <term>Decad</term>, which is "the exceedingly single Monad," in which state "it can perform whatever things it pleases," for it is united thus, "face to face," to the First, Eternal, Spiritual Unity. But concerning these Three, hear the oracle of magic, the great and solemn Agrippa:

> "There are then, as we have said, four elements, without the perfect knowledge of which we can effect nothing in magic. Now, each of them is threefold, so that the number of four may make up the number of twelve; and by passing the number of seven into the number of ten, there may be a progress to the supreme Unity, upon which all virtue and wonderful operation depends. Of the first order are the pure elements, which are neither compounded nor changed, nor admit of mixture, but are incorruptible; and it is not *from* which but *through* which the virtues of all natural things are brought forth into action. No man is able to declare their virtues, because they can do all things upon all things. He who is ignorant of these shall never be able to bring to completion any wonderful matter.
>

Their virtue, when they are thus reduced to their simplicity, does above all things perfect all occult and common operations of Nature; these are the foundations of all natural magic. Of the third order are those elements which originally, and of themselves, are not elements, but are twice compounded, various, and changeable one into the other. They are the infallible medium, and therefore are called the middle nature, or Soul of the middle nature. Very few there are that understand the deep mysteries of this. In them, by means of certain numbers, degrees, and orders, is the perfection of every effect in whatever thing, whether natural, celestial, or super-celestial. They are full of wonders and mysteries and are operative in both natural and divine magic; for from these, and through them, proceed the bindings, loosenings, and transmutations of all things, the knowing and foretelling of things to come, as well as the driving forth of evil spirits and the gaining of good ones. Let no man, therefore, without these three sorts of elements and the knowledge of them, be confident that he is able to work anything in the occult sciences of magic and nature. But whoever shall know how to reduce those of one order into those of another—impure into pure, compounded into simple—and shall know how to understand distinctly their nature, virtue, and power in number, degrees, and order, without dividing the substance, he shall easily attain to the knowledge and perfect operation of all natural things and celestial secrets.

This is the man with the “*black spaniel*,” or rather, this is he “*who, even from his earliest age, did ever appear as an inquiring and intrepid investigator into the abounding operations of mysterious things and of miraculous effects.*” Now, for your further instruction, hear also the dark disciple of the even darker Libanius Gallius:

> “The first principle consists in that one substance through which, rather than from which, every potentiality of natural marvels is developed into the actual. We have said ‘through which,’ because the Absolute which proceeds out of unity is not compounded, nor does it have any change. To that, from the <term>Triad</term> and from the <term>Tetrad</term>, there is a mysterious progression to the <term>Monad</term> for the completion of the <term>Decad</term>, because by this there is the regression of number into unity, and, in like manner, the descent to the <term>Tetrad</term> and the ascent to the <term>Monad</term>. By this only can the <term>Duad</term> be completed. With joy and triumph, the <term>Monad</term> is converted into the <term>Triad</term>.
