Had they mastered all the books of the wise, were they perfectly familiar with the courses of the stars—with their virtues, powers, operations, and properties—and did they keenly and clearly understand their types, signets, sigils, and their most secret matters whatever, no performance of marvels could possibly follow these operations without the knowledge of this principle, which comes out of a principle and returns into a principle. From this, all people, without exception, whom I have found experimenting in natural magic have either attained nothing or, after long and unproductive operations, have been driven into vain, trifling, and superstitious pursuits.

Now, the second principle, which is separated from the first in order and not in dignity, and which alone existing creates the Triad, is that which works wonders by the Duad. For in the one is the one, and yet there is not the one; it is simple, yet in the Tetrad it is compounded. Being purified by fire, it comes forth as pure water, and when reduced to its simplicity, it shall reveal to the performer of arcane mysteries the completion of his labors. Here lies the center of all natural magic, whose circumference, united to itself, displays a circle—a vast line in the infinite. Its virtue is above all things purified, and it is less simple than all things, composed on the scale of the Tetrad. But the <term>Pythagoric</term> Tetrad supported by the Triad—the pure and purified in one—can, if order and rank be observed, most assuredly perform marvels and secrets of nature regarding the Duad within the Triad. This is the Tetrad within whose capacity the Triad, joined to the Duad, makes all things one, and which works wonderfully. The Triad reduced to unity contains all things, *per aspectum* <note>by sight or appearance</note>, in itself, and it does whatever it wills.

The third principle is by itself no principle; but between this and the Duad is the end of all science and mystic art, and the infallible center of the mediating principle. It is no easier to blunder in the one than in the other, for few flourish on earth who fundamentally comprehend its mysteries—both progressing by an eightfold multiplication through the septenary into the Triad, and remaining fixed. Therein is the consummation of the scale and series of Number. By this every philosopher and every true <term>Scrutator</term> <note>investigator or examiner</note> of natural secrets has attained admirable results. By this, reduced in the Triad to a simple element, they suddenly performed miraculous cures of diseases and of all kinds of sickness in a purely natural manner; and the operations of natural and supernatural magic attained results through the direction of the Tetrad. By this, the prediction of future events was truthfully performed, and in no other way was the narrow

entrance into secret things wrested from Nature. By this medium alone was the secret of Nature laid bare to Alchemists; without it, no comprehension of the art can be acquired, nor the end of experiment discovered. Believe me, they err, they all err, who, devoid of these three principles, dream it possible for them to accomplish anything in the secret services of Nature.” Thus far Trithemius, where for your better understanding of him, I must inform you there is a twofold <term>Binarius</term>, one of light and confusion; but peruse Agrippa seriously *Of the Scales of Numbers*, and you may apprehend all, for our abbot borrowed this language from him, whose books he read before he published anything of this nature of his own. Now, for your further instruction, go along with me, not to Athens or Stagira but to that secretary and penman of God Almighty who stood on a cleft of the rock when He made all His goodness pass before him. I am certain the world will wonder I should use Scripture to establish physiology, but I would have them know that all secrets, physical and spiritual, all the close connections, and that mysterious Kiss of God and Nature are clearly and punctually discovered there. Consider that merciful mystery of the Incarnation, wherein the fullness of the Godhead was incorporated, and the Divine Light united to the Matter in a far greater measure than at the first creation. Consider it, I say, and you shall find that no philosophy has perfectly united God to His creature but the Christian; therefore also it is the only true philosophy, and the only true religion, for without this union there can neither be a natural temporal life, nor a spiritual eternal life. Moses tells us that in the beginning God created the Heaven and the Earth, that is the Virgin Mercury and the Virgin Sulphur. Now, let me advise you not to trouble yourselves with this Mercury unless you have a true friend to instruct you, or an express illumination from the first Author of it, for it is a thing attained “by a marvelous Art.” Observe then what I shall now tell you. There is in every star, and in this elemental world, a certain principle which is “the Bride of the Sun.” These two in their coition emit seed, which seed is carried in the womb of Nature, but the ejection of it is performed invisibly, and in a sacred silence, for this is the conjugal mystery of Heaven and Earth, their act of generation—a thing done in private between males and females, but how much more, think you, between the two universal natures? Know therefore that it is impossible for you to extract or receive any seed from the sun without this feminine principle which is the Wife of the Sun. Now then, my small sophisters of the Stone, you

You who consume your time and resources in making waters and oils with a dirty *Caput Mortuum* <note>Literally "dead head"; the useless residue left after distillation</note>, you who deal in gold and quicksilver, being infatuated with the legends of some recent and former charlatans: consider the final end of such men. Did they obtain anything by it but diseases and poverty? Did they not in their old age, 

> "the greybeards of an evil time," 

fall to clipping and counterfeiting coins? And as a final note to their memory, did they not die in despair, which is the child of ignorance? Know then for certain that the magician’s Sun and Moon are two universal peers, male and female, or king and queen regents, always young and never old. These two are adequate to the whole world and coextend through the universe. The one is not without the other, God having united them in His work of creation in a solemn, sacramental union. It will then be a hard and difficult enterprise to rob the husband of his wife, to put those asunder whom God Himself has put together; for they both sleep in the same bed, and he who discovers the one must necessarily see the other. The love between these two is so great that if you use this Virgin kindly, she will fetch back her Cupid after he has ascended from her on wings of fire. 

Observe, moreover, that material principles can only be multiplied materially—that is, by the addition of parts, as you see in the growth of bodies, which is performed by a continual intake of nourishment into the stomach. But it is not the body that transmutes the nourishment into flesh and blood, but that spirit which is the light and life of the body. Material principles are passive and can neither alter nor purify, though they may well be altered and purified; nor can they communicate themselves to another substance beyond their own extension, which is finite and determinate. Do not question these impostors, then, who tell you of a <term>Sulphur Tingens</term> and I know not what other fables, and who also pin that narrow name of <term>Chemia</term> <note>Chemistry or Alchemy</note> on a science both ancient and infinite. It is the Light only that can be truly multiplied, for this ascends to and descends from the first fountain of multiplication and generation. This Light, applied to any body whatsoever, exalts and perfects it according to its own species. If applied to animals, it exalts animals; if to vegetables, vegetables; if to minerals, it refines minerals and transforms them from the worst to the best condition. Note here, by the way, that every body has passive principles within itself for this Light to work upon, and therefore does not need to borrow any from gold or silver. Consider then what it is you search for, you who hunt after the Philosophers' Stone, for 

> "it is His to transmute who creates; you seek for that which is most high, but you look at that which is most low."

...tian may and ought to look after—the true and the necessary. Truth is the Arcanum, the mystery and essence of all things, for every secret is truth, and every substantial truth is a secret. I speak not here of outward historical truths, which are but relatives to actions, but I speak of an inward essential truth which is Light; for Light is the truth, and it discovers falsehood, which is darkness. By this truth, all that which is necessary may be achieved, but never without it.

> "I preferred wisdom (said the wise king) before scepters and thrones, and esteemed riches as nothing in comparison to her. Neither did I compare any precious stone to her, because all gold, in respect to her, is like a little sand, and silver shall be counted as clay before her. I loved her above health and beauty, and chose to have her instead of light, for the light that comes from her never goes out. All good things came to me together with her, and innumerable riches in her hands. And I rejoiced in them all, because wisdom goes before them, and I did not know that she was the mother of them. If riches are a possession to be desired in this life, what is richer than wisdom that works all things? For she is privy to the mysteries of the knowledge of God, and a lover of His works. God has granted me to speak as I would, and to conceive as is fitting for the things that are given me, because it is He who leads unto wisdom and directs the wise; for in His hand are both we and our words, all wisdom also, and knowledge of workmanship. For He has given me certain knowledge of the things that are—namely, to know how the world was made, and the operation of the elements. The beginning, ending, and middle of the times; the alterations of the turning of the sun, and the change of seasons; the circuit of years and the position of stars; the natures of living creatures and the furies of wild beasts; the violence of winds and the reasoning of men; the diversities of plants and the virtues of roots; and all such things as are either secret or manifest—these I know. For wisdom, which is the worker of all things, taught me. For in her is an understanding spirit: holy, only begotten, manifold, subtle, lively, clear, undefiled, plain, not subject to harm, loving the thing that is good, quick, which cannot be hindered, ready to do good, kind to man, steadfast, free from care, having all power, overseeing all things, and going through all understanding, pure, and most subtle spirits. For wisdom is more moving than any motion; she passes and goes through all things by reason of her pureness. For she is the brightness of the everlasting light, the unspotted mirror of the power of God, and the image of His goodness. And being but one, she can do all things,"

<page-num>63</page-num>

...and remaining in herself she makes all things new, and in all ages entering into holy souls, she makes them friends of God and prophets. For God loves no one except him who dwells with wisdom.

> For she is more beautiful than the sun, and above all the order of stars; being compared with the light, she is far before it, for after this comes night, but vice shall not prevail against wisdom.

Thus says Solomon, and again one greater than Solomon:

> First seek the Kingdom of God, and all these things shall be given to you.

For, in truth, temporal blessings are but ushers to the spiritual, or to speak more plainly, once we begin to love the Spirit, then he sends us these things as tokens and pledges of his love; for promotion comes neither from the East nor from the West, but from God who gives it.

> He truly is (says one) from whom nothing is absent, whom nothing supports, and whom nothing—much less—can harm: that all-necessary thing, having which it is impossible to be destitute.

Truth, therefore, is the highest excellence and an impregnable fortress, having few friends abiding within it and assailed by innumerable enemies; invisible in these days to nearly all the world, but an invincible security to those who possess it. In this Citadel is contained the true and undoubted <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> and the Treasure, which—uneaten by moths and unstolen by thieves—remains for eternity, though all other things dissolve, set up for the ruin of many and the salvation of some. This is the matter which to the crowd is vile, exceedingly contemptible and odious, yet not odious but love-worthy and precious to Philosophers above gems and gold; it is itself the lover of all, yet to nearly everyone an enemy; to be found everywhere, yet by few, scarcely by any, discovered; crying along the streets to all, "Come to me all you who seek, and I will lead you into the true path." This is that one thing proclaimed by the veritable Philosophers, which overcomes all, and is itself overcome by nothing, searching heart and body, penetrating everything stony and solid, and strengthening all things tender, and establishing its own power on the opposition of that which is most hard. It sets itself before us all, crying and proclaiming with an uplifted voice:

> I am the way of truth, walk by me, for there is no other path to life;

and yet we will not listen to her. She gives off the odor of sweetness, but we do not perceive it. Daily she liberally offers herself in sweetness to us in the holy festivals, and we will not taste her. Softly she draws us to salvation, and resisting her pressure, we refuse to endure it. For we have become like stones, having eyes and seeing not, having ears and hearing not, having nostrils and smelling not, furnished

O most miserable race of men, who are not superior to stones—indeed, so much the more inferior because knowledge of their actions is given to them and not to the stones. Be transformed (she cries), be transformed from dead stones into living <term>philosophical stones</term>. I am the true Medicine, rectifying and transforming that which is no more into what it was before corruption, and into something far better; and that which is not into what it ought to be. Look, I am at the door of your conscience, knocking night and day, and you will not open to me; yet I stand mildly waiting. I do not depart in anger; I suffer your insults patiently, hoping by that patience to lead you to that which I urge upon you. Come again and again, come often, you who seek wisdom, and buy for free—not with gold nor with silver, still less with your own labors—what is voluntarily offered to you. O sonorous voice, O voice sweet and gracious to the ears of the sages, O fountain of inexhaustible riches to those thirsting after truth and justice! O solace to the needs of those who are desolate! Why do you seek further, anxious mortals? Why torment your minds with innumerable anxieties, you miserable people? I pray you, what madness blinds you? When all that you seek outside yourself, instead of within, is in you and not outside you. This characteristic is commonly the vice of the common people: that, despising what is their own, they always desire what is foreign to them—and not altogether unreasonably, for of ourselves we possess nothing that is good. For if it is possible for us to have any goodness within us, we receive it from Him who alone is eternal Goodness; while, on the contrary, our disobedience has appropriated the Evil of our nature from an evil principle outside us. Man, then, has no possession of his own beyond the evil which he individualizes within himself. Whatever is good within him he receives from the Lord of goodness, not from himself; nevertheless, he has the faculty of incorporating what he receives from the good principle. That Life which is the light of men shines (albeit dimly) within us—that Life which is not of us, but from Him who possesses it from Everlasting. He has implanted it in us so that in His Light—He who dwells in light inaccessible—we may behold the Light. By this we surpass the rest of His perishable creatures; thus are we fashioned in His own likeness, because He has given us one ray of His own inherent illumination. Truth must not, therefore, be sought in our lower selves, but in the likeness of God which is within us."

most of their instructions addressed to a certain German postulant. But so that you may better understand how to obtain this Stone, hear what he says in another place.

> “True knowledge begins when, after the comparison of the imperishable and the perishable, of life and annihilation, the Soul—forcibly attracted by the delights of the supersensual—does elect to be made one with its divine spirit. The intellectual principle does issue from this knowledge and chooses the voluntary separation of the body, beholding with the Soul, on the one hand, the foulness and corruption of the body, and on the other, the everlasting splendor and felicity of the Spirit; with this (the Divine Pneuma so ordering) it yearns to be connected, completely discarding the body, so that it may seek only what it knows is encompassed by God himself with salvation and glory. Nevertheless, he is constrained to permit the body itself to participate in the union of Soul and Spirit; and this is that marvelous philosophical transmutation of body into spirit, and of spirit into body, about which this aphorism has come down to us from the wise of old: ‘Fix that which is volatile, and volatilize that which is fixed, and you shall attain to our <term>Grand Magisterium</term>.’ That is to say, ‘make the unyielding body pliant, which, by the supreme virtue of the Spirit acting in concert with the Soul, will endow the physical organism itself with an invariable constancy and enable it to resist every test.’ For gold is proven with fire, by which process all that is not gold is cast aside. O preeminent Gold of the philosophers with which the Sons of the Wise are enriched, not with that which is coined. Come here, all you who seek with such multitudinous exertions for the Philosophical Treasure; behold that Stone which you have rejected, and learn first what it is before you set out in search of it. It is more astonishing than any miracle that a man should seek what he does not know. It is undeniably foolish that an object should be sought by men about the truth of which the investigators know nothing, for such a search is hopeless. Let whoever seeks with so great toil first ascertain the existence and nature of the object they are in search of, and thus they will not be frustrated in their attempts. The wise man seeks what he loves and cannot love what he does not know; otherwise, he would be a fool. Out of knowledge, therefore, springs Love, the truth which is in all things, and which alone flourishes in all true philosophers.”

Thus he said, and again:

> “You but labor in vain, all you exposers of the hidden secrets of Nature, when—having entered upon the wrong path—you endeavor to discover by material means the informing virtues of material things. Learn therefore to know

Learn to discern the potentialities of material things through the heavenly—by Heaven, and not by the earth. No one can ascend into the Heaven that is your ambition unless he who descended from a Heaven you do not seek first grants him light. You seek an incorruptible medicine that will not only transform the body from corruption into a perfect organism but will also preserve that perfected body for an indefinite period; however, you will never discover such a medicine anywhere except in Heaven itself. The celestial virtue penetrates all the elements along invisible lines which, starting from all points, meet at the earth’s center; it generates and fosters the elemental worlds. No one can be brought forth there except in its likeness, which is also drawn from it. In the same way, the combined fetus of either parent retains its special nature within itself, so that both parents may be potentially and actually discovered within it. What shall cling more closely than the <term>Stone</term> in <term>philosophical generation</term>? Learn from within yourself to know whatever is in Heaven and on Earth, so that you may become wise in all things. You do not see that Heaven and the elements were once only one substance, and were separated from each other by divine skill to accomplish the generation of yourself and all that exists. If you knew this, the rest could not escape you; otherwise, you are devoid of all capacity. Again, in every generation such a separation is necessary, just as I have already told you that you must make it before embarking on the study of the true philosophy. You will never extract the one thing you desire out of the many things around you until that one thing I have proclaimed to you is extracted from yourself. For such is the will of God: that the devout should perform the devout work they desire, and the perfect accomplish another toward which they have been directed. To men of bad will, there shall be no harvest other than what they have sown; furthermore, because of their malice, their good seed shall very often be changed into weeds <note>The original text uses "cockle," a common weed found in grain fields.</note>. Perform, then, the work you seek in such a way that, as far as it is in your power, you may escape a similar misfortune.”

This is now the true, essential mystery of regeneration, or spiritual death. This is, and ever was, the only scope and result of <term>Magick</term>. But, for your further instruction, consider this other mystical speech of his.

> “Wake up now, therefore, my soul and body also; rise now, and follow the flight of your spirit. > “Do you not see that splendid and impregnable tower? Within it is the Philosophical love from whose fountain flows living water, and he who drinks of it once shall never thirst for vanity again. From that very pleasant and delightful place, there is a clear path to one more delightful still, where the Divine Sophia dwells; from her fountain leap waters far more blessed than the first, and if they are given to an enemy, he is immediately forced to grant them peace. Most of those who go there direct their course still higher, but not all can accomplish their desire. There is another place that mortals can scarcely reach unless they are received by the Divine Numen into the plane of immortality; and before they are introduced, they are forced to cast off the world, being weighed down by the garments of perishable life. In those who reach it, there is no longer any fear of death; instead, they welcome it more favorably from day to day, because they judge that everything in nature is worthy of their embrace. Whoever progresses beyond these three places vanishes from the sight of men. If it is granted to us to behold the second and third places, let us ascend higher. Thus, beyond the first crystalline arch, you see a second of silver, beyond which is a third of adamant; however, the fourth does not come within the senses until the third has been passed under. This is the golden region of undying happiness, free of care and filled entirely with perpetual joy.”

This is the height and the place to which, if any man ascends, he enters into chariots of fire with horses of fire and is translated from the earth, both soul and body. Such were Enoch, Elijah, and Esdras, to whom this medicine was administered by the angel Uriel. Such was Saint Paul, who was carried up to the third Heaven; such was Zoroaster, who was transfigured; and such was that anonymous individual mentioned by Agrippa.

> “The same wise man (he says) also appeared in such a way that burning rays came from him with a great sound.”

I suppose this was R. C., the founder of a most Christian and famous society, whose body is also preserved whole to this day by virtue of the medicine he took during his lifetime, surrounded by the epitomes of two worlds. Such "Elijahs" are also members of this Fraternity, who, as their own writings testify, walk in the supernatural light.

> “To join our assembly, it is necessary that you should perceive this Light, for without it, it is impossible to see us, except when we ourselves may will it.”

Go on still, and proceed in your own corrupt fancies, so that the occasion of justice may be upheld. In truth, God himself discovered this thing to the first man, to confirm his hopes of those three supernatural mysteries: the Incarnation, Regeneration, and Resurrection. For Iamblichus, citing the Egyptian records with “It is to be believed on the authority of secret wisdom,” has these very words: “that a certain substance has been handed down from the gods by means of sacred shows, and that consequently it was known to those same men who transmitted it.” And our former Christian author in a certain place speaks thus: “It is indisputable that Deity revealed to the patriarchs, by the Holy Spirit, a certain medicine by which they healed the corruption of the flesh, and when he spoke to them, entered into a most binding compact with them.” Let me tell you then that the limit and perfection of magic is in no way physical, for this art

->“Touches the seat of Jove, and essays divine things.”<-

In a word, it ascends “through the light of Nature to the light of grace,” and the final end of it is truly theological. Remember, therefore, that Elijah laid aside his mantle and passed through the waters of Jordan before he met with the chariots of Israel. But as Agrippa says: “The storehouse of truth is closed.” The Scripture is obscure and mystical even in historical passages. Who would believe that in the history of Hagar and Sarah, the mystery of both Testaments was hidden, unless St. Paul himself had told us so?

by a bondwoman, the other by a free woman. But he who was of the bondwoman was born according to the flesh, but he of the free woman by promise. These things are an allegory; for these are the two covenants: the one from Mount Sinai, which leads to bondage, which is Hagar; for this Hagar is Mount Sinai in Arabia, and corresponds to the Jerusalem that now is, and is in bondage with her children; but the Jerusalem from above is free, which is the mother of us all.” I could cite many more such examples, such as that of the royal prophet, “that the dew of Hermon descends to Mount Zion,” which is altogether impossible in the literal sense, for every geographer knows there is a vast distance between these two. But to return to my former discourse: some philosophers, who by the special mercy of God attained to the Ternarius, could never nonetheless obtain the perfect medicine, nor did they understand it. I never met in all my reading but with six authors who fully understood this mystery—the first an Arabian, a most profound but exceedingly obscure writer, and from him, I believe, Artesius borrowed all his knowledge—The second a most ancient Christian Anonymous, the greatest that ever was in terms of practice, for he ascended to that glorious metaphysical height where the Archetype shadows the Intellectual Spheres. The other four are famously known in Christendom. To instruct you then, this mystery is perfected when the Light, in a sudden, miraculous flash, strikes from the Center to the Circumference, and the Divine Spirit has so swallowed up the body that it is “glorified like the Sun and Moon in their splendor.” In this rotation it does pass (and no sooner) from the natural to a supernatural state, for it is no more fed with visible things but with invisible things, and the eye of the Creator is perpetually upon it. After this the material parts are never more to be seen, and this is that sinless and oft-celebrated invisibility of the Magi.” Truly, this is the way that the prophets and apostles went; this is the true primitive divinity, not that clamorous sophistry of the schools. I know the world will be ready to mock me for this because my years are few and inexperienced. I lack their two crutches: the pretended modern sanctity and that solemnity of the beard which makes a man a doctor. But, reader, let me advise you: if by what is here written you attain to any knowledge in this point (which I hold impossible without divine assistance), let me advise you, I say, not to attempt anything rashly; for Agrippa tells me: “Whoever approaches unpurified calls down judgment on himself and is abandoned to the devouring of the evil spirit.” There is in the Magical Records

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

...a memorable story of a Jew who, having by permission rifled some spiritual treasures, was translated into the wilderness and is kept there as an example to others. I will give you the best counsel that can be given, and that from a poet:

->“Ask for a sober mind in a healthy frame.”<-

You must prepare yourself until you are conformable to Him whom you would entertain, and that “in every respect.” You have Three that are to receive, and there are three accordingly that give. Fit your roof to your God as best you can, and in what you cannot, he will help you. When you have thus set your house in order, do not think your guest will come without invitation. You must tire him out with pious importunities:

> ->Perpetual knockings at his door,<-
> ->Tears sullying his transparent rooms,<-
> ->Sighs upon sighs; weep more and more,<-
> ->He comes.<-

This is the way you must walk in, and if you do, you shall perceive a sudden illumination; and then there shall abide in you fire with light, wind with fire, power with wind, knowledge with power, and the integrity of a healthy mind with knowledge. This is the chain that qualifies a Magician, for Agrippa says, “to inquire into things future, and into things present, and into other arcane matters, and into the things which are indicated to men by divine providence and true maxims, and to perform works which exceed the common course of Nature, is not possible apart from a profound and perfect doctrine, an immaculate life, and faith; and it is not to be performed by light-minded and unlearned men.” And in another place: “No man can give that which he himself does not have. But no man has it except he who, having suspended the elementary forces, having overcome Nature, having compelled Heaven, and having attained the angelical, exceeds his own archetype; co-operating then with his works, he can accomplish all things.” This is the place where if you can but once ascend, and then descend,

> ->“Then often the Archetype of the World attain,<-
> ->And often to him recur, and, face to face,<-
> ->Unhindered gaze upon the Father’s grace”—<-

Then, I say, you have obtained that Spirit whom all portentous mathematicians, wonder-working magicians, envious alchemical torturers of nature, and venomous necromancers more evil than demons dare to promise—that Spirit who discerns and performs,

<meta>This page continues the author's discourse on spiritual transformation, transitioning from the preparation of the self to the identification of the true "Philosopher's Stone" as a biblical, spiritual reality and a celestial medicine.</meta>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

...and that without any crime, without offense to God, and with no injury to religion. Such is the power he shall receive, who from the clamorous tumults of this world ascends to the supernatural still voice—from this base earth and mud to which his body is allied, to the spiritual, invisible elements of his soul.* “He shall receive the life of the gods; he shall behold the heroes intermingled with the deities, and shall himself be beheld by them.” This, reader, is the Christian Philosopher’s Stone, a stone so often emphasized in Scripture. This is the Rock in the wilderness, because it is in great obscurity, and there are few who know the right way to it. This is the Stone of Fire in Ezekiel; this is the Stone with Seven Eyes upon it in Zechariah, and this is the White Stone with the New Name in Revelation. But in the Gospel, where Christ himself speaks—who was born to reveal mysteries and communicate Heaven to Earth—it is more clearly described. This is the salt which you ought to have in yourselves; this is the water and Spirit of which you must be born again; and it is that seed which falls to the ground and multiplies a hundredfold. But, reader, do not be deceived by me. I am not a man of any such faculties, nor do I expect this blessing in such a great measure in this life; God is no debtor of mine. I can affirm no more of myself than what my author previously did: “Hold me, I bid you, as a signpost which, always pointing forward, shows the way to those undertaking a journey.” Behold! I will deal fairly with you; show me but one good Christian who is capable of and fit to receive such a secret, and I will show him the right, infallible way to obtain it. Yet this I must tell you: it would sink you to the ground to hear this mystery related, for it cannot enter the heart of the natural man how near God is to him, and how he is to be found. But enough of this. I will now speak of a natural, *celestial medicine*; the latter is common among some wise men, but few are they who attain the former. The common chemist works with common fire and without any medium; therefore he generates nothing, for he does not work as God does—toward preservation—but toward destruction. This is why he always ends in ashes. Use it *cum phlegmati medii*, so your materials shall rest in a third element where the violence of this tyrant cannot reach, except for its *Anima*. There is also a better way, for if you can temper it with the Spirit of Heaven, you have altered it from a corrupting fire to a generating fire. Sublime the middle-nature fire “by the Triad and the Circle,” until you come to a breach between inferiors and superiors. Lastly,

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the alchemical and spiritual process, transitioning from the identification of the Stone to the practical and moral requirements for the practitioner to extract the "Middle Earth" and live a life of faith and charity.</meta>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

...separate from the magically compounded earth that principle which is called "the Middle Earth," because it is middlemost between the <term>Unarius</term> and the <term>Binarius</term>, for as it does not attain the simplicity of the first, so it is free from the impurities of the second. This is "the True Crystalline Stone, a bright virgin earth without spot or darkness." This is "the Magian earth within the luminous ether," for it carries in its belly wind and fire. Having obtained this fundamental of a little new world, unite the heaven in a triple proportion to the earth; then apply a generative heat to both, and they will attract from above the Star-Fire of Nature. "So shall you possess the glory of all the world, and all darkness shall flee away from you." Now because the law of Nature is infallible, and confined to the creature by God’s royal assent, do not think therefore that there is any necessity upon God; but what he has enacted in general, he can repeal in any particular. Remember who moved the dew from the earth to the fleece, and from the fleece to the earth. God bestows not his blessings where they are to turn to curses. He cursed the Earth once for Adam’s sake; take heed he does not curse it again in your work for your sake. It is in vain to look for a blessing from Nature without the God of Nature, for, as the Scripture says, "without controversy the lesser is blessed by the greater." He must be a good steward who shall oversee the treasuries of God. Have, therefore, a charitable, seraphic soul: charitable at home by being not destructive to yourself, as most men are; charitable abroad, in a diffusive goodness to the poor, as many are not. There is in every true Christian a hint—I cannot say a grain—of faith, for then we could work miracles. But know that as God is the Father, so charity is the nurse of faith. For there springs from charitable works a hope of Heaven, and who is he that will not gladly believe what he hopes to receive? On the contrary, there springs no hope at all from the works of darkness, and, consequently, no faith, but that faith of devils, to "believe and tremble." Do not settle then in the dregs and puddle of the world; have your heart in Heaven and your hands on earth. Ascend in piety and descend in charity, for this is the Nature of Light and the Way of the Children of Light. Above all things, avoid the guilt of innocent blood, for it utterly separates one from God in this life and requires a timely and serious repentance if you would find him in the next. Now for your study: in the winter time, your chamber is your best residence. Here you may use fumigations and spicy lamps, not for superstition, but because such things refresh the <term>animal spirits</term> and the brain. In the summer, move yourself to the fields, where all are green with the Breath of

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual and moral state of the practitioner, moving from the necessity of charity and stewardship to the importance of contemplating God through the "secret analogy" found in nature.</meta>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

God, and fresh with the powers of heaven. Learn to refer all natural things to their spiritual counterparts "by help of the secret analogy," for this is the way the magicians went and found miracles. There are many who bestow their thoughts on God only until the world fails them. He may say to such guests, "When it can be forced on no one else, it is brought to me." Think on him first, and he will speak to your thoughts at last. Sometimes you may walk in groves, which, being full of majesty, will much advance the soul; sometimes by clear, active rivers, for by such (say the mystic poets) Apollo contemplated:—

> “All things that Phoebus spoke in his musing,
> The blessed Eurotas heard.”

So have I spent many a quiet hour on the banks of the Usk <note>Ysca</note>.

> “It is day, my crystal Usk: now the sad night
> Resigns her place as tenant to the light.
> See the amazed mists begin to fly,
> And the victorious sun has won the sky.
> How shall I reward your streams, that keep
> Me and my soul awake when others sleep?
> I watch my stars, I move on with the skies,
> And weary all the planets with my eyes.
> Shall I seek your forgotten birth, and see
> What days have passed since your nativity?
> Did you run with ancient Kishon? Can you tell
> As many years as holy Hiddekel?
> You are not paid in this. I’ll levy more
> Such harmless contributions from your store,
> And dress my soul by you as you do pass,
> As I would do my body by your glass <note>mirror</note>.
> What a clear, running crystal here I find!
> Sure I will strive to gain as clear a mind,
> And have my spirits, freed from dross, made light,
> That no base puddle may hinder their flight.
> How I admire your humble banks! Nothing is here
> But the same simple garment all the year.
> I’ll learn simplicity from you; when
> I walk the streets I will not storm at men,
> Nor look as if I had a mind to cry—
> ‘It is my valiant cloth of gold, and I.’
> Let me not live if I am not amazed to see
> What a clear type you are of piety.
> Why should your floods enrich those shores that sin
> Against your liberty, and keep you in?
> Your waters nurse that rude land which enslaves
> And captivates your free and spacious waves.

<summary>The author encourages the reader to contemplate God through nature and the "secret analogy" between physical and spiritual things. He includes an original poem dedicated to the River Usk, using its clarity and humility as a metaphor for the desired state of the human soul.</summary>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's poetic reflection on the river Usk and concludes the treatise with practical advice for the aspiring Christian philosopher, emphasizing spiritual focus, the selection of reliable authors, and the virtue of perseverance.</meta>

# ANIMA MAGICA ABSCONDITA.

> "Most blessed Tutors! I will learn from those
> To show my charity to my foes,
> And strive to do some good to the poor,
> As your streams do to the barren shore.
> All this from you, my Usk <note>Ysca</note>? Yes, and more:
> I am for many virtues on your score.
> Trust me with your waters still: why, will you not do so?
> Let me but drink again and I will go.
> I see your course anticipates my plea,
> I'll hasten to God, as you do to the sea.
> And when my eyes in waters drown their beams,
> The pious imitation of your streams,
> May every holy, happy, hearty tear
> Help me to run to Heaven, as you do there."

This is the way I would have you walk in, if you intend to be a solid, Christian philosopher. You must, as Agrippa says, "live only to God and the angels," and reject all things "which are in opposition to Heaven," otherwise you can have no communion with superiors. Lastly, "be single, not solitary." Avoid the multitude, as well of passions as of persons. Now for authors, I wish you to trust no moderns except Michael Sendivogius <note>Sendivov</note> and that other author of *Physia Restituta*, especially his first aphoristic part. The rest whom I have seen suggest inventions of their own, such as may pass for the whimsies of Descartes <note>des Chartes</note> or Borillus's "Mathematical Roses." To conclude, I would have you know that every day is a year contracted, and that every year is an extended day. Anticipate the year in the day, and lose not a day in the year. Make use of indeterminate agents until you can find a determinate one. The many may wish well, but only one loves. Circumferences spread but centers contract; so superiors dissolve and inferiors coagulate. Stand not long in the sun, nor long in the shade. Where extremes meet, there look for complexions. Learn from your errors to be infallible, and from your misfortunes to be constant. There is nothing stronger than perseverance, for it ends in miracles. I could tell you more, but that would only puzzle you. Learn this first, and you may teach me the last.

Thus, reader, I have published that knowledge which God gave me "for the fruit of a good conscience." I have not hidden my light under a bushel <note>busheld my light</note> nor buried my talent in the ground. I will now withdraw and leave the stage to the next actor—some Peripatetic <note>Aristotelian philosopher</note> perhaps, whose *sic probo* <note>"thus I prove"</note> shall serve me for a comedy. I have seen scolds laughed at but never admired; likewise, he who multiplies discourse makes a serious cause ridiculous. The only antidote to a shrew is silence, and the best way to convince fools is to neglect them.

<meta>This page begins the dedication of "Magia Adamica" to Thomas Henshaw, moving from the general title page into a philosophical address regarding the true purpose of life and the vanity of worldly pursuits.</meta>

# TO THE MOST EXCELLENTLY ACCOMPLISHED,

## MY BEST OF FRIENDS,

### MR. THOMAS HENSHAW.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'S' in a square frame, decorated with intricate scrolling foliage and floral motifs in a symmetrical design.</image-desc>SIR, It was the question of Solomon, and it demonstrated the supremacy of his wisdom,
<margin>Ecclesiastes 2:3</margin>"What was best for man to do all the days of his vanity under the sun?" If I wish myself wise enough to know this great affair of life, it is because you are fit to manage it. I will not advise you toward pleasures, to building houses, or planting vineyards, to enlarging your private possessions, or to multiplying your gold and silver. These are old errors—like vitriol for the stone—so many false prescriptions
<margin>Ecclesiastes 2:11</margin>which Solomon has tried before you, "And behold, all was vanity and vexation of spirit." I have sometimes seen actions as varied as they were great, and my own sullen fate has forced me into several courses of life; but I find not one hitherto which does not end in excess or satiety. Let us imagine a man as fortunate as this world can make him; what does he do but move from bed to board, and provide for the circumstances of those two scenes? Today he eats and drinks, then sleeps, so that he may do the same tomorrow. A great happiness! To live by cloying repetitions, and those that have more of necessity than of a free pleasure. This is *idem per idem*, and what is held to be an absurdity in reason cannot, by that same reason, be the true perfection of life. I do not deny that temporal blessings contribute to a temporal life, and by consequence are pleasing to the body; but if we consider the Soul, she is all this while on the wing—like that dove sent out of the Ark—seeking a place to rest. She is busied in a restless inquiry, and though her thoughts—for want of true knowledge—do not differ from desires, yet they sufficiently prove she has not found her satisfaction. Show me then but a practice wherein my Soul shall rest without any further investigation—for it is the lack of this which Solomon calls vexation of spirit—and you show me "what is best for man to do under the sun." Surely,

<summary>Eugenius Philalethes dedicates his work to Thomas Henshaw, reflecting on Solomon's biblical realization that worldly wealth and repetitive physical pleasures are merely vanity. He argues that the human soul remains restless and unsatisfied by temporal things, constantly seeking a deeper, more permanent state of rest.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the dedication of "Magia Adamica" to Thomas Henshaw, with the author, E.P. (Eugenius Philalethes), presenting his work as a humble offering of past studies to his friend and mentor.</meta>

# TO MR THOMAS HENSHAW.

Sir, this is not the Philosophers' Stone <note>A legendary substance sought by alchemists, capable of turning base metals into gold or granting eternal life</note>, nor will I undertake to define it, but give me leave to speak to you in the language of Zoroaster <note>An ancient Persian prophet and philosopher</note>:

> "Seek the channel of the Soul."

I have more confidence in your opinion of me than to tell you I love you, and for my present boldness you must thank yourself; you taught me this familiarity. I trouble you here with a short discourse, the brokage <note>fragments or scraps; literally, the dealings of a middleman</note> and weak remembrances of my former and more complete studies. It is not a laboured piece, and indeed not a fitting present, but I beg your acceptance of it as a *caveat* <note>a warning or caution</note>, so that you may see what unprofitable affections you have purchased. I do not propose it for your instruction; Nature has already admitted you to her school, and I would make you my judge, not my pupil. If, therefore, among your serious and more precious retirements <note>private periods of study or contemplation</note>, you can allow this trifle just a few minutes, and think them not lost, you will fulfill my ambition. You will place me, sir, at my full height, and though it were like that of Statius <note>a Roman poet known for his epic and occasional poetry</note>, among gods and stars, I shall quickly find the earth again, and at the first opportunity present myself,

->Sir,<-

->Your obedient Servant,<-

->E. P.<-

<summary>In this concluding part of the dedication, the author humbles himself before Thomas Henshaw, offering a short discourse based on his earlier studies. He asks Henshaw to act as a judge of his work rather than a student, acknowledging Henshaw's own advanced understanding of nature.</summary>

# TO THE READER.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'W' featuring intricate scrollwork and foliage designs.</image-desc>

Well fares the Dodechedron! <note>The *Dodechedron of Fortune*, a book on divination using dice.</note> I have examined the horoscope of this book by a cast of dice, and *Deuce-Ace* <note>The lowest throw in dice, a two and a one; often signifying bad luck.</note> tells me this parable. Truth (said the witty German) was commanded into exile, and the Lady Lie was seated on her throne. To perform the intent of this sentence, Truth went from among men, but she went all alone, poor and naked. She had not traveled very far when, standing on a high mountain, she perceived a great train passing by. In the middle of it was a chariot attended by kings, princes, and governors, and in that a stately Dame, who, like some queen-regent, commanded the rest of the company. Poor Truth, she stood still while this pompous squadron passed by, but when the chariot came opposite her, the Lady Lie, who was seated there, took notice of her, and causing her pageants to stop, commanded her to come nearer. Here she was scornfully examined: where did she come from? where would she go? and about what? To these questions she answered, as the custom of Truth is, very simply and plainly. Whereupon the Lady Lie commands her to wait upon her, and that in the rear and tail of all her troop, for that was the known place of Truth. Thanks then, not to the stars but to the configurations of the dice! They have acquainted me with my future fortunes, and what preferment my book is likely to attain. I am, for my part, contented, though the consideration of this dirty rear is very nauseous, and able to spoil a stronger stomach than mine. It has been said of old: *Non est Planta Veritatis super terram.* <note>There is no plant of Truth upon the earth.</note> Truth is an herb that grows not here below, and can I expect that these few seeds which I scatter thus in the storm and tempest should thrive to their full ears and harvest? But, reader, let it not trouble you to see the truth come thus behind; it may be there is more of a chase in it than of attendance, and her conditions not altogether so bad as her station. If you are one of those who draw up to the

He expresses a humble resignation to this fate, noting that truth rarely thrives in the world's current climate.</summary>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the "To the Reader" preface, specifically completing the allegory of Truth trailing behind the chariot of the "Lady Lie" and transitioning into a philosophical argument about the innate human desire for knowledge.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

chariot, pause here a little in the rear, and before you address yourself to *Aristotle* and his *Lady Lie*, think not your courtship lost if you kiss the lips of poor Truth. It is not my intention to jest with you in what I shall write; therefore, read with good faith what I will tell you with a good conscience. God, when He first made man, planted in him a spirit of that capacity that he might know all, adding to it a most fervent desire to know, lest that capacity should be useless. This truth is evident to the posterity of man, for little children, before they can ever speak, will stare upon anything that is strange to them; they will cry and are restless until they get it into their hands, so that they may feel it and look upon it—that is to say, so that they may know what it is in some degree, and according to their capacity. 

Now, some ignorant nurse will think that they do all this out of a desire to play with what they see, but they themselves tell us the contrary; for when they are past infancy and begin to make use of language, if any new thing appears, they will not desire to play with it, but they will ask you what it is, for they desire to know. And this is plain from their actions; for if you put any rattle into their hands, they will view it and study it for some short time, and when they can know no more, then they will play with it. It is well known that if you hold a candle near to a little child, he will (if you do not prevent him) put his finger into the flame, for he desires to know what it is that shines so bright. 

But there is something more than all this, for even these infants desire to improve their knowledge. Thus, when they look upon anything, if the sight does not inform them sufficiently, they will, if they can, get it into their hands so that they may feel it; but if the touch also does not satisfy, they will put it into their mouths to taste it, as if they would examine things by more senses than one. Now, this desire to know is born with them, and it is the best and most mysterious part of their nature. It is to be observed that when men come to their full age and are serious in their investigations, they are ashamed to err, because it is the property of their nature to know. 

Thus we see that a philosopher, being caught in a mistake in his discourse, will blush as if he had committed something unworthy of himself; and truly the very sense of this disgrace prevails so far with some that they would rather persist in their error and defend it against the truth than acknowledge their infirmities—in which respect I have no doubt that many *Peripateticks* <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> are perversely ignorant. It may be they will scarcely hear what I speak, or, if they hear, they will not understand. However, I advise them not willfully to prevent and hinder that glorious end

<summary>The author argues that the desire for knowledge is an innate, God-given human instinct, demonstrating this through the behavior of infants who explore the world with all their senses. He also critiques proud philosophers, specifically the Peripatetics, who would rather defend an error than admit they are wrong.</summary>

<keywords>epistemology, human nature, child development, Aristotle, Peripatetics, Truth, desire for knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the "To the Reader" preface, moving from the innate desire for knowledge to the theological and philosophical necessity of that knowledge being attainable, while introducing the concept of the "First Matter."</meta>

# TO THE READER.

and perfection for which the very Author and Father of Nature created them. It is a terrible thing to prefer *Aristotle* to *Elohim*, and condemn the truth of God to justify the opinion of Man. Now, for my part, I dare not be so irreligious as to think God so vain and improvident in His works that He should plant in man a desire to know, and yet deny him knowledge itself. This, in plain terms, would be to give me eyes, and afterwards shut me up in darkness, lest I should see with those eyes. This earnest longing and busy investigation in which men tire themselves to attain to the truth, made a certain master of truth speak in this fashion. *Ergo liquido apparet in hac Mundi structurâ, quam cernimus, aliquam triumphare Veritatem; quæ toties rationem nostram commovet, agitat, implicat, explicat; toties inquietatem, toties insomnem miris modis sollicitat, non fortuitis, aut aliunde adventitiis, sed suis et propriis et originariis Naturæ illicibus; quæ omnia cum non fiunt frustra, utique contingit, ut veritatem eorum quæ sunt, aliquo tandem opportuno tempore amplexemur.* “It is clear therefore (says he) that in this fabric of the world, which we behold, there is some truth that triumphs; which truth so often stirs up, puzzles, complicates, and explains our reason; so often solicits her when she is restless, so often when she is watchful, and this by strange means, not casual and adventitious, but by genuine provocations and pleasures of Nature; all which motions being not to no purpose, it happens at last that in some good time we attain to the true knowledge of those things that are.” 

But because I would not have you build your philosophy on corals and whistles, which are the objects of little children, of whom we have spoken formerly, I will speak somewhat of those elements in whose contemplation a man ought to employ himself, and this discourse may serve as a preface to our whole philosophy. Man, according to *Trismegistus*, has but two elements in his power, namely, earth and water, to which doctrine I add this—and I have it from a greater than *Hermes*—that God has made man absolute Lord of the First Matter, and from the First Matter, and the management of it, all the fortunes of man, both good and bad, do proceed. According to the rule and measure of this substance, all the world is rich or poor, and he who knows it truly, and withal the true use of it, he can make his fortunes constant; but he who knows it not, though his estate be never so great, stands on a slippery foundation. Look about you then, and consider how you are surrounded with infinite treasures and miracles, but you are so blind you do not see them; nay, you are so mad, you do think there is no use to be made of them, for you believe that knowledge is a mere Peripa-

<summary>The author argues that God would not grant humans a desire for knowledge without the means to achieve it, and introduces the "First Matter" as the source of all human fortune and wealth.</summary>

# TO THE READER.

...tical chat, and that its fruits are words, not works. If this were true, I would never advise you to spend one minute of your life on learning. I would be among the first who would ruin all the libraries and universities in the world—which God forbid any good Christian should desire. Look up then to Heaven, and when you see the celestial fires move in their swift and glorious circles, think also that there are cold natures here below which they overlook, and about which they move incessantly to heat and refine them. Consider, again, that the Middle Spirit—I mean the air—is placed in between as a cooling vessel to temper and qualify that heat, which otherwise might be too violent. If you descend lower and fix your thoughts where your feet are—so that your wings may be like those of Mercury at your heels—you will find the earth surrounded by water. That water, heated and stirred by the sun and his stars, extracts from the earth the pure, subtle, and salty parts, by which means the water is thickened and coagulated as if with rennet. Out of these two, Nature generates all things. Gold and silver, pearls and diamonds, are nothing else but water and the salt of the earth refined. Behold! I have in a few words revealed to you the whole system of Nature and her royal highway of generation. It is your duty now to improve upon the truth, and in my book you may, if you are wise, find your advantages. The four elements are the objects and, implicitly, the subjects of man, but the earth is invisible. I know the ordinary person will stare at this and judge me as not being very sober when I affirm that the earth, which of all things is most coarse and palpable, is invisible. But, on my soul, it is so; and what is more, the eye of man never saw the earth, nor can it be seen without art. To make this element visible is the greatest secret in Magic, for it is a miraculous Nature, and of all others the most holy, according to that reckoning of Trismegistus: “the Heaven, the Ether, the Air, and the most sacred Earth.”

As for this foul, coarse body upon which we walk, it is a compost and not earth, though it has earth within it—and even that is not our Magical Earth. In a word, all the elements are visible but one, namely the earth; and when you have attained to such perfection as to know why God has placed the Earth *in hiding*, you have an excellent way to know God Himself, and how He is visible and how He is invisible. Hermes affirms that in the beginning the earth was a quagmire, or a quivering kind of jelly, being nothing else but water congealed by the incubation and heat of the Divine Spirit. *Cum adhuc* (he says) *terra tremula esset, lucente sole compacta est.* “When the earth was still a quivering, shaking

For as we know the sun does not only dispense heat but some other secret influence, so did God also in the Creation; and from him the sun and all the stars received what they have, for God himself is a Supernatural Sun, or fire, according to that oracle of Zoroaster:

->The Architect who by his power alone<-
->Built up the cosmos, manifests himself<-
->Another orb of flame.<-

He therefore hatched the Matter and brought out the secret essences, as a chick is brought out of the shell; hence that position of the same Zoroaster—

->One single heat produced all that exists.<-

He did not only generate them then, but he also preserves them now with a perpetual flow of heat and spirit. Hence he is styled in the Oracles,

->Eternal Father of both Gods and Men,<-
->Who the fire, the light, the starry air,<-
->And all the golden sequence of the worlds,<-
->Most copiously animates.<-

is nowhere understood. When I first saw the libel, and its address to Philalethes, I judged the author to be serious, and that his design was not to abuse me but to inform himself. This notion quickly vanished, for, perusing his opening, his ears shot out of his skin and revealed him to be a perfect ass. His observations are one continued “ass's skin,” and the oyster-wives read the same philosophy every day. It is a scurrilous, senseless piece, and, as he well styles himself, a chip off a blockhead.

His qualities indeed are extraordinary abroad, but they are peers at home; his malice is equal to his ignorance. I laughed to see the fool’s disease—a flow of gall which made him drip at the mouth, while another held the press for him like Porphyry’s basin to Aristotle’s well <note>This likely refers to a legend or metaphor regarding Porphyry's role in preserving or receiving Aristotle's works, though here used to insult the critic's dependency on others.</note>. There is something in him monstrous; his excrement runs the wrong way, for his mouth defecates, and he is so far from being a man that he is an exaggeration of a beast. These are his parts, and as for his person, I turn him over to the dog-whippers, that he may be well lashed, *a posteriori*, and bear the *errata* of his front imprinted in his rear. I cannot yet find a fitter punishment, for since his head could learn nothing but nonsense, by the logical sequence of his parts, his tail should be taught some sense.

This is all, at this time, and for my present discourse, I wish it the common fortune of truth and honesty: to deserve well and hear ill. As for applause, I do not fish in the air so much as to catch it. It is a kind of popularity which makes me scorn it, for I defy the noise of the rabble, because they do not observe the truth, but the success of it. I do, therefore, commit this piece to the world without any protection but its own worth, and the estimation of that Soul that understands it. For the rest, as I cannot force, so I will not beg for their approval. I would not be great by taxes, nor rich by briefs <note>Briefs refers to royal mandates or letters patent authorizing collections in churches for specific charities or individuals.</note>. They may be what they will, and I shall be what I am.

->EUGENIUS PHILALETHES.<-

# ADAMIC MAGIC;

->OR,<-

## THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'T' decorated with stylized vines, leaves, and scrolls within a square frame.</image-desc>That I should profess Magic in this discourse, and justify its professors as well, is impiety to many but religion to me. It is a conscience I have learned from authors greater than myself, and Scriptures greater than both. Magic is nothing else but the wisdom of the Creator revealed and planted in the creature. It is a name (as Agrippa says) *ipsi Evangelio non ingratum*, not distasteful to the very Gospel itself. Magicians were the first attendants our Savior met with in this world, and the only philosophers who acknowledged him in the flesh before he himself revealed it. I find God conversing with them, as he was formerly with the patriarchs; he directs them in their travels with a star, as he did the Israelites with a pillar of fire; he informs them of future dangers in their dreams, so that having first seen his Son, they might in the next place see his salvation. This makes me believe they were Sons of the Prophets as well as Sons of Art—men who were acquainted with the very same mysteries by which the prophets acted before them. To reconcile this science, and its masters, to the world is an attempt more plausible than possible, the prejudice being so great that neither reason nor authority can balance it. If I were to persuade a Jew to my principles, I could do it with two words: אמרו חכמים, the *Hachamim, or Wise Men*, have spoken it. Give him but the authority of his fathers, and he immediately submits to the seal. Truly, our primitive *Galileans* (I mean those Christians whose lamps burned near the cross and burial) were most direct in their initiations. A *proselyte* in those days was con-

> "You have (said he) nothing more than your *Creed* to establish your religion."
Such was the simplicity of those first times,
> "While the blood of Christ yet shone,"
while his wounds were still in their eyes, and his blood warm at their hearts. But, alas! those holy drops are frozen; our salvation is translated from the cross to the rack, and dismembered in the inquisition-house of Aristotle. Do not be angry, O Peripatetic! For what else shall I call your schools, where by several sects and factions Scripture is so seriously murdered *pro et con*! A bitterness first bred and afterwards promoted by disputes, whose damnable divisions and distinctions have minced one truth into a thousand heretical whimsies. But the breach is not considered; divinity is still but chaff if it is not sifted by the engine, if it acts not by the demonstrative hobby-horse. Thus, zeal, poisoned with logic, breathes out contentious fevers, and faith, quitting her wings and perspective, leans on the reed of a syllogism. Certainly I cannot yet conceive how reason may judge those principles, *Quorum veritas pendet á solâ Revelantis authoritate*,
> "whose certainty wholly depends on the authority of the Revealer,"
and, by consequence, is indemonstrable without the Spirit of God. But if I should grant that, which I will ever deny, truly, a true faith consists not in reason, but in love; for I receive my principles, and believe them once received, *solo erga Revelantem amore*,
> "only out of affection to Him that reveals them."

Thus our Savior would have the Jews believe him first for his own sake, and when that failed, for his work's sake. But some divines believe only for Aristotle's sake; if logic renders the tenet probable, then it is Creed; if not, it is the Alcoran <note>The Quran</note>. Nevertheless, Aristotle himself, who was the first peddler of this ware, and may for sophistry take the place of Ignatius in his own conclave, has left us this concession:
> "That reason is subject to error as well as opinion."
And Philoponus, expounding these words of his, *Non solùm scientiam, sed et principium scientiæ esse aliquod dicimus, quo cognoscimus terminos*, that is,
> "We say not only science but also the principle of science to be something by which we understand the terms,"
has this excellent and Christian observation:
> "Taking indeed (says he) the mind to be the principle or first cause of knowledge—not our own, but that of God which is above us—but taking the Terms to be Intellectual and Divine Forms."
Thus, according to Aristotle (if you trust the commentary), the Divine Mind is the first cause of knowledge; for if this Mind once unfolds itself and sheds its light

These Forms he very properly calls *ὅρους* <note>horous: boundaries or limits</note>, because they terminate or end all things, for by them the creature is defined and has its individuation, or, to speak with Scotus, its *Haecceity*, by which it is this and not that. This, now, is the demonstration we should look for: namely, the expansion or opening of the Divine Mind, not a syllogism that runs perhaps on all fours. If we are once admitted to this Communion of Light, we shall be able, with the Apostle, to give a reason for our faith, but never without it. Now, you must understand that God does not unfold himself, *nisi magno coelo priùs patefacto*, “unless the Heaven of Man is first unfolded.” <margin>Cornelius Agrippa.</margin> “*Amovete ergo velamen intellectus vestri*,” “Remove, therefore, the veil from your intellect,” or “Cast off the Veil that is before your faces,” and you shall be blind no longer. God is not a God far off, but a God at hand. “Behold,” he says, “I stand at the door and knock.” Open yourselves then, for it is written, “If any man opens, I will come in and sup with him.” This is the Inward, Mystical Supper, not the Outward, Symbolic one, and this is the Spiritual Baptism with Fire, not that elemental one with water.

Truly, I am much comforted when I consider two things—first, that Magic provided the first professors of Christianity, whose knowledge and devotion brought them from the East to Jerusalem; secondly, that this art should suffer as religion does, and for the very same reason. The main motives which have caused the present rifts and divisions of the Church are the ceremonies and the symbols used in it; for, without controversy, the Apostles instituted and left behind certain elements or signs, such as water, oil, salt, and lights, by which they signified to us some great and revered Mysteries. But our Reformers, mistaking these things for superstitions, turned them all out of doors. But, verily, it was ill-advised, for if the shadow of St. Peter healed, shall not these shadows of Christ do much more? The Papist, on the contrary, not knowing the meaning of these symbols, placed a certain inherent holiness in them, and so fell into a very dangerous idolatry. I omit many things which he invented on his own, such as images, holy lambs, and relics, adding these dead bones to the primitive and beautiful body of the Church. Now to draw the parallel: the Magicians also instituted certain signs as the Key to their Art, and these were the same as the former—namely, water, oil, salt, and light—by which they silently revealed to us their three Principles and the Light of Nature, which fills and animates all things. The common man, reading their books but not understanding their meaning, took candles, common water,

oil, and salt, and began to consecrate and exorcise them, to make up his damnable and devilish magic. The magicians had a maxim among themselves, *Quod nulla vox operatur in Magiâ, nisi prius Dei voce formetur*, "That no word is effective in Magic, unless it is first animated by the word of God." Hence in their books there was frequent mention made of *Verbum* [Word] and *Sermo* [Speech], which the common man, interpreting according to his own fancy, invented his charms and *vocabula* <note>specialized words or incantations</note>, by which he promised to do wonders. The magicians in their writings talked much of triangles and circles, by which they suggested to us their more secret Triplicity, with the rotation of Nature from the beginning of her Week to her Sabbath. By this circle also, or rotation, they affirmed that Spirits might be bound, meaning that the Soul might be united to the body. Presently upon this, the common man fancied his triangles and characters, with many strange cobwebs or figures, and a circle to conjure in; but not knowing what spirit that was which the magicians did bind, he labored and studied to bind the devil. Now, if you will question me who these magicians were, I must tell you they were Kings, they were Priests, they were Prophets—men who were acquainted with the Substantial, Spiritual Mysteries of Religion, and who dealt or dispensed the outward, typical part of it to the people. Here then we may see how Magic came to be out of request, for the lawyers and common divines, who knew not these secrets, perusing the ceremonial, superstitious trash of some scribblers who pretended to Magic, prescribed against the Art itself as impious and antichristian, so that it was a capital sin to profess it, and the punishment no less than death. In the interim, those few who were the first masters of the science, observing the first warnings of it, buried all in a deep silence. But God, having suffered his truth to be obscured for a great time, did at last stir up some resolute and active spirits, who, putting pen to paper, expelled this cloud, and in some measure discovered the light. The leaders of this brave body were Cornelius Agrippa, Libanius Gallius the philosopher, Johannes Trithemius, Georgius Venetus, Johannes Reuchlin, called in the Greek Capnion, with several others in their several days, and after all these, as an usher to the train, and one born out of due time, Eugenius Philalethes.

Seeing then I have publicly undertaken a promise, which I might have governed privately with much more content and advantage, I think it not enough to have discovered the abuses and misfortunes this science has suffered, unless I endeavor withal to

demonstrate its antiquity. For certainly it is with arts as it is with men: their age and continuance are good arguments of their strength and integrity. Most appropriate, then, was that rebuke of the Egyptian to Solon:
> *O Solon, Solon! Vos Græci semper pueri estis, nullam antiquam habendes Opinionem, nullam disciplinam tempore canam.*

> "You Grecians (said he) are always childish, having no ancient opinion, no discipline of any great age."

But as I confess myself to be no antiquarian, I wish some Selden <note>Likely a reference to John Selden (1584–1654), the famous English polymath and scholar of ancient history.</note> would stand in this gap and repair it with those fragments which are so near to dust that time may put them in his hourglass. I know, for my own part, it is an enterprise I cannot sufficiently perform; but since my hand is already in the bag, I will draw out those few pebbles I have, and thus I fling them at the mark.

This Art, or rather this Mystery, is to be considered in several ways, and that because of its various subjects. Its primitive, original existence is God himself, for it is nothing else but the practice or operation of the Divine Spirit working in matter, uniting principles into compounds, and resolving those compounds into their principles. In this sense we seek not its antiquity, for it is eternal, being a concept of the Divine Wisdom existing before all time or the creation of it. Secondly, we are to consider it in a derivative sense, as it was imparted and communicated to man; and this properly was no birth or beginning, but a discovery or revelation of the Art. From this time of its revelation we are to measure its antiquity, where it shall be our task to demonstrate God’s motives for revealing it, as well as to whom and when.

The eye does not see beyond that stage where it is focused, but the ear receives sound from a great distance. To give firsthand testimony of actions more ancient than ourselves is a thing impossible for us, unless we could look into that mirror where all occurrences may be seen—past, present, and to come. I must therefore build my discourse on the traditions of those men to whom the Word, both written and mystical, was entrusted; and these were the Jews in general, but more particularly their Cabalists. It is not my intention to rely on these Rabbis as foundational authorities, but I will justify their assertions using Scripture and entertain my reader with proofs both divine and human. Finally, I will pass out of Judea into Egypt and Greece, where again I shall meet with these Mysteries and prove that this science did stream (as the <term>Chimists</term> say their Salt Fountain does) out of Jewry and watered the whole Earth.

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the divine origins of magic, specifically addressing how the Fall of Adam disrupted the original, perfect communion between the celestial and terrestrial realms.</meta>

# MAGIA ADAMICA ; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

In Adam's time, there was a more plentiful and large communion between Heaven and Earth, God and the Elements, than there is now in our days. But upon the transgression of the first man, <term>Malchuth</term> (say the Cabalists) was cut off from the <term>Ilan</term>,* so that a breach was made between both worlds, and their channel of influences was discontinued. Now <term>Malchuth</term> is the Invisible, Archetypal Moon, by which our visible, celestial moon is governed and impregnated. And truly it may be that upon this retreat of the Divine Light from inferior things, those spots and darkness which we now see followed in the body of this planet—and not in her alone, but also around the sun, as has been discovered by the telescope. Thus (they say), God, to punish the sin of Adam, withdrew himself from the creatures, so that they were not feasted with the same measure of influences as formerly. For the Archetypal Moon, which is placed in the <term>Haschamayim</term> (the Heavens), to receive and convey down the influx of the six superior Invisible Planets, was (as the Jews affirm) either separated from the <term>Ilan</term>, or her breasts were so sealed up that she could not dispense her milk to inferior beings in that happy and primitive abundance. But because I would not dwell long on this point, let us hear the <margin>Porta Lucis.</margin> Cabalist himself state it in a clear and appropriate phrase. *Initia Creationis Mundi Divina Cohabitatio erat descendens in Inferiora, et cum esset Divina cohabitatio inferiùs reperti sunt Coeli et Terra uniti, et erant Fontes, et Canales activi in perfectione, et trahebantur à Superiore ad Inferius, et inveniebatur Deus complens supernè et infernè. Venit Adam primus, et peccavit, et diruti sunt Descensus, et compacti sunt Canales, et desiit Aquæ ductus, et cessavit Divina Cohabitatio, et divisa est Societas.* That is: “In the Beginning of the Creation of the world, the Divine Presence descended to dwell with things here below; and while the Divine Presence was here below, the Heavens and the Earth were found to be united, and the vital springs and channels were in their perfection, and flowed from the Superior to the Inferior World, and God was found to fill all things both Above and Beneath. Adam, the first man, came and sinned, whereupon the descents from above were restrained, their Channels were broken, the Water-Course was no more, the Divine Presence ceased to dwell here, and the Society was divided.” Thus far my Rabbi. Now because I have promised Scripture to support my Cabalism, I will submit the tradition to Moses; and truly that Rabbi is also on my side, for thus I read in Genesis: “And to Adam he said, Because you have eaten of the tree of which I commanded you, saying, 'You shall not eat of it,' cursed is the ground for
