your sake; in sorrow shall you eat of it all the days of your life. Thorns and thistles shall it bring forth for you, and you shall eat <margin>Genesis ch. 3, v. 17.</margin> the herb of the field. In the sweat of your face shall you eat bread, until you return to the ground, for out of it you were taken; for dust you are, and to dust shall you return.” This is the curse, and Adam was so aware of it that he informed his posterity of it. For Lamech, prophesying of his son Noah, has these words: “This same shall comfort us, concerning our work and <margin>Gen. 5:29.</margin> toil of our hands, because of the ground which the Lord has cursed.” And this indeed was accomplished in some sense after the Flood, as the same Scripture tells us: “And the Lord said in his <margin>Gen. 8:21.</margin> heart, I will not again curse the ground any more for man's sake.”

Here now we are to consider two things: first, the curse itself, and next the extent of it. To demonstrate the nature of the curse, and what it was, you must know that good essentially is light, and evil is darkness. Evil, properly, is a corruption that immediately takes place upon the removal of that which is good. Thus, God having removed his candlestick and light from the elements, immediately the darkness and cold of matter prevailed, so that the earth was nearer its first deformity, and, by consequence, less fruitful and vital. Heaven and Hell—that is, light and darkness—are the two extremes which complete good and evil. But there are some intermediate blessings which are but *in ordine* <note>In order</note>, or predisposing toward Heaven, which is their final perfection; and such were these blessings which God recalled upon the transgression of the first man. Again, there are some evils which are but degrees leading to their final extremity, or Hell; and such was this curse, or evil, which followed the transgression. Thus our Savior, under these notions of blessed and cursed, encompasses the inhabitants of light and darkness: *Come, you blessed, and go, you cursed.* In a word then, the curse was nothing else but an act repealed, or a restraint of those blessings which God out of his mere goodness had formerly shared with his creatures. And thus I conceive there is a very fair and full harmony between Moses and the Cabalists. But to omit their testimonies, though great and high, we do not need to seek in this matter for the testimony of an angel. For the tutor of Esdras <note>Referring to the Archangel Uriel in the book of 2 Esdras</note>, among his other mysterious instructions, also has this doctrine: “When Adam transgressed my statutes, then was that decreed which now is done. Then were the entrances of this world made narrow, <margin>2 Esdras 7:11-13.</margin> full of sorrow and travail: they are but few and evil, full of perils, and very painful. But the entrances of the older world were wide and sure, and brought forth immortal fruit.” Thus much for the

For if God had excluded him from Eden and continued the earth in her primitive glories, he would have only turned him out of one Paradise into another; therefore he fits the dungeon to the slave and sends a corruptible man into a corruptible world. But in truth, it was not man nor the earth alone that suffered this Curse, but all other creatures also; for God says to the serpent:

> “You are cursed above all cattle, and above every beast of the field,”

so that cattle and beasts also were cursed in some measure, but this serpent above them all. To this the apostle also refers in his Epistle to the *Romans*, where he has
<margin>Ch. 8, v. 20.</margin>
these words:

> “For the creature was made subject to vanity, not willingly, but by reason of him who has subjected the same in hope. Because the creature itself also shall be delivered from the bondage of corruption into the glorious liberty of the Children of God.”

Here, by "the creature," he understands not man but the inferior species, which he distinguishes from the Children of God, though he allows them both the same liberty. But this is clearer from the subsequent texts, where he makes a clear difference between man and the whole creation.

> “For we know (says he) that the whole creation groans and travails together in pain until now. And not only they, but ourselves also, who have the first fruits of the Spirit, even we ourselves groan within ourselves, waiting for the adoption, namely, the redemption of our body.”

Here we see the first fruits of the Spirit referred to man, and why not some second subordinate fruits of it to the creatures in general? For as they were cursed in the fall of man, for man’s sake, so it seems in his restoration they shall also be blessed for his sake. But enough of this. Let us now sum up and consider the several hardships our first parent was subject to, for they will be of some use to us hereafter. First of all, he was ejected from the presence of God and exposed to the malice and temptations of the devil. He was changed from good to bad, from incorruptible to corruptible.

> “In the day (says the Scripture) you eat of it, you shall surely die.”

He was excluded from a glorious paradise and confined to a base world, whose sickly, infected elements, conspiring with his own nature, assisted and hastened that death which had already begun to reign in his body. Heaven mourned over him; the earth and all her generations mourned around him. He looked upon himself as a felon and a murderer, being guilty of that curse and corruption which followed in the world because of his fall, as we have sufficiently proved from

the Mosaic and Cabalistic traditions. He was ignorant, and therefore hopeless, of Eternal Life, and as for this present temporal life, he was not acquainted with its provisions. The elements of husbandry were not as yet known; there was neither house nor plow, nor any of those manual arts which constitute worldly foresight. He was exposed to the violence of rains and winds, frosts and snows, and, in a word, deprived of all comforts, spiritual and natural. What more should I say? He was a mere stranger in this world, could not distinguish medicines from poisons, nor was he skilled in the ordinary preparations of food and drink. He had no food ready to his hands but the raw, unseasoned herbage of the earth, so that he had to either starve or feed, as *Nebuchadnezzar* did, with the beasts of the field. He heard indeed sometimes of a tree of life in *Eden*, but the plants of this world, for all he knew, might be so many trees of death. I conclude, therefore, that he had some instructor to initiate him in the ways of life, and to show him the intricate and narrow paths of that wilderness. For, without question, his outward miseries and his inward despair were motives for which God revealed a certain art to him by which he might relieve his present necessities, and embrace a firm hope of a future and glorious restoration. For God, having ordained a second, eternal *Adam*, did by some mysterious experience manifest the possibility of his coming to the first, who being now full of despair, and overwhelmed with the guilt of his own sin, was a very fit patient for such a divine and merciful physician. But, omitting our own reasons, which we might produce for this purpose, let us turn to the Cabalists, who indeed are very profound on this point, and thus they express themselves. God (they say) having barred the doors of his *Paradise*, and turned out *Adam*—once the dearest of his creatures—nevertheless retained his former affection for him despite the present punishment. For God is said to love his creatures, not because there is anything lovely in them apart from their creator, but because he desires their perfection. That is to say, he would have them conform to himself, and be fit to receive his image or likeness, which is a spiritual impression of his beauty. Now, to restore this likeness in *Adam* was impossible, unless God should take back to himself that which had now fallen away from him. So transcendent and almost incredible a mercy had God treasured up in his secret will, being resolved to unite the nature of man to his own, and so liberate him from death by taking him into the *Deity*, which is the true fountain and center of life. This will (say the Cabalists) was first revealed to the angels, and that by God

<margin>Gen. iii. 22.</margin>
himself, in these words: *Ecce Adam sicut unus ex nobis*: “Behold, Adam is like one of us, knowing good and evil!” This speech they call *Orationem occultissimam à Creatore mundi cum beatis angelis in suæ Divinitatis penetralibus habitam*, “a most secret conference which the Creator of the world had with the blessed angels in the inner chambers of His Divinity.” Now, that the same Scripture should speak one thing in the letter and another in the mystery is not strange to me, however difficult it may seem to another. For, truly, this text may not concern the first Adam, who, knowing evil by committing it, could not be like God in respect of that knowledge, which made him sinful and altogether unlike Him. For God (if I may so express it) knows the evil only speculatively, inasmuch as nothing can escape His knowledge, and therefore He is not guilty of evil; for, as Trithemius has well observed, *scientia mali non est malum sed usus*—“The knowledge of evil is not evil, but the practice of it.” It remains then that this speech concerned the second Adam, Christ Jesus, who knew the evil but did not commit it, and therefore was “like one of us,” that is, like one of the Trinity, knowing good and evil, and yet in no way guilty of the evil. This primitive and compendious gospel was no sooner imparted to the angels than they became ministers of it, the Law (as St. Paul says) “being ordained into their hands,” until Christ should take it into His own; and their administration to man began with this oracle. Thus (say the Cabalists), Raziel the angel was presently dispatched to communicate the intelligence to Adam, and to acquaint him with the mysteries of both worlds, eternal and temporal. For as he could not obtain the blessings of the eternal world unless by a true faith he apprehended the Three Eternal Principles of it, so neither could he fully enjoy the benefits of this temporal world unless he truly understood the Three Visible Substances of which it consists. For there are Three above and Three beneath—Three (as St. John says) in Heaven and Three on earth. The inferior bear witness to the superior, and are their only proper receptacles. They are signatures and created books where we may read the mysteries of the supernatural Trinity. But to proceed in our former discourse: The Cabalists do not only attribute a guardian to Adam, but to every one of the patriarchs, allowing them their presidents and tutors both to assist and instruct them in their wearisome and worldly travels—a doctrine in my opinion no more religious than necessary, however prodigious it may seem to some fantastic, insipid theologians. For certainly it is impossible for us to find out mysteries by ourselves; we must either have the Spirit of

God or the instruction of His ministers, whether they be men or angels. And thus we see from the traditions and doctrine of the Jews how their <term>Cabala</term> and our Magic first came into the world. I shall now examine the Scriptures and consult with them, where (if I am not much mistaken) I shall find certain consequences which must necessarily depend on these principles, and thus I apply myself to the task.

The first harvest I read of was that of Cain, and the first flocks those of Abel. A shepherd’s life, in those early days, was no difficult profession, as it was an employment of more care than art; but how the earth was plowed before the sound of Tubal’s hammers is a piece of husbandry unknown in these days. However, it was a labor performed, and not without reward. Cain has his sheaves as well as Abel his lambs; both of them receive and both acknowledge the benefit. I find established in these two a certain priesthood; they both attend the altar, and the first blood was shed by sacrifice, the second by murder.

Now, I am so dull and so lacking in syllogisms—those strange pumps and <term>hydragogues</term> <note>devices for drawing or conveying water</note> which draw the truth *from the well* like water—that all my reason cannot make these men <term>Levites</term> without Revelation. For I desire to know how they first came to sacrifice, and by whom were they initiated? If you say by Adam, the question is indeed deferred but not satisfied; for I would further know: in what school was Adam instructed? Now, I will undertake to demonstrate by invincible reason, which no adversary dare contradict, that it was impossible for him to invent these shadows and sacraments by himself.

It is most certain that the hope and expectation of man in matters of sacrifice consist in the thing signified, and not in the sign itself. For the material, corruptible shadow is not the object of faith, but rather the spiritual, eternal prototype which corresponds to it and makes the dead figure effectual. The sacrifices of the Old Testament and the elements of the New can be in no way acceptable to God except as they have a relation to Jesus Christ, who is the great, perfect sacrifice offered up once for all. It is plain, then, that sacrifices were first instituted upon supernatural grounds, for in Nature there is no reason to be found why God should be pleased with the death of His creatures. Indeed, the very opposite is written in that book; for death, both natural and violent, proceeds not from the pleasure but from the displeasure of the Creator. I know the learned Alkind builds the efficacy of sacrifices on a sympathy of parts with the great world, for there is in every animal a portion of

This indeed is true, but that motion causes no joy there, and, consequently, no reward to the sacrifices; for I shall make it appear elsewhere that the Astral Mother mourns and does not rejoice at the death of her children. Now, if we look back on these first two sacrifices, we shall find Abel and his oblation accepted, which could not be, had he not offered it up as a symbol, or figure, of his Savior. To drive home my argument, then, I say that this knowledge of the type, in whom all offerings were acceptable, could not be obtained by any human industry but by sole revelation. For the Passion of Christ Jesus was an ordinance wrapped up in the secret will of God, and he that would know it must of necessity be of His council. Hence it is called in Scripture the Hidden Mystery, for the truth and certainty of it was not to be received from any, but only from Him who had both the will and the power to ordain it.

<margin>*Porphyry, On Sacrifice*</margin>
And if you will tell me (like the author of the *Predicables*) that men sacrificed at first by the instinct of Nature, and without any respect to the type, I shall indeed thank you for my mirth whenever you give me so just a reason to laugh. It remains, then, a most firm, infallible foundation that Adam was first instructed concerning the Passion, and, in order to that, he was taught further to sacrifice and offer up the blood of beasts as types and <term>prodromes</term> <note>precursors or forerunners</note> of the blood of Christ Jesus, the altars of the Law being but steps to the Cross of the Gospel. Now, if it is objected that several nations have sacrificed who did not know God at all, much less the Son of God, who is the prototype and perfection of all oblations, to this I answer that the custom of sacrificing was communicated to heathens by tradition from the first man, who, having instructed his own children, they also delivered it to their posterity, so that this <term>vizard</term> <note>mask or outward appearance</note> of religion remained, though the substance and true doctrine of it was lost. And thus, in my opinion, it sufficiently appears that the first men sacrificed, not by Nature, as Porphyrius, that enemy of our religion, would have it, but some by revelation, others by custom and tradition. But, now I think upon it, I have Scripture to confirm me concerning this primitive revelation, for Solomon, numbering those several blessings which the Divine Wisdom imparted to the ancient Fathers, among the rest specifies her indulgence to Adam. “She preserved (says he) the first-found father of the world, that was created alone, and brought him out of his fall.” Here I find Adam in some measure restored,

and how could that be but by discovering to him the **Great Restorative**, Christ Jesus, the second Adam, in whom he was to believe? For without faith he could not have been brought out of his Fall, and without Christ revealed and preached to him, he could have no faith, for he knew not what to believe. It remains, then, that he was instructed; for as in these last days we are taught by the Son of God and his apostles, so in those first times they were taught by the Spirit of God and his ministering angels. These were their tutors, for from them they heard the Word, and verily we are told that faith comes by hearing.

It is now (as I think) sufficiently proved that Adam had his metaphysics from above. Our next service (and perhaps somewhat difficult) is to give some probable, if not demonstrative, reasons that they came not alone, but had their physics also to attend them. I know the Scriptures are not positive in this point, and hence the sects will lug in their "consequence of reprobation." Truly, for my part, I do not desire their ruin, but their patience. I have, though against the precept, for many years attended their Philosophy, and if they spend a few hours on my <margin>Acts 17:18.</margin> <term>Spermalogie</term> <note>literally "seed-talk"; a study of the origins or seeds of things</note> it may cost them some part of their justice but none of their favors. But that we may come to the matter in hand: I hold it very necessary to distinguish arts, for I have not yet seen any author who has fully considered their difference. The Art I speak of is truly physical in subject, method, and effect; but as for arts publicly professed, and allowed to the disadvantage of truth, not one of them is so qualified, for they are mere knacks and baubles of the hand or brain, having no fundamentals in Nature. These, in my opinion, Solomon numbers among his vanities, <margin>Eccl. 7:29.</margin> when he speaks in a certain place, "That God had made man upright but he had sought out many inventions." Of these inventions we have a short catalogue in Genesis, where Moses separates the corn from the chaff, the works of God from the whimsies of man. Thus we read that Jubal was the father of such as dwell in tents, his brother Tubal the father of all such as handle the harp and organ, and Tubal-Cain an instructor of every artificer in brass and iron. What mischiefs have succeeded this brass-and-iron Cyclops, I need not tell you. If you know not the fates of former times, you may study the actions of your own; you live in an age that can instruct you. Verily, it is worth our observation that these arts, and their tools, proceeded not from the posterity of Seth, in whose line our Savior stands—for, as we shall make it appear hereafter, unquestionably they had a better knowledge—but they proceeded from the seed of Cain, who in action was a murderer, and in the circumstances of it a fratricide.

# MAGIA ADAMICA: OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

<margin>On the Vanity of Sciences</margin>
To be short, there is no vanity like the vanity of sciences—I mean those inventions and their professors which produce nothing true and natural, but only effects that are either false or, in their outcomes, corrupt and violent. But it is no conquest to tread on ruins. Cornelius Agrippa has already laid these vain boasts in the dirt, and did so skillfully that they have never since enjoyed a general reputation. Give me an Art, then, that is a perfect, entire map of the creation; one that can lead me directly to the knowledge of the true God, by which I can discover those Universal, Invisible Essences which are subordinate to Him—an Art that is in no way subject to evil, and by which I can attain all the secrets and mysteries in Nature. This is the Art in which the physics of Adam and the patriarchs consisted; and I will undertake to demonstrate through the Scriptures and the practice of his posterity that this Art was revealed to him.

This truth, I am certain, will seem difficult, if not incredible, to most men, as the providence of God is unfairly judged on this point; for they will not allow Him to instruct us in natural things, but only in supernatural ones, such as may concern our souls and their salvation. As for our bodies, they think He must not prescribe for their necessities by teaching us true medicine and discovering the laws of His creation; for though He made Nature, they believe He may not tutor us in natural sciences. By no means! Aristotle and his syllogisms can do it much better. Certainly, this opinion is no different from that of the Epicurean: *Deum ad Caeli cardines obambulare, et nulla tangi mortalium cura*—"That God takes the air in some unknown walks and quarters of His Heaven, but thinks nothing of us mortals who are here under His feet." Unquestionably, it is a most eminent impiety to 
<margin>Apology Against the Heathens, chapter 24.</margin> 
make God, as Tertullian said of old, *Otiosum et inexercitum neminem in rebus humanis*—"An idle, unprofitable nobody, having nothing to do with our affairs as they are natural and human." Surely these men are afraid lest His mercy should diminish His majesty; they allow Him to deal only with our immortal parts, not with our corruptible bodies that have the most need of His assistance. They consider the body a base subject which He has turned over to Galen and the apothecaries. Not so, my friend; He has created medicine and brings it out of the earth, but the Galenist does not know it. It is He who pities our afflictions; He is the Good Samaritan who does not pass us by in our miseries, but pours oil and wine into our wounds. This I know very well and will prove it from His own mouth. Did He not instruct Noah to build an Ark, to pitch it within and without, and this to save life at a time when He Himself was resolved to destroy it? At a time when the world was acquainted with no mechanics but a little hus-

<margin>Hebrews 4:15</margin>

When he was on earth, with the dust of that earth he made the blind to see, and of mere water he made wine. These were the visible elements of his physic, or rather (if the notion does not offend you) of his magic. But shall I show you his library, and in that his three-fold philosophy? Observe then first and censure afterwards. “Have salt in yourselves,” and again, “you are the salt of the earth,” and, in a third place, “Salt is good.” This is his mineral doctrine; will you know his vegetable? It is in two little books—Mustard-seed and a Lily. Lastly, he has his animal magic, and truly that’s a scroll sealed up, I know not who may open it.

First of all, have salt in yourself, for it will season your soul that is infected, and preserve your brains, that are putrefied with the dirt of Aristotle. # MAGIA ADAMICA; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

Let those in practice know in what sense “salt is most good.” Fourthly, examine the lilies by fire, and the water of fire, so that you may see their miraculous, invisible treasures, and how that speech of Truth is verified, “That Solomon in all his royalty was not clothed like one of them.” If you wish to attempt a higher Magic, you may, once you are first seasoned; but in this place it is not my design to lead you to it. You can never perfectly obtain animal and vegetable mysteries without the knowledge of the first mineral secret, namely, the salt of the earth—which is salt and yet no salt—and its preparation. This discourse, I confess, is somewhat remote from what I first intended—namely, that philosophy was first revealed to Adam as well as Divinity—but some heads are obstacles in their own way and, as I told you formerly, will not believe that God provides any natural secrets. This made me produce these few instances out of Scripture as preparations for the proposition itself and, if the reader is at all discerning, for him as well. I do not expect his compliance with my principles; no, I am so far from it that he may even withhold his charity. Let him be as rigid as justice can make him, for I do not wish to prevail in anything but the truth, and, in the name of truth, thus I begin.

You have been told formerly that Cain and Abel were instructed in matters of sacrifice by their father Adam, but since Cain murdered his brother Abel, his priesthood descended to Seth. This is confirmed by those faculties which attended his posterity, for Enoch, Lamech, and Noah were all of them prophets. It troubles you, perhaps, that I attribute a priesthood to Abel; but I have, besides his own practice, Christ’s testimony for it, who accounts the blood of Abel among that of the persecuted prophets and wise men. Now, to conclude that these men had no knowledge in philosophy because the Scripture does not mention any use they made of it is an argument that denies something but proves nothing. To show the vanity of this inference, I will give you an example from Moses himself. We know very well there are no prophecies of Abraham extant, nor do we read anywhere that he ever did prophesy; yet, notwithstanding this, he was a prophet. For God, reproving Abimelech, King of Gerar, who had taken Sarah from him, supposing she had been his sister, used these words: “Now, therefore, restore the man his wife, for he is a prophet, and he shall pray for you, and you shall live.” From this we may learn that the Holy Spirit does not always mention the secret perfections of the soul in the public character of the person. # MAGIA ADAMICA; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

...assent to this doctrine even if the Scriptures were silent in every text, if I did not find there some infallible steps of Magic, such as may lead me without a lantern to the archives of the Art itself. I know the crowd and tumult of other affairs are both the many and the main in the history of Moses. But in the whole current, I meet with some arts which may not be numbered among the fortunes of the patriarchs, but are extraordinary performances and speak of causes that are not common. I have ever admired that discipline of Eliezer, the steward of Abraham, who, when he prayed at the well in Mesopotamia, could make his camels also kneel. I must not believe there was any trickery <note>Hocus-pocus</note> in this, or that the spirit of Banks <note>A reference to William Banks and his famous performing horse, Marocco</note> may be the spirit of prayer. Jacob makes a covenant with Laban, that all the spotted and brown cattle in his flocks should be assigned to him for his wages. The bargain is no sooner made than he finds an art to multiply his own colors, and sends his father-in-law almost wool-gathering. <margin>Gen. 30:37.</margin> “And Jacob took for himself rods of green poplar, and of the hazel and chestnut tree, and peeled white streaks in them, and made the white appear which was in the rods; and he set the rods which he had peeled before the flocks in the gutters, in the watering troughs when the flocks came to drink, that they should conceive when they came to drink. And the flocks conceived before the rods, and brought forth cattle ring-streaked, speckled, and spotted.” As for that which the Scripture tells us elsewhere, namely, that <margin>Gen. 31:10.</margin> “Jacob saw in a dream, and behold, the rams that leaped on the cattle were ring-streaked, speckled, and grizzled,” this in no way impairs our assertion, or proves this generation miraculous and supernatural; for no man, I believe, is so mad as to think those appearances, or rams of the dream, did leap and supply the place of the natural males of the flock. God used this apparition only to signify the truth of that art Jacob acted by, and to tell him that his hopes were fulfilled.

But I shall not insist long on any particular, and therefore I will pass from this dream to another. Joseph, being seventeen years old—an age of some discretion—proposes a vision to his father, not loosely and to no purpose, as we tell one another of our dreams, but expecting, I believe, an interpretation, as knowing that his father had the skill to expound it. The wise patriarch, being not ignorant of the secrets of the two Luminaries, attributes males to the Sun and females to the Moon, then allows a third signification to the minor stars, and lastly answers his son with a question: “What is this dream that you have dreamed? Shall I and your mother and your brothers indeed come to bow down ourselves to you to the earth?”

For he who knows the analogy of parts to parts in this great body which we call the world may know what every sign signifies and, consequently, may prove a good interpreter of dreams. As for Jacob’s first practice, which we have previously mentioned—namely, the breeding of his speckled flocks—it is an effect so purely magical that our most stubborn adversaries dare not question it. I could cite one more passage which refers to this patriarch and points at the fundamentals of Magic, but if it were attached to this discourse, it would reveal too much; I shall, therefore, leave it to the search of those who are considerable experts, if not masters, in the Art. The sum of all is this: Man by himself could not attain to true knowledge; it was God who, in mere mercy, instructed him. To confirm this, I shall ask the reader to consider his own experience. We have in our day many magical books in existence, in which the Art is revealed both truly and plainly. We also have an infinite number of men who study those books, but after the efforts of a long life, not one in ten thousand understands them. Now, if we, with all these advantages, cannot attain the secrets of Nature, shall we think those first fathers did, who had none of our libraries to assist them, nor any learned man upon earth to instruct them? Could they do without means that which we cannot do even with means—and very significant means at that? The Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy</note> will perhaps tell me their syllogism is the engine that will perform all this. Let them then, in *Barbara* or *Baroco* <note>Technical terms for specific forms of logical syllogisms</note>, demonstrate the First Matter of the Philosophers’ Stone. But they will tell me there is no such thing. Behold, I tell them again—and assure them on my salvation—there is; but, in truth, their logic will never find it out. It is clear, then, that God first instructed Adam; from him his children received it, and by their tradition, it descended to the patriarchs, every father bequeathing these secrets to his child as his best and most lasting legacy. I have now followed Jacob, the Israel of God, both in his pilgrimage at Padan-Aram and in his symbolic inheritance, the pledge of the land of Canaan. But two relocations do not complete the wanderings of a patriarch; God calls him from the habitation of his fathers to the prison of his posterity, and provides him a place of freedom in the house of bondage. > "See, I have made you a God to Pharaoh, and your brother Aaron shall be your prophet."

Truly, it cannot be denied that some branches of this art, though extremely corrupted, were dispersed among all nations by tradition from the first man, and this appears by more testimonies than one. For in the land of Canaan, before Israel ever possessed it, Debir—which Athniel, the son of Kenaz, conquered—was a university, or at least had in it a famous library; therefore the Jews called it Kiriath-Sepharim. I might speak in this place of the universality of religion, for never yet was there a people who did not have some confused notion of a Deity, though accompanied by lamentable ceremonies and superstitions. Besides, the religions of all nations have always pretended to extraordinary powers, even to the performance of miracles and the healing of all diseases, and this by some secret means not known to the common man: and, truly, if we examine all religions, whether false or true, we shall not find one that does not pretend to something that is mystical. Certainly, if men are not determined against reason, they must grant these deviations in the matters of faith proceeded from the corruption of some principles received (as we see that heretics are but so many false interpreters), but notwithstanding, in those very errors there remained some marks and imitations of the first truth. Hence it comes to pass that all parties agree in the action but not in the object. For example, Israel sacrificed, and the heathen sacrificed, but the one to God and the other to his idol. Neither were they only conformable in some rites and solemnities of divinity, but the heathens also had some hints left of the Secret Learning and philosophy of the patriarchs, as we may see in their false Magic, which consisted, for the most part, in astronomical observations, images, charms, and characters. But it is my design to stay on the main road, not to follow these deviations and misfortunes of the Art, which, nonetheless, do not lack the weight of argument, the existence of things being proved as well by their failure as by their success. To proceed then, I say that during the pilgrimage of the patriarchs, this knowledge was delivered by tradition from the father to his child, and indeed it could be no otherwise, for what was Israel in those days but a private family? Nevertheless, when God appointed them their possession, and that this private house was multiplied to a nation, then these secrets remained with the elders of the tribes, as they did formerly with the father of the family. Now, that Moses was acquainted with all the abstruse operations and principles of Nature is a truth, I suppose, which no man will resist. That the Sanhedrim also participated in the same instruction and knowledge with him is plain from Scripture, where we read <margin>Num. 11:25.</margin> that “God took of the spirit that was in Moses, and gave it to the seventy.”

But, lest any man should deny that which we take for granted—namely, the philosophy of Moses—I shall demonstrate from his own books, both by reason as also by his practice, that he was a <term>Natural Magician</term>.

First of all then, it is most absurd, and therefore improbable, that he who was in no way skilled in the secrets of God and Nature should write of the creation, for both of these must of necessity be known before we should undertake to write of the creation. But Moses did write of it, therefore—. Now, I desire to know what he has written—truth or lie. If truth, how dare you deny his knowledge? If a lie (which God forbid), why will you believe him? You will tell me perhaps he has done it only in general terms, and I could tell you that Aristotle has done no otherwise; but do you think in good earnest that he knew no more than what he wrote? There is nothing you can say on this point but we can disprove it, for in Genesis he has discovered many, and especially those, secrets which have the most relation to this Art. For instance, he has discovered the *minera* of man, or that substance out of which man and all his fellow-creatures were made. This is the <term>First Matter</term> of the <term>Philosophers’ Stone</term>; Moses calls it sometimes water, sometimes Earth, for, in a certain place, I read thus: <margin>Gen. 1:20.</margin> “And God said, Let the waters bring forth abundantly the moving creature that has life, and fowl that may fly above the earth in the open firmament.” But elsewhere we read otherwise: <margin>Gen. 2:19.</margin> “And out of the ground the Lord God formed every beast of the field and every fowl of the air.” In this latter text he tells us that God made every fowl of the air out of the ground, but in the former it is written he made them out of the water. Certainly, Aristotle and his organ can never reconcile these two places, but a little skill in Magic will make them kiss and be friends without a philtre. This substance then is both earth and water, yet neither of them in their common complexions, but it is a thick water and a subtle earth. In plain terms it is a slimy, spermatic, viscous mass, impregnated with all powers celestial and terrestrial. Did they not write as Moses did? This is the true Damascene Earth <note>The red earth or clay from which Adam was said to be created.</note>, out of which God made man; you then who would be chemists, do not seem to be wiser than God, but use that subject in your Art which God himself makes use of in Nature. He is the best workman and knows what matter is most fit for his work; he who will imitate him in the effect must first imitate him in the subject. Talk not then of flint stones and antimony; they are the poet’s pin-dust and eggshells; seek this earth, this water. But this is not all that Moses has written for this purpose; I could cite many more magical and mystical passages, but in doing so I would be too revealing; therefore, I must forbear.

I shall now speak of his practice, and, truly, this is what no distinction, nor any other logical quibble, can set aside; nothing but experience can repel this argument, and it runs thus: “And Moses took the calf which they had made, and ground it to powder, and strewed it upon the water, and made the children of Israel drink of it.” Certainly, here was a strange kind of spice, and an art as strange as the spice itself. This calf was pure gold, the Israelites having contributed their earrings to its making. Now, I would gladly know by what means a body as solid and heavy as gold may be brought to such a light powder that it may be sprinkled on the surface of the water and afterwards drunk. I am sure here was *Aurum potabile* <note>Drinkable gold, a legendary alchemical medicine.</note>, and Moses could never have brought the calf to this state had he not "plowed with an heifer" <note>A biblical idiom from Judges 14:18 meaning to solve a riddle using a secret source or help.</note>. But enough of this; if any man thinks he did it by common fire, let him also do the same, and when he has performed it, he may sell his powder to the apothecaries. If I should dwell in this place on the Mosaic ceremonial law, with its several venerable shadows and their significations, I might lose myself in a wilderness of mysteries both divine and natural; for, truly, that whole system is but one vast screen, or a certain majestic shadow drawn over two worlds, visible and invisible. But these are matters of a higher speculation than the scope of our present discourse will allow.

people in both parts, spiritual and literal, therefore the Lawgiver instituted the Sanhedrim, a council of seventy elders, upon whom he had poured his spirit, so that they might discern (as Esdras did) the “deep things of the night,” in plain terms, the hidden things of his Law. From these elders the Cabala (I believe) had its origin; for they imparted their knowledge by word of mouth to their successors, and hence it came to pass that the science itself was styled Cabala, that is, a "Reception." This continued as long as Israel held together, but when their framework began to disintegrate, and the ruin of that house proved desperate, then Esdras—a prophet incomparable (notwithstanding the label of Apocrypha)—wrote that Law on tablets of boxwood which God himself had once written on tablets of stone. As for the more secret and mysterious part of it, it was written at the same time in seventy secret books, according to the number of Elders in whose hearts it had once been written.

And this was the very first time the Spirit married the Letter, for these sacraments were not previously trusted to corruptible volumes, but to the eternal tablets of the soul. But perhaps there is a blind generation who will believe nothing but what they see right in front of them, and therefore will deny that Esdras composed any such books. To these owls (though an unequal match) I shall oppose the honor of Picus <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, 1463–1494, a prominent Renaissance philosopher.</note>, who himself affirms that in his time he found the Secret Books of Esdras and bought them at a great price. Nor was this all, for Eugenius, Bishop of Rome, ordered their translation; but he died, and the translators also "fell asleep" <note>Metaphor for dying or ceasing their work.</note>. It is true indeed that something may be objected to me here concerning the Cabala—an art which I in no way approve of, yet neither do I condemn it (as our adversaries condemn Magic) before I understand it; for I have spent several years in the search and contemplation of it. But why then should I propose that as a truth to others which I account as an error myself? To this I answer, that I do not condemn the true Cabala, but rather the inventions of some dispersed, wandering rabbis, whose brains were more distracted than their fortunes. Of this "thirteenth tribe," I understand the satirist <note>Referring to the Roman poet Juvenal, who wrote on the Jews in his Satires.</note> when he promises so largely—

> “Whatever dreams you want, the Jews will sell.”

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of the Cabala, moving from its historical preservation by the Sanhedrim and Esdras to a critique of common Rabbinical understanding and an explanation of its physical principles.</meta>

# MAGIA ADAMICA ; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

embrace them as so many sacred truths; but, truly, those truths were unknown to most of those rabbis whom I have seen, even to Rambam himself—I mean Rabbi Moses Egyptius <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher.</note>—whom the Jews have so magnified with their famous hyperbole: "From Moses until Moses there has not arisen one who is like Moses."

But, to deal honestly with my readers, I say the Cabala I admit of consists of two parts: the Name and the Thing. The former part is merely symbolic in reference to the latter, serving only as the shadow to the substance. I will give you some instances. The literal Cabala, which is but a veil cast over the secrets of the physical, has Three Principles, commonly styled *Tres Matres*, or the Three Mothers. In the masculine form the Jews call them אמש, *Emes*; in the feminine אמש, *Asam*; and they are א, *aleph*, מ, *mem*, and ש, *schin*. Now I will show you how the physical Cabala explains the literal. *Tres Matres* אמש, *Emes* (says the great Abraham, or as some think, Rabbi Akiba), *id est, Aer, Aqua, et Ignis; Aqua quieta, Ignis sibilans, Aer spiritus medius.* That is,

> "The Three Mothers, *Emes*, or Aleph, Mem, and Schin, are Air, Water, and Fire; a still Water (mark that), a hissing Fire, and Air, the middle spirit."

Again, says the same rabbi: *Tres Matres* אמש, *Emes in Mundo, Aer, Aqua, et Ignis. Coeli ex Igne creati sunt, Terra ex Aquis, Aer egressus est ex spiritu qui stat medius.*

> "The Three Mother *Emes* in this world are Air, Water, and Fire. The Heavens were made of the Fire, the Earth was made of the Water (mark well this Cabalism), and the Air proceeded from a middle spirit."

Now, when the Cabalist speaks of the generation of the Three Mothers, he brings in Ten Secret Principles, which, I think, ten men have not understood since the Sanhedrim; such nonsense do I find in most authors when they undertake to discuss them. The First Principle is a Spirit which sits *in retrocesso suo fontano*, "in his primitive, incomprehensible retreats," like water in its subterranean channel before it springs. The Second Principle is the Voice of that First Spirit; this breaks forth like a well-spring, where the water flows out of the earth and is discovered to the eye. They call it "Spirit from Spirit." The Third Principle is "Spirit from Spirits," a Spirit which proceeds both from the First Spirit and from his Voice. The Fourth Principle is "Water from Spirit," a certain Water which proceeded from the Third Spirit, and out of that Water went Air and Fire. But God forbid that I should speak any more of them publicly; it is enough that we know the origin of the creature, and to whom we ought to ascribe it. The Cabalist, when he would tell us what God did with

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical and metaphysical Cabala, specifically how the "Three Mothers" (the elements) relate to the proportions of nature and how Jacob’s Ladder represents the connection between the human and the divine.</meta>

# MAGIA ADAMICA ; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

regarding the Three Mothers, he uses no other phrase than this: *Ponderavit Aleph cum omnibus, et omnia cum Aleph, et sic de singulis.* "He weighed (he says) Aleph with all, and all with Aleph, and so he did with the other Mothers." This is very clear if you consider the various mixtures of the elements and their secret proportions. And so much for the physical part of the Cabala; I will now show you the metaphysical.

It is strange to consider what unity of spirit and doctrine there is among all the Children of Wisdom. This proves infallibly that there is a Universal Schoolmaster who is present with all flesh, and whose principles are always uniform—namely, the Spirit of God. The Cabalists agree with all the world of Magicians that man, in spiritual mysteries, is both agent and patient. This is plain, for Jacob’s ladder is the greatest mystery in the Cabala. Here we find two extremes—Jacob is one, at the foot of the ladder, and God is the other, who stands above it, *emittens* (says the Jew) *Formas et Influxus in Jacob, sive Subjectum Hominem*—"shedding some secret influx of Spirit upon Jacob, who, in this place, typifies man in general." 

The rounds or steps in the ladder signify the Middle Nature, by which Jacob is united to God, and inferiors are united to superiors. As for the angels, of whom it is said they ascended and descended by the ladder, their motion proves they were not of the superior hierarchy but some other secret essences; for they ascended first and descended afterward, but if they had been from above, they would have descended first, which is contrary to the text. And here, reader, I would have you study. 

Now to return to Jacob: it is written of him that he was asleep, but this is a mystical speech, for it signifies death—namely, that death which the Cabalist calls *Mors Osculi*, or the Death of the Kiss, of which I must not speak one syllable. To be short, they agree with us "in the arcanum of theology"—that no word is efficacious in Magic unless it be first quickened by the Word of God. This appears from their *Semhamphores* <note>Also known as the Shem HaMephorash, the explicit or divided name of God.</note>, for they hold that the names of the angels are not effectual unless some name of God, such as יה or אל, be united to them; then (they say) in the power and virtue of those names they may work. An example of this we have in all extracted names, such as Vehu-Jah, Elem-Jah, Jeli-El, Sita-El. 

Now, this practice in the letter was a most subtle administration of the conjunction of the Substantial Word or Spirit with the Water. See that you understand me rightly, for I mean with the elements; and so much for the truth. To conclude, I would have the reader observe that the false, grammatical Cabala consists only in rotations of the alphabet and a metathesis of letters in the text, by which means the Scrip-

# MAGIA ADAMICA ; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

...scripture has suffered many racks and excoriations. As for the true Cabala, it uses the letter only as a device, by which to obscure and hide its physical secrets, just as the Egyptians previously used their hieroglyphics. In this sense, the original professors of this Art had a literal Cabala, as appears by that wonderful and most ancient inscription in the rock at Mount Horeb. It contains a prophecy of the Virgin Mother and her Son, Jesus Christ, engraved in hieroglyphics and formed by a combination of Hebrew letters—though only God knows by whom; it may be by Moses or Elijah. This is most certain; it can be seen there to this day, and we have for it the testimony of Thomas Obecinus, a most learned Franciscan, and Petrus a Valle, a gentleman, both of whom traveled to those parts. Now, that the learning of the Jews—I mean their Cabala—was chemical and resulted in true physical operations, cannot be better proved than by the *Book of Abraham the Jew*, in which he laid down the secrets of this Art in fairly plain terms and figures for the benefit of his unfortunate countrymen when, by the wrath of God, they were scattered all over the world. This book was accidentally found by Nicholas Flamel, a Frenchman, and with its help, he finally attained that miraculous medicine which men call the Philosophers’ Stone. But let us hear the *Monsieur* himself describe it.

> "There fell into my hands (he says), for the sum of two florins, a gilded book, very old and large. It was not made of paper or parchment, as other books are, but of delicate rinds (as it seemed to me) of tender young trees. Its cover was of brass, well bound, and entirely engraved with letters or strange figures; for my part, I think they might well have been Greek characters or some such ancient language. I am sure I could not read them, and I know well they were not notes or letters of Latin or of Gaul, for I understood those a little. As for what was inside, the bark leaves were engraved and written with admirable diligence using an iron point, in fair and neat colored Latin letters. It contained three times seven leaves—for so the leaves were numbered at the top—and every seventh leaf was always without any writing. Instead, upon the first seventh leaf, there was a painting of a Virgin and serpents swallowing her up; on the second seventh, a cross where a serpent was crucified; and on the last seventh, there were painted deserts or wildernesses, in the midst of which ran many fair fountains from which issued a number of serpents that ran up and down here and there. Upon the first of the leaves was written in great capital letters of gold—"

# ABRAHAM THE JEW, PRINCE, PRIEST, LEVITE, ASTROLOGER, AND PHILOSOPHER, TO THE NATION OF THE JEWS, DISPERSED BY THE WRATH OF GOD AMONG THE GAULS, SENDS GREETINGS.

After this, it was filled with great execrations and curses (with the word *Maranatha*, which was often repeated there) against every person who should cast his eyes upon it if he were not a sacrificer or a scribe. He who sold me this book knew not what it was worth, no more than I when I bought it. I believe it had been stolen, or taken by violence, from the miserable Jews, or found hidden in some part of their ancient dwelling place. Within the book, on the second leaf, he comforted his nation, counseling them to flee vices and, above all, idolatry, while awaiting with sweet patience the coming of the Messiah, who should vanquish all the kings of the earth and reign with his people in glory eternally. Undoubtedly, this had been a wise and understanding man. On the third leaf, and in all the other writings that followed, to help his captive nation pay their taxes to the Roman Emperors and to do other things which I will not speak of, he taught them in plain words the transmutation of metals. He painted the vessels on the sides and informed them of the colors and of all the rest, except the <term>First Agent</term>, of which he spoke not a word; but only (as he said) on the fourth and fifth leaves had he entirely painted and depicted it with very great skill and workmanship. For though it was well and intelligibly depicted and painted, yet no man could ever have been able to understand it without being well skilled in their <term>Cabala</term>, which is passed down by tradition, and without having well studied their books. 

The fourth and fifth leaves, therefore, were without any writing, but full of fair figures, shining or, as it were, illuminated, for the work was very exquisite. First, he painted a young man with wings on his ankles, having in his hand a caduceus rod, wreathed with two serpents, with which he struck a helmet that covered his head; he seemed, in my small judgment, to be Mercury, the pagan God. Against him came running and flying with open wings a great old man, who had an hourglass fastened upon his head and in his hands a hook or scythe, like Death, with which, in a terrible and furious manner, he would have cut off the feet of Mercury. On the other side of the fourth leaf, he painted a fair flower on the top of a very high mountain, which was severely shaken by the North winds; it had a blue root, white and red flowers, and leaves shining like fine gold; and round about it, the dragons and griffins of the North made their nests. The blood of these infants was afterwards gathered up by other soldiers and put into a large vessel, to which the Sun and the Moon came to bathe themselves. And thus you see what was in the first five leaves. I will not describe to you what was written in good and intelligible Latin on all the other written leaves, for God would punish me because I would be committing a greater wickedness than he who (as it is said) wished that all the men in the world had but one head, so that he might cut it off at a single blow.”

<margin>Luke 11:52.</margin>
I could now move from *Moses* to *Christ*, from the Old Testament to the New—not that I would interpret it myself, but I ask for the understanding of the enlightened. I desire to know what my Savior means by the key of knowledge, which the lawyers (as he tells me and them as well) had taken away. Undoubtedly it cannot signify the Law itself, for that was not taken away, being read in the synagogue every *Sabbath*. But to let this go: I am certain, and I could prove it all along from his birth to his passion, that the doctrine of *Jesus Christ* is not only consistent with the Laws of Nature, but is verified and established by them. When I speak of the Laws of Nature, I do not mean her excessive, irregular appetites and inclinations to which she has been subject since her corruption; for even *Galen* viewed those deviations as diseases, but studied Nature herself as their cure. We know by experience that too much of anything weakens and destroys our Nature; but if we live temperately and according to law, we are well, because our life accords with Nature. Hence, diet is a primary rule in medicine, far better indeed than the pharmacopoeia, for those messy recipes do nothing but oppress the stomach, being no fit fuel for a celestial fire. Believe it then, these excessive, beastly appetites proceeded from our fall; for Nature of herself is no lavish, insatiable glutton, but a most fine and delicate essence. This

<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s transition from the mystical alchemy of Nicholas Flamel to a theological and philosophical discussion on the inherent harmony between the laws of nature and the teachings of the Gospel, specifically referencing archaeological evidence from China.</meta>

# MAGIA ADAMICA; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

It appears by those fits and pangs she is subject to whenever she is overcharged. In common, customary excesses, there is no one who does not know this truth by experience; indeed, in spiritual sins, the body is not immediately troubled, but the conscience is terrified, and surely the body cannot be very well when the soul itself is sick. We see then that corruption and sin do not so much agree with us as they disturb us; for in what sense can our enemies be our friends, or those things which destroy nature be agreeable to nature? How then shall we judge the Gospel? Shall we say that the preservation of man is contrary to man, and that the doctrine of life does not agree with life itself? God forbid: The laws of the resurrection are founded upon those of the Creation, and those of regeneration upon those of generation; for in all these, God works upon one and the same matter by one and the same spirit. Now that this is so—I mean that there is a *harmony* between *nature* and the *Gospel*—I will prove from the *Chinese* monument of *Kim Cim*, a priest of *Judea*.<note>Note 16.</note> In the year of redemption 1625, there was dug up in a village of *China* called *Sanxuen* a square stone, being nearly ten hand-breadths long and five broad. On the uppermost part of this stone, a cross was figured, and underneath it an inscription in *Chinese* characters, being the title of the monument, which I find thus rendered in *Latin*:

->*Lapis in Laudem et memoriam æternam*<-
->*Legis Lucis, et veritatis portatæ*<-
->*de Judæa, et in China*<-
->*promulgatæ,*<-
->*Erectus.*<-

That is: A stone erected to the praise and eternal remembrance of the Law of Light and Truth, brought out of *Judea* and published in *China*. After this followed the body of the monument, being an account of how the Gospel of Jesus Christ was brought by one *Olo puen* out of *Judea* and afterwards, by the assistance of God, planted in *China*. This happened in the year of our Lord 636. *Kim Cim*, the author of this history, in the very beginning of it speaks mysteriously of the Creation. Then he mentions 365 sorts of sectarians who succeeded one another, all of them striving to see who could get the most proselytes. He recites some of their vain opinions, which indeed are very consistent with the rudiments and vagaries of the heathen philosophers. Lastly, he describes the professors of Christianity, along with their way of life and the excellence of their law. *It is difficult (he says) to give it a name con-*

*...to find a fit name for it, since its effect is to illuminate and fill all things with clarity; therefore it was necessary to call it* Kim ki ao, *that is, the Great Law of Light.* That is: “It is a hard matter to find a fit name for their law, seeing the effect of it is to illuminate and fill all with knowledge; it was necessary, therefore, to call it *Kim ki ao*, that is, the Great Law of Light.” To be short, Olo puen was admitted to the court by Tai cum veu huamti, king of China; here his doctrine was thoroughly searched, examined, and sifted by the king himself, who, having found it most true and solid, caused it to be proclaimed through his dominions. Now, upon what this doctrine was founded, and what estimate the king had both of it and its professors, we may easily gather from the words of his proclamation. First, then, where he mentions Olo puen, he calls him *Magnæ virtutis Hominem*, “a man of great virtue or power;” it seems he did something more than talk idly and preach; he could confirm his doctrine, as the Apostles did theirs, not with words only, but with works. Secondly, the proclamation speaking of his doctrine runs thus: *Cujus intentum docendi nos a fundamentis examinantes, invenimus doctrinam ejus admodum excellentum, et sine strepitu exteriori, fundatam principaliter in creatione mundi*: That is, “the purpose of whose teaching we have examined from the very fundamentals; we find his doctrine very excellent, without any worldly noise, and principally grounded on the creation of the world.” And again in the same place: *Doctrina ejus non est multorum verborum, nec superficie tenus suam fundat veritatem*: “His doctrine consists of but few words, not full of noise and notions, neither does he build his truth on superficial probabilities.” Thus we see the Incarnation and birth of Jesus Christ (which to the common philosopher are fables and impossibilities, but in the Book of Nature are plain, evident truths) were proved and demonstrated by the primitive Apostles and teachers out of the creation of the world. But instead of such teachers, we have in these our days two widespread goblins: a schoolman and a supposed saint. The one swells with a syllogistic pride; the other wears a broad face of revelation. The first cannot tell me why grass is green; the second, with all his devotion, does not know his A.B.C.s, yet he pretends to possess that infinite Spirit which knows all in all; and truly, of them both, this last is the worst. Surely the Devil has been very busy to put out the candle, for had all written truths been extant, this false learning and hypocrisy could never have prevailed. Kim Cim mentions twenty-seven books which Jesus Christ left on earth to further the conversion of the world. It may be that we do not have even one of them; for though the books of the New Testament are exactly that many, yet being all written,

# MAGIA ADAMICA; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

...at least some of them a long time after Christ, they may not well pass for those Scriptures which this author attributes to our Savior, even at the time of his ascension. Why should I speak of those many books cited in the Old Testament, but nowhere to be found, which if they were now extant, no doubt they would prove to be so many reverend, invincible patrons of magic. But ink and paper will perish, for the hand of man has made nothing eternal. The Truth alone is incorruptible, and when the letter fails, she shifts that body and lives in the spirit.

I have now, not without some labor, traced this science from the very Fall of Man to the day of his redemption—a long and solitary pilgrimage, the paths being unfrequented because of the briars and difficulties of antiquity, and in some places overgrown with the poppy of Oblivion. I will not deny that in the shadows and ivy of this wilderness, there are some birds of night, owls and bats, of a different feather from our phoenix; I mean some conjurers whose dark, indirect affection for the name of magic made them invent traditions more prodigious than their practices. These I have purposely avoided, lest they should embitter my stream with wormwood, and I seduce the reader through all these groves and solitudes to the waters of Marah. The next stage I must move to is that from which I first came out with the Israelites, namely Egypt; here, if books fail me, the stones will cry out. Magic having been so enthroned in this place, it seems she would be buried here also; she hid so many monuments in this earth, which have since been dug up and serve now to prove that she was once above ground. To begin then, I will first speak of Egyptian theology, so that you may see how far they have advanced, having no leader but the light of Nature. Trismegistus is so orthodox and plain regarding the Mystery of the Trinity that the Scripture itself does not exceed him; but since he is a specific author, and perhaps one who knew more than those of his order in general, I shall at this time dispense with his authority. Their universal doctrine, and that in which I find them all to agree, is this: Emepht, by which they express their supreme God (and truly they mean the true one), signifies properly an intelligence or spirit converting all things into himself, and himself into all things. This is very sound divinity and philosophy, if it is rightly understood. Now, they say that Emepht produced an egg out of his mouth, a tradition which Kircher explains imperfectly and, moreover, erroneously. In the production of this egg, another deity was manifested, whom they call Phtha, and out of some other

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of Egyptian theology, specifically detailing the hieroglyphic representation of the divine creation process and the nature of the primal chaos.</meta>

# MAGIA ADAMICA; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

...natures and substances enclosed in the egg, this Phtha formed all things. But to deal a little more openly, we will describe to you their hieroglyphic, in which they have very cleverly but obscurely discovered most of their mysteries. First of all then, they draw a circle, and in the circle a serpent, not coiled, but stretched out across the diameter; her head resembles that of a hawk, the tail is tied in a small knot, and a little below the head her wings are flying. The circle points to Emepht, or God the Father, who is infinite, without beginning and without end. Moreover, it comprehends or contains within itself the second deity, Phtha, and the egg or chaos out of which all things were made. The hawk in Egyptian symbols signifies light and spirit; his head, joined here to the serpent, represents Phtha, or the Second Person, who is the first light, as we have told you in our *Anthroposophia* <note>Refers to the author's work *Anthroposophia Theomagica*</note>. He is said to form all things out of the egg because in him, as if in a mirror, are types or images—namely, the distinct conceptions of the Father Deity—according to which the creatures are formed by the cooperation of the Spirit, namely the Holy Ghost. The lower part of this figure signifies the matter or chaos, which they call the egg of Emepht. So that you may better understand it, we will teach you something uncommon. The body of the serpent tells you it is a fiery substance, for a serpent is full of heat and fire, which made the Egyptians consider him divine. This is shown by his quick motion without feet or fins, much like the beating of a pulse, for his impetuous, hot spirit shoots him forward like a firework. There is also another analogy, for the serpent renews his youth (so strong is his natural heat) and casts off his old skin. Truly, matter is like a serpent, for she renews herself in a thousand ways and is never a permanent tenant of the same form. The wings tell you this subject or chaos is volatile, and in its outward appearance airy and watery. But to teach you the most secret resemblance of this hieroglyphic: the chaos is a certain creeping substance, for it moves like a serpent *sine pedibus* <note>Latin: without feet</note>, and truly Moses calls it not water, but *serpitura aquæ*, "the creeping of water," or a water that creeps. Lastly, the knot on the tail tells you this matter is of a very strong composition, and that the elements are fast bound within it—all of which the philosophers know to be true by experience. As for the affinity of inferior things with superior ones, and their private, active love which manifests in certain secret mixtures of Heaven with matter, their opinion is this: In the vital fire of all things here below, the sun (they say) is King. In their secret water, the moon is Queen. In their pure air, the five lesser planets rule; and in their

central, hypostatical earth, the fixed stars. For these inferiors, according to their doctrine, are provinces or thrones of those superiors, where they sit regent and paramount. To speak plainly, Heaven itself was originally extracted from inferiors, yet not so entirely but that some portion of the heavenly natures remained still below, and are the very same in essence and substance with the separated stars and skies. Heaven here below differs not from that above except in her captivity, and that above differs not from this below except in her liberty. The one is imprisoned in the matter; the other is freed from the grossness and impurities of it; but they are both of one and the same nature so that they easily unite. And hence it is that the superior descends to the inferior to visit and comfort her in this sickly, infectious habitation. I could speak much more, but I am in haste; and even if I were at leisure, you cannot in reason expect that I should tell you all. I will, therefore, move past these general principles to tell you something that supports the Egyptian practice and proves them to be adept philosophers. The first monument I read of for this purpose is that of Synesius, a very learned, intelligent man. He found in the temple of Memphis <term>petrōas biblous</term>, "books of stone," and in those hard leaves these difficult instructions:

->Η' φύσις τὴν φύσιν νικᾶται<-
->Η' φύσις τὴν φύσιν κρατεῖ<-

That is, "One Nature delights in another; One Nature overcomes another; One Nature overrules another." These short lessons, though of no small consequence, are attributed to the great Hostanes. ->All that is above, is also beneath;<-

# MAGIA ADAMICA; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

...close to all this dedicatory inscription (I find it only in Coptic characters, but since our fonts lack those letters, I must give it to you in Greek).

->ΣΥΝΘΡΟΝΟΙΣ ΤΟΙΣ ΕΝ ΑΙΓΥΠΤΟΥ<-

And now, though I formerly suspended the authority of Trismegistus, I might, like the Italian, produce his weapons *Sfodrato* <note>Unsheathed or drawn</note>; but I love no skirmishes, and truth is so brave it needs no embellishment. *That which is above* (said Hermes) *is like that which is below, and that which is below is like that which is above.* This is his mystery, and it is great; the benefit that attends the attainment is no less: *you shall have the glory of the whole world*; "All the pomp and splendor of the world shall be yours." To this language, the dialect of Isias so echoes that these two—like Euphorbus and Pythagoras—might pass for the same person.

->Heaven above (said he), heaven below;<-
->Stars above, stars below:<-
->All that is above, all that is below.<-

And then follows a reward for the intelligent: *Take these things and be happy*; "Understand this, and you are fortunate." You have made yourself very happy. This is enough to prove that magic once flourished in Egypt, and no doubt they received the truth of it from the Hebrews, who lived among them for a period of four hundred and thirty years. This is plain; for their own native learning was mere sorcery and witchcraft, and this appears by the testimony of Moses, who tells us their magicians produced their miracles by enchantments. And why, I ask you, should this instruction seem impossible? For since Joseph was married to Asenath, daughter of Potipherah, Priest of On, some of the Egyptian priests—and likely those of his own alliance—might for that very relationship receive a better doctrine from him. But this is not all I could say of this nation and their secret learning, if I were disposed to be their Mercury. I believe there is no one who claims an interest in antiquity or philosophy but has seen that famous monument which Paul the Third bestowed on his Cardinal Petrus Bembus, and has ever since been called the Bembine Table. There is no doubt that the hieroglyphics contained within it, if they were all reduced to letters, would make a volume as extensive

But it is not my design to comment on Memphis; that would be to make bricks and look for the straw as well, as Egypt has no complete record except the world itself, over which her monuments are scattered. This place, then, was the pitcher to the fountain, for they received their mysteries directly from the Hebrews; but their doctrine, like their Nile, swelling above its private channel, eventually overran the universe. Iamblichus the divine, in that excellent discourse of his *De Mysteriis* [On the Mysteries], tells us that Pythagoras and Plato derived all their learning *ex Columnis Mercurii*—"from the pillars or hieroglyphic monuments of Trismegistus." But the ancient Orpheus, in his poem *De Verbo Sacro* [On the Holy Word], where he speaks of God, has these words:

->*Nemo Illum, nisi Chaldæo de sanguine quidam progenitus vidit.*<-

"No one," he says, "has ever seen God, except a certain man descended from Chaldean blood." Now this was Moses, of whom it is written that he spoke with God face to face, as one man speaks with another. After this, he gives us a short characterization or description of the Deity, not in the remote and abstract, but in reference to the incubation of His Spirit upon Nature. Lastly, he acquaints us with the origin of his doctrine, showing where it first came from, and truly he derives it from the source:

->*Priscorum nos hæc docuerunt Omnia Vates,*<-
->*Quæ binis tabulis Deus olim tradidit Illis.*<-

"The priests (or prophets) of the ancient fathers," he says, "taught us all these things, which God delivered to them long ago in two tables." Thanks be to that God who made a heathen speak so clearly. I need not tell you to whom these tables were delivered; Cavellero D'epistola <note>Literally "The Knight of the Letter"; likely a reference to a specific authority or an epithet for a biblical commentator.</note> can inform you. I cited this passage so that it might appear that although the philosophy of Greece generally came out of Egypt, some Greeks were also educated by the Jews, and this is proved by no insignificant testimonies. Aristobulus, who lived in the days of the Maccabees and was himself a Jew, writes to Ptolemy Philometor, King of Egypt, and affirms that the Pentateuch—the five books of Moses—was translated into Greek before the time of Alexander the Great, and that these books came into the hands of Pythagoras and Plato. Indeed, Numenius the Pythagorean calls Plato *Mosem Atticâ linguâ loquentem*, "Moses speaking in the Attic [Greek] dialect"; by which he did not mean a similarity of style, but a conformity of principles. There is a story from Clearchus the Peripatetic in his first book *De Somno* [On Sleep]; how true it is I do not know, but the substance of it is this: he introduces

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the source of ancient wisdom is divine and was transmitted through the Hebrews, asserting that even the most celebrated Greek philosophers—excluding Aristotle and his followers—derived their magical and chemical knowledge from this tradition.</meta>

### MAGIA ADAMICA; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC.

...his master Aristotle, who relates how he met with a very reverend and learned Jew with whom he had much discourse about things natural and divine; but his special confession is that he was much rectified by him in his opinion of the Deity. This perhaps might be, but certainly it was after he wrote the *Organon* and his other lame discourses that move by the logical crutch. Now if you will ask me, what Greek did ever profess any magical principles? To this I answer that if you exclude Aristotle and his ushers—who are born like the *insecta*, *ex putredine* ["insects, out of rot"], out of their master’s corruptions—Greece yielded no philosopher who was not, in some of his positions, magical. If any man challenges my demonstration in this, I now promise him my performance. To give you some particular instances: Hippocrates was altogether chemical, and this I could prove out of his own mouth, but at this time his works are not with me. Democritus, who lived in the same age with him, wrote his *φυσικὰ καὶ μυστικά*, that is, "Physical and Mystical Things"—in plain English, "Natural Secrets." To this mystical piece Synesius added the light of his comments and dedicated them to Dioscorus, priest of Serapis. Of this Democritus, Seneca reports in his *Epistles* that he knew a secret union of pebbles by which he turned them into emeralds. Theophrastus, a most ancient Greek author, in his book *De Lapidibus* [On Stones], mentions another mineral work of his own wherein he had written something of metals. It is true indeed that this discourse of his is lost; but notwithstanding, his opinion is on record, namely, that he referred the origin of metals to water. This is confirmed by his own words (*ὕδατος μέν τα μεταλλευόμενα κατά περ ἄργυρος καὶ χρύσος* [of water are those things that are mined, such as silver and gold]) as I find them cited by Picus in his book *De Auro* [On Gold]. But that the art of transmutation was in demand in his days, and is no late invention or imposture as some think, appears by the attempts and practice of that same age of Theophrastus; for he mentions one Callias, an Athenian, who, endeavoring to make gold, turned his materials into cinnabar. It would be an endless labor for me to recite all the particulars that Greece can afford in support of my present design. I will therefore close everything in this short summary: There is no wisdom in Nature but what proceeded from God, for He made Nature; He first found out and afterwards ordained the very ways and method of how to corrupt and how to generate. This, His own wisdom and knowledge, He communicated in some measure to the first man; from him his children received it, and they taught it to their posterity. But the Jews, having the spiritual birthright, this mystery was their inheritance, and they possessed it entirely, being the Anointed Nation.

<summary>The author argues that all true Greek philosophy, except for Aristotle's logical systems, is inherently magical and derived from divine wisdom first given to Adam and preserved by the Jews. He cites several ancient figures, including Democritus and Theophrastus, to prove that the study of alchemy and the transmutation of metals has ancient, respected origins.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's history of magical knowledge, tracing its transmission from the Jews to the Egyptians, Greeks, and Romans, before describing its survival and eventual resurgence in medieval and early modern Europe.</meta>

# MAGIA ADAMICA ; OR, THE ANTIQUITY OF MAGIC. 123

...upon whom God had poured forth his Spirit. By the tradition of the Jews, the Egyptians came to be instructed. From the Egyptians these secrets descended to the Greeks, and from the Greeks (as we all know) the Romans received their learning, and among other common arts, this magical, mysterious one. This is confirmed by some proper, genuine effects and monuments of it, namely that flexible, malleable glass produced in the days of Tiberius, and the miraculous Olybian Lamp. But these times in which I am now, and those through which I have passed, are like some tempestuous day; they have more clouds than light. I will therefore enter Christendom, and here I shall find the Art in her infancy: True indeed, the cradle is only in some private hands; few know where, and many believe there is no such thing. The schoolmen are loud in their noise and condemn everything except what they themselves profess. It is Aristotle’s <gloss>marketplace</gloss>; they put his errors up for sale, and this continues for a long time. But everything (as the Spaniard says) has its *Quando* <note>Its appointed time</note>; many years have passed, and now the child begins to lisp and peeps out in the pompous language of Arnold and Lully. I need not tell you how he has thrived since; just look at his following, for nowadays who does not claim to know magic—and that so masterfully, as if the rewards of the Art were in his power? I do not know of any opponents except some sickly Galenists whose pale, tallow faces speak more of disease than of medicine. These indeed complain that their lives are too short and philosophy is too tedious, and so they fill their mouths with *Ars longa, vita brevis* <note>Art is long, life is short</note>. This is true (says the Spanish *Picaro*), for they cure either late or never, which makes their art long; but they kill quickly, which makes life short, and so the riddle is explained.

<summary>The author traces the lineage of magical secrets from the Jews and Egyptians through to the Romans and into Christendom, where the art survived in private hands despite the dominance of Aristotelian schoolmen. He notes the resurgence of magic in the works of Arnold of Villanova and Raymond Lully, contrasting true practitioners with the ineffective and sickly Galenist physicians.</summary>
