# CŒLUM TERRÆ;

## OR,

### THE MAGICIAN'S HEAVENLY CHAOS AND FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial letter "I" within an ornate square woodcut border featuring swirling floral and foliate patterns.</image-desc>

I HAVE now, reader, performed my promise, and according to my ability proved the antiquity of Magic. I am not such a fool as to expect a general agreement with my endeavors; every man's *placet* <note>approval or vote</note> is not the same as mine, but "the die is cast;" I have done this much, and he who would overthrow it must know, in the first place, that it is his task to do more. There is one point I can justly bind an adversary to: that he shall not oppose man to God, or heathen romances to Divine Scripture. He who would foil me must use the same weapons I do, for I have not fed my readers with straw, nor will I be refuted with stubble. In the next place, it is my design to speak somewhat of the Art itself, and this I shall do in rational terms—a form different from the ancients, for I will not stuff my discourse like a wilderness with lions and dragons. To common philosophers, that fault is very appropriate which Quintilian observed in some orators—*Operum fastigia spectantur, latent fundamenta*—"The spires of their Babel are in the clouds, its foundations nowhere; they talk indeed of fine things but tell us not upon what grounds." To avoid these flights in this, my *Olla* <note>literally a "pot" or "stew"; used here to mean a miscellany or medley</note> (for I care not much what I shall call it), observe this composition: first, I shall speak of that One Only Thing, which is the Subject of this Art and the Mother of all things. Secondly, I will discourse on that most admirable and more than natural Medicine which is generated out of this One Only Thing. Lastly, though with some disorder, I will reveal the means how and by which this Art works upon the

This is all, and here you must not consider how long or short I shall be, but how full the discovery; and truly it shall be such, and so much, that you cannot in modesty expect more. Now then, you who would be what the ancient physicians were—"the health-imparting hands of the gods"—not quacks and ointment-makers of the cook-pot; you who would perform what you publicly profess, and make your callings honest and conscientious, attend to the truth without resentment. Remember that prejudice is no religion and, consequently, has no reward. If this Art were damnable, you might safely study it nonetheless, for you have a precept to "prove all things," but to "hold fast that which is good." It is your duty not to fail yourselves, and for my part, so that I may fail no one, I begin thus.

Said the Cabalist, *Domus Sanctuarii quæ est hic inferiùs, disponitur secundum Domum Sanctuarii, quæ est superiùs*: "The Building of the Sanctuary which is here below is framed according to that of the Sanctuary which is above." Here we have two worlds, visible and invisible, and two universal natures, visible and invisible, from which both those worlds proceeded. The Passive Universal Nature was made in the image of the Active Universal One, and the conformity of both worlds, or sanctuaries, consists in the original conformity of their principles. There are many Platonists (and this last century has produced some apish disciples) who discourse very boldly on the similitude of inferiors and superiors; but if we thoroughly search their trash, it is a pack of small correspondences, namely, of the heliotrope and the sun, iron and the loadstone, the wound and the weapon. It is excellent sport to hear how they crow, being perched on these pitiful particulars, as if they knew the Universal Magnet which binds this great frame and moves all its members to a mutual compassion. This is a humor much like that of Don Quixote, who knew Dulcinea but never saw her. Those students, then, who would be better instructed must first know there is a Universal Agent, who, when He was disposed to create, had no other pattern or exemplar by which to frame and mold His creatures but Himself; but having infinite inward ideas or conceptions in Himself, as He conceived, so He created—that is to say, He created an outward form answerable to the inward conception or figure of His mind. In the second place, they ought to know there is a Universal Patient, and this Passive Nature was

This general patient is the immediate universal <note>The original text uses "Catholic" here in its literal sense of "universal" or "all-encompassing."</note> character of God himself in his unity and trinity. In plain terms, it is that substance which we commonly call the First Matter. But, truly, it is to no purpose to know this concept—matter—unless we know the thing itself to which the concept relates; we must see it, handle it, and by experimental ocular demonstrations know its very central, invisible essences and properties. But of these things hear the most excellent Capnion <note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a German humanist and scholar.</note>, who informs his Jew and his Epicure of two universal natures, material and spiritual. *Alteram* (he says) *quæ videri oculis, et attingi manu possit, prope ad omne momentum alterabilem. Detur enim venia* (*ut ait Madaurensis*) *novitati Verborum, rerum obscuritatibus inservienti. Hæc ipsa cum eadem et una persistere nequeat, nihilominus à tali virtute animi hospitio suscipitur, pro modo rectiùs quo est, quam quo non est, qualis in veritate res est, id est, mutabilis. Alteram autem substantiarum naturam incorruptam, immutabilem, constantem eandemque ac semper existentem.* The English of it says this: “One Nature is such that it may be seen with the eyes and felt with the hands, and it is subject to alteration almost in every moment. You must pardon (as Apuleius says) this strange expression, because it serves the obscurity of the thing. This very Nature, since she may not continue as one and the same, is, notwithstanding, apprehended by the mind under her qualification—more rightly as she is than as she is not—namely, as the thing itself is in truth, that is to say, changeable. The other Nature, or Principle of Substances, is incorruptible, immutable, constant, one and the same forever, and always existent.” 

Thus he says; now, this changeable Nature of which he speaks is the first visible, tangible substance that God ever made; it is white in appearance, and Paracelsus gives you the reason why. *Omnia* (he says) *in Dei manu alba sunt, is ea tingit ut Vult*—“All things when they first proceed from God are white, but he colors them afterward, according to his pleasure.” We have an example in this very Matter, which the philosophers call sometimes their *Red Magnesia*, sometimes their *White*, by which descriptions they have deceived many men; for in the first preparation the *Chaos* is blood-red, because the *Central Sulphur* is stirred up and discovered by the *philosophical fire*. In the second, it is exceedingly white and transparent, like the *Heavens*. It is in truth somewhat like common *quicksilver*, but of a celestial transcendent brightness, for there is nothing upon earth like it. This fine substance is the *Child of the Elements*, and it is a most pure, sweet *Virgin*, for nothing as yet has been generated out of her; but if at any time she breeds, it is by the *fire of Nature*, for that is her hus-

substance. She is no animal, no vegetable, no mineral; neither is she extracted out of animals, vegetables, or minerals, but she is pre-existent to them all, for she is the mother of them. Yet one thing I must say, she is not far from life, for she is almost animal. Her composition is miraculous and different from all other compounds whatsoever. Gold is not so compact, but every sophist concludes it is no simple [substance]; but she is so much a unity that no man believes she is anything more. She yields to nothing but love, for her end is generation, and that was never yet performed by violence. He that knows how to dally and play with her, the same shall receive all her treasures. First, she sheds at her nipples a thick, heavy water, as white as any snow—the philosophers call it **Virgin-Milk**. Secondly, she gives him blood from her very heart; it is a quick, heavenly fire; some improperly call it their **sulphur**. Thirdly, and lastly, she presents him with a secret **Crystal**, of more worth and luster than the white rock and all her rosials <note>The original term "rosials" likely refers to rose-colored gems or precious stones.</note>. This is she, and these are her favors. Catch her if you can.

To this characterization and these discoveries of my own, I shall add some more descriptions, as I find her portrayed and dressed by her other lovers. Some few (but such as know her very well) have written that she is not only one and three, but also four and five, and this truth is essential. The titles they have bestowed on her are diverse. They call her their **universal** <note>The original text uses "Catholic" here in its literal sense of "universal."</note> or **Magnesia**, and the **Sperm of the World**, out of which all natural things are generated. Her birth (say they) is singular, and not without a miracle; her complexion is heavenly and different from her parents. Her body also in some sense is incorruptible, and the common elements cannot destroy it, neither will she mix with them essentially. In the outward shape or figure, she resembles a stone, and yet is no stone, for they call her their **white gum**, and **water of their sea**, **water of life**, **most pure**, and **most blessed water**, and yet they do not mean water from the clouds, or rain-water, nor water from the well, nor dew: but a certain thick, permanent, saltish water—a water that is dry and does not wet the hand, a viscous, slimy water generated out of the saltish fatness of the earth. They call her also their twofold **Mercury**, and **Azoth** begotten by the influences of two globes, **celestial** and **terrestrial**. Moreover, they affirm her to be of that nature that no fire can destroy her, which of all other descriptions is most true, for she is fire herself, having in her a portion of the **universal fire** of nature, and a secret celestial spirit, which spirit is animated and quickened by God himself; for which reason they also call her their **most blessed stone**. Lastly, they say she is a middle nature between thick and thin, neither

This is enough: but so that I may speak something myself in plain terms, I say she is a true salt, but extremely soft, and somewhat thin and fluid; not as hard, nor as thick as common extracted salts, for she is none of them, nor any kind of salt whatsoever that man can make. She is a sperm that Nature herself draws out of the elements, without the help of art; man may find it where Nature leaves it. It is not his role to make the sperm, nor to extract it; it is already made, and lacks nothing but a matrix and suitable heat for generation. Now you should consider for yourselves where Nature leaves the seed, and yet many are so dull they know not how to work even when they are told what they must do. We see in animal generations, the sperm does not part from both the parents, for it remains with the female, where it is perfected. In the Great Work, though all the elements contribute to the composition of the sperm, yet the sperm does not part from all the elements, but remains with the earth, or with the water—though more immediately with the one than with the other.

Do not let your thoughts feed now on the phlegmatic, undigested vomits of Aristotle; look on the green, youthful, and flowery bosom of the earth; consider what a vast universal receptacle this element is. The stars and planets look down upon her, and though they may not descend here themselves, they shed down their golden locks, like so many bracelets and tokens of their love. The sun is perpetually busy, bringing his fire all around her, as if he would <term>sublime</term> something from her bosom and rob her of some secret, enclosed jewel. Is there anything lost since the Creation? Would you know his very bed and pillow? It is the earth. How many cities do you think have perished by the sword? How many by earthquakes? And how many by the deluge? You perhaps desire to know where they are at this present; believe it, they have one common sepulcher—what was once their mother is now their tomb. All things return to that place from which they came, and that very place is earth. If you but have leisure, run over the alphabet of Nature; examine every letter—I mean every particular creature in her book. What becomes of her grass, her grain, her herbs, her flowers? It is true that both man and beast use them, but this is only by the way, for they do not rest until they come to earth again. In this element they had their first station, and in this they will have their last. Think (if other vanities will give you leave) on all those generations that were before you, and anticipate all those that shall come after you. Where are all

# AND FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

I

...those beauties that past times have produced, and what will become of those that shall appear in future ages? They will all return to the same dust; they have one common house, and there is no family so numerous as that of the grave. Just look at the daily sports of Nature: her clouds and mists, the scene and pageantry of the air. Even these momentary things retreat to the closet of the earth. If the sun makes her dry, she can drink just as fast; what rises into clouds comes down as water. The earth swallows up all and, like that philosophical dragon, eats her own tail. The wise poets saw this, and in their mystical language called the earth Saturn, telling us as well that she fed on her own children. Truly, there is more truth in their stately verse than in Aristotle’s dull prose, for he was a blind beast, and malice made him so. 

But to proceed a little further with you, I wish you to digest what you read, to dwell a little upon the earth, and not to fly up immediately and admire the meteors of your own brains. The earth, as you know, in the wintertime is a dull, dark, dead thing—a contemptible, frozen, phlegmatic lump. But towards the Spring, and the warming of the sun, what rare pearls are there in this dunghill? What glorious colors and tinctures does she reveal? A pure eternal green overspreads her, and this is attended with innumerable other beauties: roses red and white, golden lilies, azure violets, the bleeding hyacinths, with their several celestial odors and spices. If you will take my advice, learn from where the earth has these invisible treasures—this annual flora, which does not appear without the complements of the sun. 

Behold, I will tell you as plainly as I may. There are in the world two extremes: matter and spirit. One of these, I can assure you, is earth. The influences of the spirit animate and quicken the matter, and in the material extreme, the seed of the spirit is to be found. In intermediate natures, such as fire, air, and water, this seed does not stay; for they are but dispensers, or media, which convey it from one extreme to the other—from the spirit to the matter, which is to the earth. 

But stay, my friend; this information has stirred you somewhat, and now you come on so furiously as if you would ransack the cabinet. Allow me to hold you back. I am not referring to this common, foul, impure earth; that does not fall within my discourse, except as it serves for your <term>manuduction</term>. That which I speak of is a mystery: it is a *cœlum terræ* and *terra cœli*—not this dirt and dust, but a most secret, celestial, invisible earth. <margin>Ch. 1</margin> Raymund Lully, in his compendium of Alchemy, calls the principles of magic art—*spiritus fugitivos in aere condensatos, in formâ monstrorum diversorum, et animalium etiam hominum, qui vadunt sicut nubes, modo hùc, modo*

<summary>The author describes the earth as the ultimate destination for celestial spirits, which animate matter to produce the beauties of nature. He distinguishes between common soil and a "celestial earth" that serves as the secret foundation for alchemical and magical work.</summary>
<keywords>First Matter, Celestial Earth, Spirit and Matter, Alchemy, Saturn, Aristotle, Raymund Lully, Nature's cycles, Ouroboros</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "first matter," comparing its restless, transformative nature to the movement of clouds and the constant creative activity of Nature.</meta>

# CŒLUM TERRÆ ; OR, THE MAGICIAN’S HEAVENLY CHAOS,

*Illùc.* "Certain fugitive spirits condensed in the air, in the shape of various monsters, beasts, and men, which move like clouds here and there." As for the meaning of our Spaniard, I refer it to his readers; let them make the most of it.

This is true: as the air, and all the volatile substances in it, are restless, even so it is with the first matter. The eye of man never saw her twice under one and the same shape; but as clouds driven by the wind are forced into this or that figure, but cannot possibly retain one constant form, so is she pursued by the fire of Nature. For this fire and this water are like two lovers: they no sooner meet, but presently they play and toy, and this game will not be over until some new baby is generated. I have often admired their subtle, perpetual motion, for at all times and in all places these two are busy, which occasioned that notable sentence of <term>Trismegistus</term>, that action was the life of God. 

But most excellent and authoritative is that oracle of <term>Marcus Antoninus</term>, who in his *Meditations* <note>Originally titled "To Himself"</note>, speaks indeed things worthy of himself. "The Nature (he says) of the universe delights not in anything so much as to alter all things and then to make the like again." This is her tick-tack <note>A transformation or rhythmic game, similar to backgammon</note>; she plays one game to begin another. The matter is placed before her like a piece of wax, and she shapes it into all forms and figures. Now she makes a bird, now a beast, now a flower, then a frog; and she is pleased with her own magical performances, as men are with their own fancies. 

Hence she is called by <term>Orpheus</term>, "the mother that makes many things, and ordains strange shapes or figures." Neither does she, as some sinful parents do, who having had their pleasure, do not care for their child; she loves them still after she has made them, has an eye over them all, and provides even for her sparrows. It is strange to consider that she works as well privately as publicly, not only in gardens where ladies may smell her perfumes, but in remote solitudes and deserts. The truth is, she seeks not to please others so much as herself; therefore many of her works, and those the choicest, never come to light. 

We see little children, who have newly come from under her hand, will be dabbling in dirt and water, and other idle sports liked by none but themselves. The reason is, they are not as yet captivated, which makes them seek their own pleasures; but when they come of age, then love or profit makes them square their actions according to other men's desires. Some cockney <note>A pampered or effeminate town-dweller</note> puts his entire income on his back, but his gallantry is spoiled if his mistress does not observe it. Another fights, but his victory is lost if it is not printed; it is the world that must hear of his 

<summary>The author describes the "first matter" as a restless substance constantly reshaped by Nature, comparing her creative process to a game or a mother’s care. He emphasizes that Nature works for her own pleasure, often creating beauty in solitude far from human observation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the creative and restless nature of the "first matter," focusing on how Nature imprints her internal ideas onto matter to create variety.</meta>

# AND FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

valour. Now <term>Nature</term> is a free spirit that seeks no applause; she observes no one more than herself, but is pleased with her own magic, as philosophers are with their <term>secret philosophy</term>. This is why we find her busy, not only in the flower pots on balconies, but in wildernesses and ruinous places, where no eyes observe her but the stars and planets. In a word, wherever the fire of nature finds the <term>Virgin Mercury</term>, there he has found his love, and there they will both set to their husbandry—a pleasure not subject to excess, for it always presents new varieties. It is reported of <term>Marc Antony</term>, a famous but unfortunate Roman, how he sent his agent across the world to copy all the handsome faces so that, among so many excellent features, he might select the most pleasing face for himself. Truly, <term>Nature</term> is much of this character, for she has infinite beautiful patterns within herself, and all these she would gladly see outside of herself; this she cannot do without the matter, for that is her mirror. This makes her generate perpetually and imprint her conception into the matter, communicating life to it and shaping it according to her imagination. By this practice, she places her fancy, or idea, beyond herself—or as the <term>Peripatetics</term> say, <term>*extra intellectum*</term> (outside the intellect), namely in the matter. But because the ideas are innumerable and also different, the pleasures of the agent are maintained by their variety, or to speak more properly, by the agent's own fruitfulness; for among all the beauties the world affords, no two are exactly the same. Much could be said here concerning beauty: what it is, where it came from, and how it may be defaced—not only in the outward figure, but in the inward idea—and lost forever in both worlds. But I am not at all acquainted with these pretty intricacies; I have no mysteries but <term>Nature</term>, and therefore I shall leave the fine ladies to fine lads and speak of my simple <term>Ælia Lælia</term> <note>A reference to the famous "Aelia Laelia Crispis" alchemical enigma.</note>.

I know some sophisters <note>Those who use fallacious arguments</note> of the <term>Heptarchy</term> <note>Likely referring to the academic establishment or the seven liberal arts</note>—I mean those whose learning is all noise, in which sense even <term>py-annets</term> <note>Magpies</note> and <term>paraquitoes</term> <note>Parakeets</note> are philosophical—will conclude this is all bait and poetry; that we are being pleasing, not factual, and that we cheat even the reader’s discretion. To prevent such weak slanders, and to shed a little more of our secret light upon the well-disposed student, I shall in this place produce the testimonies of some able philosophers concerning the **First Matter** itself, as it is naturally found before any alteration by art. And here, truly, the reader may discover the mark; it is most easily done if he will but watch the flights of my verse or follow the more serious pace of their prose. The first I shall cite is **Arnoldus de Villa Nova**, an absolutely perfect master of the Art; he describes the philosophical <term>Chaos</term> in these plain terms: *Lapis est et non lapis* <note>Latin: "It is a stone, and yet not a stone"</note>.

# AND THE FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

*Spiritus, Anima, et Corpus; Quem si dissolvis, dissolvitur, et si coagules coagulatur, et si volare facis, volat; est enim volatilis, albus ut lacryma oculi: postea efficitur citrinus, salsus, pilis carens: quem nemo suâ linguâ tangere potest. Ecce ipsum jam sua demonstravi descriptione non tamen nominavi. Modo volo ipsum nominare, et dico, quod si dixeris eum Aquam esse, verum dicis; et si dixeris eum Aquam non esse, mentiris. Ne igitur decipiaris pluribus descriptionibus et operationibus, unum enim quid est, cui nihil alieni infertur.* "It is (he says) a stone and no stone, Spirit, Soul, and Body; which if you dissolve, it will be dissolved, and if you coagulate it, it will be coagulated, and if you make it fly, it will fly, for it is volatile, or flying, and clear as a tear; afterwards it is made citrine, then saltish, but without shoots or crystals, and no man may touch it with his tongue. Behold, I have described it truly to you, but I have not named it! Now, I will name it, and I say that if you say it is Water, you say the truth, and if you say it is not Water, you lie. Be not, therefore, deceived with manifold descriptions and operations, for it is but one thing, to which nothing foreign may be added."

Thus Arnoldus, and he borrowed this from the *Turba*.

Let us now hear his disciple, Raymond Lully, who, speaking very enviously and obscurely of seven metallic principles, describes the third, wherein four of the seven are included, in these words: *Tertium* (he says) *est Aqua clara composita, et illa est res Argento vivo magis propinqua, quæ quidem reperitur supra terram currens et fluens. Et istud argentum vivum in omni Corpore Elementato à materiâ æris est proprie generatum, et ideo ipsius humiditas est valde ponderosa.*

That is: "the third Principle is a clear compounded water, and it is the substance nearest in complexion to <term>Quick-silver</term>, which is found running and flowing upon the earth. This <term>Quick-silver</term> is generated in every compound body from the substance of the air, and, therefore, its moisture is extremely heavy."

To these I will add Albertus Magnus, whose testimony <note>The original word 'suffrage' implies support or expert consensus</note> in this kind of learning is like the <term>stylanx</term> <note>A touchstone or standard for testing purity</note> to gold, for he had thoroughly searched it and knew very well what part of it would withstand the test. *Mercurius Sapientum* (he says) *est Elementum Aquam frigidum, et humidum, Aqua permanens, spiritus Corporis, vapor unctuosus, Aqua Benedicta, Aqua virtuosa, Aqua Sapientum, Acetum Philosophorum, Aqua Mineralis, Ros cœlestis gratiæ, Lac Virginis, Mercurius Corporalis, et aliis infinitis nominibus in Philosophorum libris nominatur, quæ quidem nomina quamvis varia sunt, semper tamen unam et eandem rem significant, utpote Solum Mercurium Sapientum. Ex ipso toto elicitur omnis virtus Artis Alchimiæ, et suo*

*modo Tinctura alba et rubea.* In plain English thus: “The Mercury of the Wise Men is a watery element, cold and moist. This is their permanent water, the spirit of the Body, the unctuous vapor, the Blessed Water, the virtuous water, the water of the Wise Men, the Philosopher’s Vinegar, the Mineral Water, the Dew of Heavenly Grace, the Virgin’s Milk, the Bodily Mercury, and it is named by countless other names in the books of the Philosophers. These names, though they are diverse, nonetheless always signify one and the same thing, namely, the Mercury of the Wise Men. Out of this Mercury alone all the Virtue of the Art is extracted, and, according to its Nature, the Tincture, both red and white.” To this agrees Rachaidibi the Persian: *Sperma Lapidis (says he) est frigidum et humidum in Manifesto, et in Occulto calidum et siccum.* “The Sperm, or first matter, of the Stone, is outwardly cold and moist, but inwardly hot and dry,” all of which is confirmed by Rhodian, another instructor (it seems) of Kalid, King of Persia. His words are these: *Sperma est album et liquidum, postea rubeum. Sperma istud est lapis fugitivus, et est aereum et volatile, et est frigidum et humidum, et calidum et siccum.* “The Sperm (says he) is white and liquid, afterwards red. This Sperm is the flying stone, and it is aerial and volatile, cold and moist, hot and dry.” To these subscribes the author of that excellent tract entitled *Liber Trium Verborum. Hic est Liber (says he) Trium Verborum, Liber Lapidis preciosi, qui est Corpus æreum et volatile, frigidum et humidum, aquosum et adustivum, et in eo est caliditas et siccitas, frigiditas et humiditas, alia virtus in occulto alia in manifesto.* “This is the Book of Three Words,” meaning thereby Three Principles, “the Book of the Precious Stone, which is a body aerial and volatile, cold and moist, watery and adustive; and in it is heat and dryness, coldness and moisture, one virtue inwardly, the other outwardly.” Belus, the philosopher, in that famous and most classic Synod of Arisleus, inverts the order to conceal the practice; but if rightly understood, he speaks to the purpose. *Excelsum (says he) est hoc apud Philosophos magnos Lapidem non esse lapidem, apud idiotas vile et incredibile. Lapis enim est hæc ipsa permanent aqua, et dum aqua est lapis non est.* “Amongst all great philosophers it is magisterial that our stone is no stone, but amongst the ignorant it is ridiculous and incredible. For who will believe that water can be made a stone, and a stone water, nothing being more different than these two? And yet in very truth it is so. For this very permanent water is the

But in this sense the ancient Hermes abounds, and almost reveals too much. *Scitote filii sapientum, quod priscorum philosophorum aquæ est divisio, quæ dividit ipsam in alia quatuor.* "Know (he says), you who are the children of the wise, the separation of the ancient philosophers was performed upon water, which separation divides the water into four other substances." There is extant a very learned author, who has written something to this purpose, and that more openly than any whom we have formerly cited. *Sicuti mundus originem debet aquæ, cui Spiritus Domini incubabat, rebus tàm cœlestibus, quàm terrestribus omnibus indè prodeuntibus; ita Limbus hic emergit ex aquâ non vulgari, neque ex Rore Cœlesti, aut ex aere condensato in cavernis terræ, vel in recipiente ipso, non ex abysso maris, fontibus, puteis, fluminibusquè hausta, sed ex aquâ quadam perpessâ, omnibus obviâ, paucissimis cognitâ, quæ in se habet, quæ cunque ad totius operis complementum sunt necessaria, omni amoto extrinseco.* "Just as the world (he says) was generated out of that water upon which the Spirit of God did move—all things proceeding from there, both celestial and terrestrial—so this chaos emerges out of a certain water that is not common. It is not made from dew, nor air condensed in the caverns of the earth or artificially in the receiver; not from water drawn from the sea, fountains, pits, or rivers, but from a certain tortured water that has suffered some alteration. It is obvious to all, but known to very few. This water has all in it that is necessary for the perfection of the work, without any extrinsic addition." I could produce a thousand more authors, but that would be tedious; I shall conclude with one of the Rose Brothers, whose testimony is equivalent to the best of these, but his instruction is far more excellent. His discourse on the first matter is somewhat large, and to avoid prolixity, I shall forgo the Latin, but I will give you his meaning in exact, plain English.

> "I am a Goddess (he says, speaking in the person of Nature), famous for beauty and extraction, born out of our own proper Sea, which encompasses the whole Earth and is ever restless. Out of my breasts I pour forth milk and blood; boil these two until they are turned into silver and gold. O most excellent subject! Out of which all things in this world are generated, though at first sight you are poison, adorned with the name of the flying eagle. You are the first matter, the seed of Divine blessing, in whose body there is heat and rain, which, nonetheless, are hidden from the wicked because of your habit and virgin vestures which are scattered over all the world. Your parents are the Sun and Moon; in you there is Water and Wine, gold also and silver upon earth, that mortal man...

<meta>This page continues the author’s alchemical discourse, transitioning from the description of the "tortured water" to an allegorical explanation of the process involving the symbolic Eagle and Lion and the five gates of the Great Work.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>

may rejoice. In this manner, God sends us his blessing and wisdom with rain, and the beams of the sun, to the eternal glory of his name. But consider, O man, what things God bestows upon you by this means. Torture the eagle until she weeps, and the lion is weakened and bleeds to death. The blood of this lion, incorporated with the tears of the eagle, is the treasure of the earth. These creatures are accustomed to devour and kill one another, but, nevertheless, their love is mutual, and they put on the property and nature of a salamander, which, if it remains in the fire without any harm, cures all the diseases of men, beasts, and metals. 

After the ancient philosophers had perfectly understood this subject, they diligently sought in this mystery for the center of the middlemost tree in the terrestrial paradise, entering in by five litigious gates. The first gate was the knowledge of the true matter, and here arose the first—and a most bitter—conflict. The second was the preparation by which this matter was to be prepared, so that they might obtain the embers of the eagle and the blood of the lion. At this gate there is a most sharp fight, for it produces water and blood, and a spiritual, bright body. The third gate is the fire, which leads to the maturity of the medicine. The fourth gate is that of multiplication and augmentation, in which proportions and weights are necessary. The fifth and last gate is projection. But most glorious, very rich and high, is he who attains to the fourth gate, for he has gained a universal medicine for all diseases. This is that great character of the book of Nature, out of which her whole alphabet arises. The fifth gate serves only for metals.* 

This mystery, existing from the foundation of the world and the creation of Adam, is of all others the most ancient: a knowledge which God Almighty, by his word, breathed into nature; a miraculous power; the blessed fire of life; the transparent carbuncle and red gold of the wise men; and the divine blessing of this life. But this mystery, because of the malice and wickedness of men, is given only to a few; nevertheless, it lives and moves every day in the sight of the whole world, as it appears by the following parable. 

I am a poisonous dragon, present everywhere, and to be had for nothing. My water and my fire dissolve and compound; out of my body you shall draw the green and the red lion. But if you do not exactly know me, you will with my fire destroy your five senses. A most pernicious, quick poison comes out of my nostrils, which has been the destruction of many. Separate, therefore, the thick from the thin skillfully, unless you delight in extreme poverty. I give you faculties both male and female and

...the powers both of Heaven and earth. The mysteries of my art are to be performed magnanimously <note>original: "magnanimously" — with greatness of spirit and generosity</note>, and with great courage, if you would have me overcome the violence of the fire, in which attempt many have lost both their labor and their wealth. I am the Egg of Nature <note>The "Egg of Nature" is an alchemical symbol for the *Prima Materia* or First Matter, containing the potential for all life and transformation.</note> known only to the wise—those who are pious and modest, who make of me a little world. I was ordained by the Almighty God for men, but (though many desire me) I am given only to a few, so that they may relieve the poor with my treasures and not set their minds on gold that perishes. 

I am called by the philosophers, Mercury; my husband is gold (philosophical gold <note>original: "gold (philosophicall)" — refers to the spiritual or essential quality of gold rather than the common metal.</note>). I am the old dragon that is present everywhere on the face of the earth; I am father and mother; youthful and ancient; weak and yet most strong; life and death; visible and invisible; hard and soft, descending to the earth and ascending to the heavens; most high and most low; light and heavy. In me, the order of Nature is oftentimes inverted in color, number, weights, and measure. I have in me the light of Nature; I am dark and bright; I spring from the earth, and I come out of Heaven; I am well known, and yet a mere nothing; all colors shine in me, and all metals by the beams of the sun. I am the Carbuncle of the Sun <note>The Carbuncle of the Sun is a metaphorical term for the Philosopher's Stone, described as a glowing, radiant red gemstone.</note>, a most noble purified earth, by which you may turn copper, iron, tin, and lead into most pure gold." *

Now, gentlemen, you may see which way the philosophers move; they commend their secret water, and I admire the tears of Hyanthe <note>Hyanthe is the name Thomas Vaughan (the likely author) gave to his wife, Rebecca, whom he viewed as a spiritual and philosophical partner.</note>. There is something in the imagination besides poetry, for my mistress is very philosophical, and in her love, a pure Platonic. But now that I think upon it, how many rivals shall I attract by this discourse? Every reader will fall in love, and some "fine thing" <note>likely referring to a dandy or a shallow suitor</note> may break her heart with nonsense. This love indeed would be mere luck; but for my part, I dare trust her. Lest any man should mistake her for some things formerly named, I will tell you truly what she is: She is not any known water whatsoever, but a secret, generative <note>original: "spermatic"</note> moisture, or rather, the Venus that yields that moisture. Therefore, do not imagine that she is any raw, phlegmatic, thin water, for she is a fat, thick, heavy, slimy humidity. But lest you should think I have grown jealous and would not trust you with my mistress, Arnold of Villanova shall speak for me; hear him:

"Furthermore, I say to you, that in no way have we been able to find—nor similarly have the philosophers been able to find—any thing persisting in the fire, except only an unctuous humidity <note>original: *Ampliùs tibi dico, quod nullo modo invenire potuimus, nec similiter invenire potuerunt philosophi, aliquam rem perseverantem in igne, nisi solam unctuosam humiditatem.*</note>. We see that watery humidity evaporates easily; the dry part remains, and therefore they are separated because they are not natural <note>original: *Aqueam humiditatem videmus de facili evaporare, arida remanet, et ideo separantur, quia non sunt naturales.*</note>. If, however, we consider those humidities which are with difficulty separated from those things which are natural, not..." <note>original: *Si autem eas humiditates consyderemus, quæ difficulter separantur ab his quæ sunt naturales, non*</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the "First Matter," moving from its personified monologue to technical alchemical descriptions of its "unctuous" or oily nature, and concluding with a stern warning about the spiritual danger of revealing these secrets too plainly.</meta>

-># CŒLUM TERRÆ; OR, THE MAGICIAN’S HEAVENLY CHAOS,<-

<note>original: "invenimus aliquas nisi unctuosas, et viscosas."</note> "...we have found nothing except those that are oily and sticky." "I tell you further," says he, "that we could not possibly find, nor could the philosophers find before us, anything that would persist in the fire, except for the oily moisture <note>original: "unctuous humiditie" — a vital alchemical concept referring to a non-volatile, oily substance that can survive intense heat without evaporating.</note>. We see that a watery moisture will easily evaporate away, leaving the earth behind and its parts separated, because their composition is not natural. But if we consider those moistures which are difficult to separate from those parts which are natural to them, we find no such thing except the oily, sticky moistures."

Perhaps some flint-and-antimony doctors <note>These are "vulgar" alchemists who attempt to break down minerals through physical force or harsh chemical reagents like antimony, rather than following the subtle path of nature.</note>, who perform their philosophical grinding <note>original: "contrition" — the act of grinding or reducing to powder.</note> with a hammer, will expect me to reveal this thing outright, and not allow this strange bird-lime <note>original: "bird-lime" — a sticky substance used to trap birds; here used metaphorically for the sticky "matter" that traps the pride of the ignorant.</note> to hold their pride by its feathers. To these people, I say: it is water of silver, which some have called "Water of the Moon," but it is "Mercury of the Sun," and partly of Saturn, for it is extracted from these three metals, and without them it can never be made. Now they may solve the riddle and tell me what it is, for it is the truth, if they are able to understand it.

To the ingenious and modest reader, I have something else to say in reply, and I believe it will sufficiently excuse me. Raymond Lully <note>original: "Raimund Lullie" — a famous 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and alchemist.</note>, a man who had reached the very center of Nature and without question understood a great part of the Divine will, gives me a most terrible charge not to prostitute these principles.

<margin>Theory, chapter 6.</margin>
<note>original: "Juro tibi supra animam meam, quod si ea reveles, damnatus es. Nam a Deo omne procedit bonum, et ei soli debetur. Quare servabis, et secretum tenebis illud, quod ei debetur revelandum, et affirmabis quam per rectam proprietatem subtrahis, quæ ejus honori debentur. Quia si revelares brevibus verbis illud quod longinquo tempore formavit, in die magni judicii condemnareris, tanquam qui perpetrator existens contra majestatem Dei læsam, nec tibi remitteretur casus læsæ majestatis. Talium enim revelatio ad Deum, et non ad alterum spectat."</note>
"I swear to you upon my soul," says he, "that you are damned if you should reveal these things. For every good thing proceeds from God, and to Him alone it is due. Therefore, you shall reserve and keep that secret which God alone should reveal, and you shall affirm that you justly withhold those things whose revelation belongs to His honor. For if you should reveal in a few words that which God has been forming over a long time, you would be condemned on the great Day of Judgment as a traitor to the majesty of God <note>original: "læsæ majestatis" — lese-majesty, or treason against a sovereign.</note>, nor would your treason be forgiven you. For the revelation of such things belongs to God, and not to man." So said the wise Raymond.

Now, for my part, I have always honored the magicians, their...

# AND THE FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

Philosophy is both rational and majestic, dwelling not upon mere ideas, but upon effects—those effects that confirm both the wisdom and power of the Creator. When I was a mere novice <note>original: "errant" — one who is wandering or inexperienced.</note> in their books and did not understand them, I believed them. Time rewarded my faith and repaid my curiosity with knowledge. In the meantime, I suffered many bitter slanders <note>original: "calumnies"</note> from certain envious adversaries who had nothing of the scholar about them but their gowns and a little specialized language to give vent to their nonsense. But these people could not discourage me; with a Spartan patience, I endured <note>original: "concocted" — here meaning to digest or stomach an insult.</note> my injuries and found at last that Nature was magical, not peripatetic <note>original: "peripateticall" — referring to the school of Aristotle, which dominated universities but which the author finds insufficient compared to "magical" or Hermetic study.</note>. I have no reason, then, to distrust those writers in spiritual things whom I have found so orthodox and faithful even in natural mysteries. I do believe Raymond <note>Referring to Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and reputed alchemist.</note>, and in accordance with that faith, I provide for my salvation. I will not reveal everything, so that I may not be condemned. But if this will not satisfy you, whoever you are, let me whisper a word in your ear, and afterward you may proclaim it from the rooftops. Do you know from whom, and how, that sperm or seed—which men, for want of a better name, call the "first matter"—proceeded? A certain illuminate <note>A person claiming special spiritual enlightenment.</note>, and in his days a member of that society <note>Likely a reference to the Rosicrucians.</note> which some pretentious fools <note>original: "painted buzzards"</note> are used to laughing at, writes thus: "God, most good and great, created something out of nothing; that something indeed became a certain one thing, in which all things—creatures both celestial and terrestrial—were contained." <note>original: "Deus optimus maximus ex nihilo aliquid creavit, illud aliquid vero fiebat unum aliquod, in quo omnia, creaturæ cœlestres et terestres."</note>

<margin>See Jacob Boehme <note>original: "Behmen"</note> in his most excellent and profound *Discourse of the Three Principles.*</margin>
"God," says he, "who is incomparably good and great, created something out of nothing; but that something was made into one thing, in which all things were contained—creatures both celestial and terrestrial." This first "something" was a certain kind of cloud or darkness, which was condensed into water, and this water is that "one thing" in which all things were contained. But my question is: what was that "Nothing" out of which the first cloudy chaos, or "something," was made? Can you tell me? It may be that you think it is a mere nothingness. It is indeed "nothing" as far as we are concerned <note>original: "nihil quò ad nos"</note>—nothing that we perfectly know. It is nothing, as Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher known for "Negative Theology."</note> says, "Nothing of those things which exist, and nothing of those things which do not exist." <note>original: "Nihil eorum quæ sunt, et nihil eorum quæ non sunt."</note> It is nothing that was created, or of those things that currently are; and it is nothing of that which you call nothingness—that is, of those things which do not exist in your empty, destructive sense. But, by your leave, it is the *true* thing, of whom we can affirm nothing; it is that transcendent essence whose theology is negative <note>Negative theology (Apophatic theology) attempts to describe God only by what He is not, as He is beyond human language and comprehension.</note> and was known to the primitive church, but is lost in these our days. <margin>See Dionysius the Areopagite, *Mystical Theology*, on Negation <note>original: "Dyonys: Ar. Th. Neg."</note>.</margin> This is that "Nothing" of Cornelius Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous polymath and occult writer.</note>, and in this nothing—when he was tired of human things, I mean human sciences—he did at last find rest. "To know nothing," he said, "is the happiest life." <note>original: "nihil scire est vita felicissima"</note> This is true indeed, for to know this divine "Nothing" is life eternal.

<summary>The author identifies the "first matter" of the universe as a substance derived from a divine, incomprehensible "Nothing," a concept he supports using the "negative theology" of Dionysius and the mystical writings of Jacob Boehme and Cornelius Agrippa.</summary>
<keywords>First Matter, Negative Theology, Jacob Boehme, Dionysius the Areopagite, Cornelius Agrippa, Raymond Lully, Alchemy, Hermeticism, Creation ex nihilo</keywords>

# CŒLUM TERRÆ; OR, THE MAGICIAN’S HEAVENLY CHAOS,

Learn then to understand that magical axiom: <note>original: "Ex invisible factum est visibile"</note> "From the invisible, the visible was made," for all visible things came out of the invisible God. For he is the wellspring from which all things flow, and the creation was a certain astounding metaphysical birth or delivery. This fine virgin-matter, or chaos, was the "second nature" proceeding from God himself and—if I may say so—the child of the Blessed Trinity. What doctor, then, is he whose hands are fit to touch that subject upon which God himself lays his own spirit when he works? For, verily, so we read: <margin>Genesis, chapter 1</margin> *The Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters.* And can it be expected, then, that I should prostitute <note>To "prostitute" here means to make common or unworthy by exposing it to those who do not value it.</note> this mystery to just anyone, or that I should proclaim it and shout it like street vendors crying their oysters? 

Truly, these considerations, along with others which I will not put to paper for all the world, have made me almost displease my dearest friends, to whom I nonetheless owe a better explanation. Had it been my fortune merely to know this matter as most men do, I might perhaps have been less careful with it. But I have been instructed in all its secret circumstances, which few upon earth understand. I speak this not for any ostentation, but I speak a truth which my conscience knows very well. Let me then, reader, request your patience, for I shall leave this discovery to God, who—if it be his blessed will—can call to you and say: "Here it is, and this is how I work it." 

I would not have spoken all this in my own defense had I not been assaulted (as it were) on this very point, and told to my face that I was bound to reveal all that I knew. For this age looks for dreams and revelations as the accompaniment <note>original: "traine"</note> to their invisible righteousness. I have now sufficiently discussed the matter, and if it is not your fortune to find it by what is written here, yet you cannot be deceived by what I have said; for I have purposely avoided all those terms which might make you mistake any common salts, stones, or minerals for it. 

I advise you, furthermore, to beware of all vegetables and animals; avoid them and every part of them whatsoever. I say this because some ignorant, messy "broilers" <note>original: "sluttish broylers" — "sluttish" meaning careless or disorganized, and "broilers" referring to pseudo-alchemists who merely boiled substances over heat without understanding.</note> are of the opinion that human blood is the true subject. But alas! Is human blood in the bowels of the earth, so that metals should be generated out of it? Or was the world and all that is within it made of human blood as their "first matter"? Surely no such thing. The first matter existed before man and all other creatures whatsoever, for she is the mother of them all. They were made of the first matter, and not the first matter of them. Take heed, then; let no man deceive you. It is totally impossible to reduce any particular thing back to the first matter, or to a sperm <note>In this context, "sperm" refers to the original seed or creative essence of a substance.</note>, without our Mercury <note>The author refers to "Philosophical Mercury," a secret substance used in alchemy, not the common metal.</note>.

<summary>The author explains that the "first matter" of the world is a divine chaos born from God and warns seekers not to look for it in common minerals, plants, or human blood.</summary>
<keywords>First Matter, Chaos, creation, alchemy, Mercury, Genesis, secrecy, metaphysics</keywords>

# AND FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

...and being so reduced, it is not universal, but the particular sperm of its own species, and works no effects except those that agree with the nature of that species; for God has sealed it with a particular idea.

<margin>Anima Magica <note>The magical soul.</note></margin>
Leave those people alone then, who experiment upon human blood in their chemical stones and furnaces <note>original: athanors</note>, or, as Sendivogius <note>original: Sendivow</note> has it, *in fornaculis mirabilibus* (in marvelous furnaces); they will eventually regret their error and sit, without even sackcloth, in the ashes of their own compositions. But I have finished with that; I will now say something about generation and its ways, so that the process of the philosophers regarding this matter may be better understood. You must know that Nature has two extremes, and between them a middle substance, which elsewhere we have called the "middle nature." We have enough examples of this in the creation. The first extreme was that cloud, or darkness, of which we have spoken previously; some call it the "remote matter" and the "invisible chaos," but very improperly, for it was not invisible. This is the Jewish *Ein Sof* <note>original: Ensoph; the Infinite or Divine Origin</note> outwardly, and it is the same as that Orphic Night—

> "O Night! You black nurse of the golden stars."

Out of this darkness, all things that are in this world came, as if out of their fountain or womb <note>original: matrix</note>. Hence comes that position of all famous poets and philosophers, *omnia ex Nocte prodiisse*: "that all things were brought forth out of Night." The middle substance is the water, into which that night or darkness was condensed, and the creatures formed out of the water make up the other extreme. But the magicians, when they speak strictly, will not allow for this last extreme, because Nature does not stop here. Therefore, their philosophy runs like this: Man (they say), in his natural state, is in the middle creation; from this, he must move toward one of two extremes. Either he moves toward corruption, as all men commonly do—for they die and moulder away in their graves—or else toward a spiritual, glorified condition, like Enoch and Elijah, who were translated <note>Taken directly to heaven without dying.</note>. This, they say, is a true extreme, for after it, there is no further change.* Now the magicians, reasoning among themselves as to why the middle creation should be subject to corruption, concluded that the cause and origin of this disease was in the chaos itself; for even that was corrupted and cursed upon the Fall of Man. But examining things further, they found that Nature, in her generations, only matured <note>original: concoct</note> the chaos with a gentle heat; she did not separate the parts and purify each of them individually. Instead, the purities and impurities of the sperm remained together in all her productions; and this domestic enemy...

# CŒLUM TERRÆ ; OR, THE MAGICIAN’S HEAVENLY CHAOS,

...prevailing at last, caused the death of the compound. From this they wisely gathered that to administer vegetables, animals, or minerals as medicine <note>original: physic</note> was mere madness, for even these also had their own impurities and diseases, and required some medicine to cleanse them. Upon this advice, they resolved (God, without any question, being their guide) to practice on the chaos itself; they opened it, purified it, united what they had formerly separated, and fed it with a twofold fire, thick and thin, until they brought it to the immortal extreme and made it a spiritual, heavenly body. This was their medicine; this was their magic. In this performance, they saw the image of that face which Zoroaster calls *Triadis Vultus ante essentiam* <note>The Face of the Triad before essence</note>, etc. They perfectly knew the *secundea* <note>A term likely referring to a secondary spiritual nature or the celestial soul of the world</note>, which contains all things in herself naturally, just as God contains all things in himself spiritually. They saw that the life of all things here below was a thick fire, or fire imprisoned and incorporated in a certain incombustible aerial moisture. They found, moreover, that this fire was originally derived from Heaven, and in this sense Heaven is styled in the oracles,

->*Ignis, Ignis derivatio, et ignis penu.*<-
->*Fire, the derivation of Fire, and the storehouse of Fire.*<-

In a word, they saw with their eyes that Nature was male and female; *Ignis ruber super dorsum ignis candidi* <note>Red fire upon the back of white fire</note>, as the cabalists express it: "A certain fire of a most deep red color, working on a most white, heavy, lustful <note>original: salacious</note> water, which water also is fire inwardly, but outwardly very cold." By this practice, it was manifested to them that God himself was Fire, according to that of Eximidius in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum*, an alchemical assembly of philosophers</note>: *Omnium rerum initium esse Naturam quandam eamque-perpetuam infinitam, omnia foventem coquentemque*. "The beginning of all things (he says) is a certain nature, and that eternal and infinite, nourishing and cooking everything." The truth is, life—which is nothing else but light and heat—proceeded originally from God and applied itself to the chaos, which is elegantly called by Zoroaster, *fons fontium, et fontium cunctorum, matrix continens cuncta*. "The fountain of fountains, and of all fountains, the womb <note>original: matrix</note> containing all things." We see by experience that all individuals live not only by their own heat, but they are preserved by the outward universal heat, which is the life of the great world. Even so, truly, the great world itself lives not altogether by that heat which God has enclosed in its parts, but it is preserved by the surrounding, flowing heat of the Deity; for above the heavens, God is manifested like an infinite burning world of light and fire, so that he

## AND FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

He overlooks all that he has made, and the entire structure stands in his heat and light, just as a man stands here on earth in the sunshine. I say then that the God of Nature employs himself in a perpetual coction <note>a process of boiling or digestion</note>, and this is not only to generate, but to preserve that which has been generated; for his spirit and heat coagulate that which is thin, rarefy that which is too gross, quicken the dead parts, and cherish the cold. There is indeed one operation of heat, whose method is vital and far more mysterious than the rest; those who have a use for it must study it. I have, for my part, spoken all that I intend to speak, and though my book may prove fruitless to many because it is not understood, yet some few may be of such a spirit as to comprehend it: *amplæ mentis ampla flamma* <note>a great flame for a great mind</note>, said the great Chaldean. 

But because I will not leave you without some satisfaction, I advise you to take the Moon of the Firmament, which is of a middle nature. Place her so that every part of her may be in two elements at one and the same time; these elements must also attend her body equally, one no further off and one no nearer than the other. In the regulation of these two, there is a twofold geometry to be observed: natural and artificial. But I may speak no more. The true furnace is a small, simple shell; you may easily carry it in one of your hands. The glass is one, and no more, but some philosophers have used two, and so may you. 

As for the work itself, it is in no way troublesome; a lady may read the *Arcadia* and at the same time attend to this philosophy without disturbing her imagination. For my part, I think women are better suited for it than men, for in such things they are more neat and patient, being used to the minor chemistry of sack-possets <note>a drink of hot milk curdled with ale or wine</note> and other finical sugar-sops. Concerning the effects of this medicine, I shall not say anything at this time; he who desires to know them should read the *Revelation of Paracelsus*, a discourse altogether incomparable and, in very truth, miraculous. 

And here, without any partiality, I shall give my judgment of honest Hohenheim <note>Paracelsus</note>. I find in the rest of his works, and especially where he discusses the stone, a great many false processes, but his doctrine of it in general is very sound. The truth is, he had some pride alongside his irritable temper <note>original: justice of his spleen</note>, and in many places he erred on purpose, not caring what bones he threw before the schoolmen; for he was a pilot of Guadalcana and sailed sometimes in his *Rio de la recreación* <note>River of recreation</note>. 

But I had almost forgotten to tell you that which is all in all, and it is the greatest difficulty in all the art: namely, the Fire. It is a close, airy, circular, bright fire; the philosophers call it their sun, and the glass must stand in the shade. It does not cause the matter to evaporate, not even so much as to sweat; it only digests.

# CŒLUM TERRÆ; OR, THE MAGICIAN’S HEAVENLY CHAOS,

with a still, piercing, vital heat. It is continual, and therefore at last alters the chaos and corrupts it: The proportion and governance of it is very precise, but the best rule to know it by is that of the synod: *facite nè fasianus volet ante insequentem.* <note>“make sure that the pheasant does not fly before the follower”</note> “Let not the bird fly before the fowler;” make it sit while you give fire, and then you are sure of your prey. To conclude, I must tell you the philosophers called this fire their balneum <note>bath</note>, but it is *balneum naturae*, “a natural bath,” not an artificial one; for it is not any kind of water, but a certain subtle, temperate moisture which encompasses the glass and feeds their Sun, or Fire. In a word, without this bath, nothing in the world is generated. Now, so that you may better understand what degree of fire is required for the work, consider the generation of man or any other creature whatsoever. It is not kitchen fire, nor fever, that works upon the sperm in the womb, but a most temperate, moist, natural heat which proceeds from the very life of the mother. 

It is just so here; our matter is a most delicate substance, and tender like animal sperm, for it is almost a living thing—nay, in very truth, it has some small portion of life, for Nature produces some animals out of it. For this very reason, the least violence destroys it and prevents all generation; for if it is overheated for even a few minutes, the white and red sulphurs <note>the active and passive principles in alchemy</note> will never essentially unite and coagulate. On the contrary, if it takes cold for even half an hour, once the work has well begun, it will never result in any good purpose. I speak from my own experience, for I have (as they phrase it) given myself a box on the ear, and that twice or thrice, out of a certain confident negligence, expecting that which I knew well enough could never be. Nature moves not by the theory of men, but by their practice, and surely wit and reason can perform no miracles unless the hands support them. 

Be sure then to know this Fire in the first place, and accordingly be sure to make use of it. But for your better security, I will describe it to you once more. It is a dry, vaporous, humid fire; it goes all around the glass and is both equal and continual. It is restless, and some have called it the white philosophical coal. It is in itself natural, but the preparation of it is artificial: it is a heat of the dead, which is why some call it their unnatural, necromantic fire. It is no part of the matter, nor is it taken out of it; rather, it is an external fire and serves only to stir up and strengthen the inward, oppressed fire of the chaos. But let us hear Nature herself, for thus she speaks in the serious romance of Mehung <note>Jean de Meun, author of the Roman de la Rose</note>.

> *Post putrefactionem sit ipsa generatio, id que per internum incomburbilem calorem, ad argenti vivi frigiditatem* <note>“After putrefaction comes generation itself, and that through an internal incombustible heat, toward the coldness of quicksilver.”</note>

## AND FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.

*calefaciendam, quod tantum equidem patitur, ut tandem cum sulphure suo uniatur. Omne illud uno in vase complexum est, ignis, aer, et aqua videlicet, quæ in terreno suo vase accipio, eademque uno in alembico relinquo; et tum coquo, dissolvo, et sublimo, absque malleo, forcipe, vel lima, sine carbonibus, vapore, igne aut máriæ-balneo, et sophistarum alembicis; cœlestem, namque meum ignem habeo, qui elementatem, prout materia idoneam decentemque formam habere desyderat, excitat.* That is:
> "After putrefaction follows generation, and that because of the inward incombustible sulphur that heats or thickens the coldness and crudities of the quicksilver, which suffers so much from it that at last it is united to the sulphur and made one body with it. All this (namely fire, air, and water) is contained in one vessel; in their earthly vessel, that is, in their gross body or composition, I take them, and then I leave them in one alembic, where I digest, dissolve, and sublime them without the help of hammer, tongs, or file; without coals, smoke, fire, or bath, or the alembics of the sophists. For I have my heavenly fire, which excites or stirs up the elemental one, according as the matter desires a becoming, agreeable form."

Now Nature everywhere is one and the same; therefore she reads the same lesson to Madathan, who, thinking in his ignorance to make the stone without dissolution, receives from her this check: *An tu nunc cochleas, vel cancros cum testis devorare niteris? An non prius à vetustissimo planetarum coquo maturari, et præparari illos oportet?*
> "Do you think (says he) to eat oysters, shells and all? Ought they not first to be opened and prepared by the most ancient cook of the planets?"

With these agrees the excellent Flammel, who, speaking of the Solar and Lunar Mercury and the plantation of the one in the other, has these words: *Sumantur itaque, et noctu, interdiuque assidué supra ignem in alembico foveantur. Non autem ignis carbonarius, vel è ligno confectus, sed clarus pellucidusque ignis sit, non secus ac Sol ipse, qui nunquam plus justo calidus ardensque sed omni tempore ejusdem caloris esse debet.*
> "Take them therefore (says he) and cherish them over a fire in your alembic. But it must not be a fire of coals, nor of any wood, but a bright shining fire, like the sun itself, whose heat must never be excessive, but always of one and the same degree."

This is enough, and too much, for the secret in itself is not great, but the consequences of it are so, which made the philosophers hide it. Thus, reader, you have the outward agent most fully and faithfully described. It is, in truth, a very simple mystery, and if I should tell it openly, it would be ridiculous. However, by this, and not without it, did the magicians unlock the

# CŒLUM TERRÆ; OR, THE MAGICIAN’S HEAVENLY CHAOS,

chaos, and certainly it is no news that an iron key should open a treasury of gold. In this Universal Subject they found the natures of all particulars, and this is signified to us by that maxim: *Qui Proteum novit, adeat Pana.* “He who is familiar with Proteus is on the threshold of the knowledge of Pan.” This Pan is their chaos, or Mercury, which explains Proteus—namely, the particular creatures commonly called individuals. For Pan transforms himself into a Proteus; that is, into all varieties of species: into animals, vegetables, and minerals. For out of the universal Nature, or first matter, all these are made, and Pan has their properties within himself. Hence it is that Mercury is called the interpreter or expositor of inferiors and superiors, under which notion the ancient Orpheus invokes him: “Hear me, O Mercury, you messenger of Jove and son of Maia, the expositor of all things.” Now, as for the birth of this Mercury and its location, I find but few philosophers who mention it. Zoroaster points to it, and that very obscurely, where he speaks of his Jynges or the Ideas in these words:

->*Multæ quidem hæ scandunt lucidos mundos,*<-
->*Insilentes : quarum summitates sunt tres.*<-
->*Subjectum est ipsis principale pratum.*<-

<margin>* Note 20.</margin>
This *pratum*, or meadow of the ideas—a place well known to the philosophers (Flammel calls it their Garden and the Mountain of the Seven Metals; see his Summary, where he describes it most learnedly, for he was instructed by a Jew)—is a certain secret but universal region. One calls it *regio lucis*, “the region of light,” but to the cabalist it is *Nox Corporis*, a term extremely opposite and significant. In short, it is the rendezvous of all spirits, for in this place the ideas are incorporated when they descend from the bright world to the dark one. For your better understanding, you must know that spirits, while they move in Heaven (which is the fire-world), contract no impurities at all, according to Stellatus:

->*Omne quod est supra lunam, æternumque bonumque*<-
->*Esse scias, nec triste aliquid cælestia tangit.*<-

“All,” says he, “that is above the moon is eternal and good, and there is no corruption of heavenly things.” On the contrary, when spirits descend to the elemental matrix and reside in her kingdom, they are blurred with the original leprosy of the matter; for here the curse raves and rules, but in Heaven it is not predominant. To put an end to this point, let us hear the admirable Agrippa state it; this is he between whose lips the truth breathed, and who knew no

<summary>The author explains the alchemical Mercury as a universal chaos (Pan) that transforms into all individual species (Proteus). This "first matter" is located in a secret region known as the "meadow of ideas," where celestial spirits are first incorporated into physical matter.</summary>

another oracle. *Cœlestium vires, dum in se existunt, et à Datore Luminum per sanctas intelligentias, et cœlos influuntur, quousque ad lunam pervenerint : earum influentia bona est, tanquam in primo gradu ; deinde autem quando in subjecto viliori suscipitur, ipsa etiam vilescit.* That is: "the heavenly powers or spiritual essences, while they are in themselves, or before they are united to the matter, and are showered down from the Father of Lights through the holy Intelligences and the Heavens, until they come to the moon: their influence is good, as in the first degree; but when it is received in a corrupt subject, the influence also is corrupted." Thus he says; now the astronomers pretend to a strange familiarity with the stars, and the natural philosophers talk as much: and truly an ignorant man might well think they had been in heaven, and conversed, like Lucian's Menippus, with Jove himself. But, in good earnest, these men are no more eagles than Sancho; their fancies are like his flights in the blanket, and every way as short of the skies. Ask them but where the influences are received, and how; bid them by fair experience prove they are present in the elements, and you have undone them; if you will trust the four corners of a figure, or the three legs of a syllogism, you may; this is all their evidence. Well fare the magicians then, whose art can demonstrate these things, and put the very influences in our hands. Let it be your study to know their Region of Light, and to enter into the treasures thereof, for then you may converse with spirits, and understand the nature of invisible things. Then will appear to you the Universal Subject, and the <term>Two Mineral Sperms</term>, white and red, of which I must speak somewhat, before I make an end.

In the Pythagorean synod, which consisted of seventy philosophers, all masters of the art, it is thus written. *Ignis spissum in aera cadit ; aeris vero spissum, et quod ex igne spisso congregatur, in aquam incidit ; aquæ quoque spissum, et quod ex ignis et aeris spisso coadunatur, in terrâ quiescit. Ista istorum Trium spissitudo in Terra quiescit, inque eâ conjuncta sunt. Ipsa ergo terra omnibus cæteris elementis spissior est, uti palàm apparet et videre est.* That is, "the thickness, or sperm of the fire falls into the air; the thickness or spermatic part of the air, and in it the sperm of the fire, falls into the water; the thickness or spermatic substance of the water, and in it the two sperms of fire and air, fall into the earth, and there they rest, and are conjoined. Therefore the earth itself is thicker than the other elements, as it openly appears, and to the eye is manifest." Remember now what I have told you formerly concerning the earth; what a general hospital it is, how it receives all

<summary>The text explains how celestial influences are pure until they reach the moon and enter corruptible matter, and describes the alchemical process where the "spermatic" essence of the upper elements (fire, air, water) descends to settle in the earth.</summary>

things, not only beasts and vegetables, but proud and glorious man: when death has ruined him, his coarser parts stay here, and know no other home. This "earth to earth" is exactly the doctrine of the Magi; metals (they say) and all things may be reduced into that from which they were made. They speak the absolute truth; it is God’s 
<margin>Gen. ch. 3, ver. 19.</margin>
own principle, and he first taught it to Adam: "Dust you are, and to dust you shall return." But lest any man should be deceived by us, I think it right to inform you that there are two reductions; one is violent and destructive, reducing bodies to their extremes, and properly it is death, or the calcination of the common chemist. The other is vital and generative, resolving bodies into their sperm, or middle substance out of which Nature made them; for Nature does not make bodies immediately from the elements, but from a sperm which she draws out of the elements. I shall explain myself to you by example. An egg is the sperm, or middle substance out of which a chick is engendered, and its moisture is viscous and slimy—a water and yet no water, for such a sperm ought to be. Suppose Dr. Coal—I mean some broiler—had a mind to generate something out of this egg: unquestionably he would first distill it, and that with a fire hot enough to roast the hen that laid it; then would he calcine the *caput mortuum*, and finally produce his nothing. Here you are to observe that bodies are nothing else but coagulated sperm, and he who destroys the body, by consequence, destroys the sperm. Now to reduce bodies into the elements of earth and water, as we have illustrated with the egg, is to reduce them into extremes beyond their sperm; for elements are not sperm, but the sperm is a compound made of the elements, containing in itself all that is required for the frame of the body. Therefore, be well advised before you distill and quarter any particular bodies; for once you have separated their elements, you may never generate anything unless you can make a sperm out of those elements—but that is impossible for man to do; it is the power of God and Nature. Labor then, you who would be accounted wise, to find out our Mercury; thus you shall reduce things to their middle spermatical chaos, but avoid the broiling destruction. This doctrine will spare you the vain task of distillations, if you will only remember this truth: that sperms are not made by separation, but by the composition of elements; and to bring a body into sperm is not to distill it, but to reduce the whole into one thick water, keeping all its parts in their first natural union. But that I may return at last to my former citation of the Synod: all those influences of the elements, being united in one mass, make our sperm or our earth, which is earth and yet no earth. Take it, if you know it,

Here you shall find a miraculous white water, an influence of the Moon, which is the Mother of our Chaos; it rules in two elements—earth and water. After this appears the sperm or influx of the sun, which is its father. It is a living *celestial* fire, incorporated in a thin, oily, *aerial* moisture. It is incombustible, for it is fire itself, and feeds upon fire; and the longer it stays in the fire, the more glorious it grows. These are the two mineral sperms, masculine and *feminine*: if you place them both on their crystalline basis, you have the philosopher’s flying fire-drake, which at the first sight of the sun breathes such a poison that nothing can stand before him. I do not know what more to tell you, unless, following the fashion of some authors, I should give you a phlegmatic description of the whole process—and that I can dispatch in two words. It is nothing else but a continual coction <note>A term for "cooking" or "boiling" in alchemy</note>, the volatile essences ascending and descending, until at last they are fixed according to that excellent *prosopopeia* <note>Personification</note> of the stone.

# CŒLUM TERRÆ ; OR, THE MAGICIAN’S HEAVENLY CHAOS,

nature: The philosophers call it the venerable nature, but among all the pretenders I have not yet found one who could tell me why. Hear me then, so that whenever you attempt this work, it may be with reverence—not like some proud, ignorant doctor, but with less confidence and more care. This chaos has in it the four elements, which of themselves are contrary natures, but the wisdom of God has so placed them that their very order reconciles them. For example, air and earth are adversaries, for one is hot and moist, the other cold and dry. Now to reconcile these two, God placed the water between them, which is a middle nature, or of a mean constitution between both extremes. For she is cold and moist; and as she is cold, she partakes of the nature of the earth, which is cold and dry; but as she is moist, she partakes in the nature of the air, which is hot and moist. Hence it is that air and earth, which are contraries in themselves, agree and embrace one another in the water, as in a middle nature which is proportionate to them both and tempers their extremities. 

But, truly, this remedy does not fully heal the breach; for though the water reconciles two elements like a friendly third party, yet she herself fights with a fourth, namely with the fire. For the fire is hot and dry, but the water is cold and moist, which are clear contraries. To prevent the imbalances of these two, God placed the air between them, which is a substance hot and moist; and as it is hot, it agrees with the fire, which is hot and dry; but as it is moist, it agrees with the water, which is cold and moist; so that by mediation of the air, the other two extremes—namely, fire and water—are made friends and reconciled. Thus you see, as I told you at first, that contrary elements are united by that order and texture in which the wise God has placed them. 

You must now permit me to tell you that this agreement, or friendship, is but partial—a very weak love, cold and skittish; for whereas these principles agree in one quality, they differ in two, as you yourselves may easily calculate. They have great need, therefore, of a stronger and more able mediator to confirm and preserve their weak unity, for upon it depends the very eternity and incorruption of the creature. This blessed cement and balsam is the Spirit of the living God, which some ignorant scribblers have called a quintessence; for this very Spirit is in the chaos, and, to speak plainly, the fire is his throne, for in the fire he is seated, as we have sufficiently told you elsewhere.

<margin>*Anthroposophia Theomagica.* <note>A reference to another work by the author, Thomas Vaughan, meaning "The Magical Wisdom of Humanity."</note></margin>
