<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the elements, reconciled by the Spirit of God, are brought into a harmonious structure, and transitions into a discussion on the relationship between the Soul, the Body, and the Creator.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>AND FIRST MATTER OF ALL THINGS.</header>

...already tend toward union and peace (for God works with Nature, not against her) and brings them at last to a beautiful, specific structure. Now, if you will ask me: where is the Soul—or as the Schoolmen abuse her, the *form*—all this while? What does she do? To this I answer that she is, as all instruments ought to be, subject and obedient to the will of God, awaiting the perfection of her body <note>Note 21.</note>; for it is God who unites her to the body, and the body to her.

Soul and body are the work of God, the one as well as the other. The soul is not the builder of her house; for that which can make a body can also repair it and prevent death. But the soul cannot do this; it is the power and wisdom of God. In a word, to say that the soul formed the body because she is in the body is like saying that the jewel made the casket because the jewel is in the casket, or that the sun made the world because the sun is in the world and cherishes every part of it.

Learn, therefore, to distinguish between agents and their instruments; for if you attribute that to the creature which belongs to the Creator, you bring yourselves into danger of hellfire, for God is a jealous God and will not give His glory to another. I advise my doctors, therefore—both theologians and physicians—not to be too rash in their judgments nor so authoritative in their discourse as I have known some professors of medicine to be, who would correct and undervalue the rest of their brethren when, in truth, they themselves were most shamefully ignorant. It is not ten or twelve years of experience with drugs and syrups that can acquaint a man with the mysteries of God’s creation. "Take this and make a world" and "Take I know not what and make a pill or enema" are very different recipes. We should, therefore, consult our judgment before we venture our tongues, and never speak except when we are sure we understand.

I knew a gentleman who, meeting with an adept philosopher and receiving enough courtesy to be admitted to conversation, followed his first instructions well enough. But when this magician left my friend’s familiar path and began to touch upon and drive the great wheel of Nature, my gentleman immediately "took up the cudgels" and, urging all the authorities that his vain judgment provided for him, overwhelmed this noble philosopher with a most noisy and senseless mockery. It was a fine sight, and worthy of our imitation, to see with what admirable patience the other man received him. But this fellow concluded at last that lead or quicksilver must be the subject, and that Nature worked upon one of the two. To this the adept replied, “Sir, it may be so at this time; but if hereafter I find Nature in those old elements, where..."

"I have sometimes seen her very busy; I shall, at our next meeting, refute your opinion." This was all he said, and it was something more than he actually did. Their next meeting was postponed until the <term>Greek Calends</term> <note>A phrase meaning "never," as the ancient Greeks did not use "calends" in their timekeeping.</note>, for he could never be seen afterward, despite a thousand solicitations. Such talkative, babbling people as this gentleman was—who run to every doctor for his opinion and follow every bird they flush like a spaniel—are not fit to receive these secrets; they must be serious, silent men, faithful to the art, and most faithful to their teachers. We should always remember that doctrine of <term>Zeno</term>: "Nature," said he, "gave us one tongue, but two ears, so that we might hear much and speak little." Therefore, let no man be ready to vomit forth his own shame and ignorance: let him first examine his knowledge, and especially his practice, lest after the experience of a few violent tricks, he presume to judge Nature in her very sober moments.

To conclude: if you do know the <term>First Matter</term>, know also for certain that you have discovered the sanctuary of Nature; there is nothing between you and her treasures but the door—that, indeed, must be opened. Now, if your desire leads you to the practice, consider well within yourself what manner of man you are, and what it is you would do, for it is no small matter. You have resolved within yourself to be a co-operator with the Spirit of the living God and to serve Him in His work of generation. Take care, therefore, that you do not hinder His work; for if your heat exceeds the natural proportion, you have stirred the wrath of the <term>moist Natures</term>, and they will stand up against the <term>central fire</term>, and the central fire against them, and there will be a terrible division in the <term>chaos</term>. But the sweet spirit of peace—the true eternal <term>quintessence</term>—will depart from the elements, leaving both them and you to confusion; nor will He apply Himself to that matter as long as it is in your violent, destroying hands. Take heed, therefore, lest you become a partner with the Devil, for it is the Devil’s design from the beginning of the world to set Nature at variance with herself, so that he may totally corrupt and destroy her. *Nè tu augeas fatum*, "Do not further his designs."

I have no doubt that many men will laugh at this, but, on my soul, I speak nothing but what I have known through very good experience; therefore, believe me. For my own part, it was always my desire to bury these things in silence, or to depict them in shadows, but I have spoken thus clearly and openly out of the affection I bear to some who have deserved much more from me. It is true, I intended at some time to publish a greater work to the world, which I promised in my

->FINIS.<-

And now, my book, let it not stop your flight
That your just author is not a lord or knight.
I can define myself, and have the art
Always to present one face, and always one heart.
But for nine years, some "great ones" cannot see
What they have been, nor do they know what to be.
What though I have no rattles <note>Likely referring to hollow titles or honors.</note> attached to my name,
Do you consider simple honesty to be no fame?
Or are you such a stranger to the times
That you cannot distinguish my fortunes from my crimes?
Go forth, and fear not: some will gladly be
Your learned friends whom I have never seen,
Nor should you fear your welcome: your small price
Cannot ruin them, even though they pay excise;
Your size is not great; it will not much distress
Their empty pockets, and their studies even less.
You are no <term>galleon</term>, as heavy books are,
Which cannot rest anywhere but in a library;
You are a fine and little thing: it may happen
That ladies will buy you for a new romance.
Oh, how I will envy you when you are spread open
In the bright sunshine of their eyes, and read
With breath of amber and lips of rose that lend
Perfumes to your pages that shall never fade:
When from their white hands they shall let you fall
Into their bosoms, which I may not call
Anything of a misfortune; you drop to rest
In a more pleasing place and are more blessed.
There, in some soft silken fold, you shall lie
Hidden like their love, or your own mystery.
Nor should you grieve that your language is not elegant,
For it is not my best, though it is yours.
I could have sent you forth in such a dress
That spring would have seemed a slattern compared to your expression;
Such as might satisfy this rude, unpolished age
And fix the reader’s soul to every page:
But I have used a coarse and simple style,
Because it suits the truth, which should be plain.

Last, my dear book, if anyone looks on you
As if you were three suns, or some great prodigy,
And swears completely that I deride
All other sects just to publish my own pride;
Tell such people they lie, and since they do not love you,
Bid them go learn some common heresy. <note>"High-shoe" typically refers to something rustic, common, or unsophisticated, like the heavy shoes of a laborer.</note>
Nature is not so simple, but she can
Procure a solid reverence from man;
Nor is my pen so lightly feathered that I
Should serve ambition with her majesty.
It is truth that makes me come forth, and having written
This, her short scene, I would not stifle it:
For I have called it my child, and I would rather
See it torn by them than strangled by its father.

# NOTES.

### NOTE 1 (page 5).

This is an important and conspicuous instance of a direct, though veiled, reference to the most exalted phenomena of the ecstatic trance, to which the common magnetic trance of modern psychology is scarcely the threshold or the stepping-stone. The ancient mystics appear to have discovered an arcane process for the elevation of hypnotism by which the divine everlasting *pneuma* was joined for a period to the *psyche*, or sensitive soul, and the spiritual correspondences of the subject were extended in an upward direction, so as to establish an ineffable intercourse with superior forms of existence. This condition of lucidity is unmatched by the operations of mesmerism, which are formed by the intervention and influenced by the special character of another human mind. Now, it must be established as a radical principle, from the true mystic standpoint, that the development of the arch-natural faculties in man can never be accomplished by this process. The creation of the Magus is personal in the strictest sense. “Magnetism between two individuals,” says Éliphas Lévi, “is undoubtedly a marvelous discovery, but to create in one’s self the magnetic condition, to induce one’s own lucidity, and to direct one’s own clairvoyance, is the perfection of magical art.” Those of an impressionable temperament, and especially women, who imagine they can make progress in practical mysticism by the subjection of their individuality to a stronger and positive mind, should learn on the authority of practical mystics that they will not attain their end. Possibly the dangers of ordinary mesmerism in its branches other than healing have been to some extent exaggerated, but it is not an exaggeration to affirm that the many mansions of the mystic house of light are not to be discovered by the exploration of blind avenues.

### NOTE 2 (page 10).

This passage, as will readily occur to the student, is equivalent to an informal statement of the evolutionary hypothesis, and can also be reconciled with the cosmic expirations and inspirations of Para-Brahma. The substantial identity of the first universal matter or chaos with the philosophical chaos which is the root of the universal medicine, and with

<summary>These notes distinguish between common mesmerism and the advanced "ecstatic trance" of ancient mystics, emphasizing that true magical lucidity must be achieved independently. It also links the concept of universal chaos to the evolutionary hypothesis and Eastern cosmology.</summary>
<keywords>ecstatic trance, magnetism, mesmerism, pneuma, psyche, Éliphas Lévi, Magus, clairvoyance, evolution, Para-Brahma, universal medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Notes" section, providing commentary on the "red Adamic earth," the concept of palingenesis, and parallels with modern occult philosophy.</meta>

# NOTES.

### NOTE 2 (page 10).

The red Adamic earth is a point of particular importance. This first matter is still the foundation or heart of things, and is said to be strangely hidden in the "very bosom" of Nature. Its application to man is one part of the "grand secret" of the mystics; its extraction from man is another part, and both branches of the art are among those *magnalia Dei et Naturæ* <note>great wonders of God and Nature</note> which were piously preserved by the adepts, but are now gradually becoming known.

### NOTE 3 (page 13).

The phenomena of vegetable palingenesis, now generally rejected as mythical, are described seriously by several writers of the past. Duchêne states that he was acquainted at Krakow with a Polish doctor who had the ashes of numerous plants separately preserved in vials, and that the heat of an ordinary candle was sufficient to revive the perfect likeness of a rose. Palingenesis, however, was not confined to plants, and there are ghastly details in such writers as Van der Beck concerning the apparitions of moaning phantoms from the fumes of distilled blood.

### NOTE 4 (page 18).

With this fourth essence—the chariot of Nature, the mask of the Almighty, the vestment of the Divine Majesty—the hypothesis of the Astral Light as elaborated by Lévi may be profitably compared. This light he describes in a number of symbolic terms, which are strictly parallel with those I have just cited—for example, "the burning body of the Holy Ghost."

### NOTE 5 (page 21).

Numerous true recipes for the universal medicine are given in mystical physics, and all in their literal sense have as much value as alchemical formulas. Such mystifications were often used by even the highest adepts, not excluding "the dark disciple of the more dark Libanius Gallius," the illustrious Trithemius, who has left us the following process:—

->Reduce to a powder.<-

Dose:—5 gr. 859 milligr. taken morning and evening in brodium <note>broth</note> or wine during the first month; during the second month, in the morning only; during the third month, three times a week, and so from then on through life. It strengthens the stomach, clears the brain, preserves the sight, invigorates memory, and prevents epilepsy and apoplexy.

### NOTE 6 (page 29).

The Great Arcanum, according to Eliphas Lévi, is concerned with the mystery of universal generation; the "Doctrine and Ritual of Transcendental Magic" contains many important references to the mysticism of the Garden and Tree. Students will be aware that the Pentagram is the sign of the Microcosmos, or Man, and by considering the nature of the Terrestrial Paradise of Man, and the tree which is in the center of that, the Hermetic Doctrine of Analogy will lead them upward by several singular speculations to the secrets of psychic generation.

### NOTE 7 (page 33).

The original edition of *Anthroposophia Theomagica* is decorated with a portrait of Cornelius Agrippa, which has been judged unnecessary to reproduce here.

### NOTE 8 (page 36).

The hypothesis of the astral body, and the role which it plays in the phenomena of apparitions, is now so well known that it hardly requires explanation, but a full and synthetic account will be found by those who desire it in the third division of "The Mysteries of Magic."

### NOTE 9 (page 51).

This passage concerning a "certain chain" in Nature is of high mystical importance. It contains an explanatory theory of the inter-communion of the several worlds of spirits, and of the possibility of our establishing, even in this life, an ecstatic connection with other forms of existence. This "certain chain" is the Jacob’s Ladder whose symbolism is explained elsewhere in the writings of Eugenius. The union of the

# NOTES.

individual spirit with the universal God-consciousness, if not actually declared in this passage, is at least analogically implied, and a secret of spiritual procreation or multiplication is also darkly hinted at. Those who are resolved to attempt the practical application of mystic principles should apply themselves with particular devotion to the philosophical expositions of this ineffable union which are to be found in the writings of adepts. They will also do well to seek with due discrimination for insights in the works of orthodox Christian mystics, who were undoubtedly in possession of the keys of the process, though they do not seem to have unlocked the most secret doors, and whose principles were developed with more clarity than those of the professed expositors of the mysteries. There is much to be learned from the treatise of Saint Bonaventura, entitled *Itinerarium Mentis ad Deum*, who borrowed from Trismegistus, and seems to have been on the track of the Great Arcana.

->NOTE 10 (page 53).<-

There is one substantial ground for the hope that consistent and painstaking students of the mystics may yet penetrate the “luminous obscurities” of <term>Hermetic allegory</term>, and that ground is the great additional clarity which characterizes the expositions of the highest <term>pneumatic mysteries</term>, as compared with those upon subjects which, however strange and marvelous, are of indefinitely minor importance to the true soul-seeker. The passages which occasion this remark, and to which this note has reference, are a practically direct explanation of the significance of the <term>magical duad</term> in the three intelligible worlds, and should be marked for particular investigation; and the <term>hypostatic union</term> of the soul with God is controversially based on analogies alleged to exist in the occult principles of the natural world.

->NOTE 11 (page 54).<-

The evolution of the whole <term>physical cosmos</term> out of a single <term>homogeneous substance</term> of infinite thinness is a hypothesis which has found favor with the scientists of today; but how motion and differentiation originated in this homogeneous substance has been a serious difficulty for the theorists. Here in <term>mystic physics</term> we find the same doctrine, that “the origin of all things is one thing,” that “there is but one matter out of which are found so many different shapes and constitutions,” while the difficulty which has baffled materialism is overcome by recourse to the postulate of “a <term>discerning spirit</term>,” which actuates this substance—that divine, intellectual agent which is always ultimately inevitable in any intelligent solution of the great problem of existence. It is worth noting that the fundamental distinction between matter and mind which has serious philosophical obstacles is practically destroyed by this hypothesis in its mystical aspect, which may be briefly epitomized as follows: There is but one substance infinitely differentiated in the universe.

<summary>This page discusses the mystical union of the individual spirit with God-consciousness and the Hermetic theory that the entire physical universe evolved from a single homogeneous substance guided by a discerning spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Notes" section, transitioning from the discussion of the hypostatic union and the magical duad to an exploration of the "Virgin-Spirit" and historical polemics between Henry More and Thomas Vaughan.</meta>

# NOTES.

### NOTE 12 (pages 56 and 71).

The passages considered in this note lack an apparent connection, but I have a purpose in combining them that is not at odds even with their obvious meaning. Regarding the "pure white Virgin," the "Bride of God and of the Stars," I can do no better than to quote an important passage from the late Edward Vaughan Kenealy’s *Book of God*. While I personally consider this work to have been written in the interests of a fraudulent theory, it and its many sequels constitute a mine of theosophy, mythology, and legend that is scarcely surpassed by *Anacalypsis* or Cudworth’s *Intellectual System*.

> "The ancients did not suppose God, though One, to be alone in solitary and gloomy magnificence, as modern ascetics do. . . . Although none could share the glory and surpassing majesty of the supreme heaven with HIM, they nevertheless declared that He was perpetually surrounded by other gods of light, beauty, purity, and divinity: immortal in their essence, because it emanated from the Most High; yet all proceeding in a fiery stream from Him; and all alike dependent upon His laws, as they were encompassed by His love. Chief among these—preeminent in wisdom, loveliness, and all that is essentially celestial and most pure—they held one Divine Nature to be. . . . The golden imagination of the Past exhausted itself in describing the matchless glory of this exalted Being. She was the Virgin-Spirit of most inexpressible loveliness; the <term>Logos</term>, the <term>Protogonos</term>; the <term>Mimra-Daya</term>, or Word of God, by whose intermediate agency the whole spiritual and material Universe was developed, fashioned, beautified, and preserved."

Now, there is an internal and spiritual Virgin in every person, through whom they can work upward toward Divinity and can ascend to the invisible elements of their own undying <term>Pneuma</term>. This <term>Pneuma</term> corresponds in the <term>microcosmos</term> to the uncaused God, just as the <term>psychal Virgin</term> corresponds to the "Virgin-Spirit." The <term>Psyche</term> is thus the very key and root of the divine mysteries, and through her, in the mystical doctrine of <term>pneumatology</term>, the progress to those powers and capacities described on page 70 is achieved.

### NOTE 13 (page 85).

The attempts referred to here were from the pen of the illustrious Platonist, Henry More, who in the year 1650 published, under the name of *Alazonomastix Philalethes*, some *Observations upon Anthroposophia Theomagica and Anima Magica Abscondita*. Two replies were issued by *Eugenius*—*The Man-Mouse taken in a Trap and tortured to Death for gnawing the Margins of Eugenius Philalethes*, 1650, octavo, and *The Second Wash, or the Moore scoured once more*, 1651, octavo. Both writers resorted to the insults common in that period, but Thomas Vaughan proved superior to his opponent in his wealth of abusive epithets. He has been called an irritable alchemist using <term>Billingsgate</term> <note>Coarse or abusive language, named after the London fish market.</note> phraseology; but the controversy that earned him this fitting description is of no interest to the modern student of mysticism.

<summary>This page discusses the concept of the "Virgin-Spirit" as a divine intermediary and an internal spiritual guide within the human soul. It also details a 17th-century literary feud between the Platonist Henry More and the alchemist Thomas Vaughan.</summary>

<keywords>Virgin-Spirit, Logos, Pneuma, Psyche, pneumatology, Henry More, Thomas Vaughan, Eugenius Philalethes, Edward Vaughan Kenealy, mysticism, alchemical controversy</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Notes" section, providing commentary on Hermetic symbolism, ceremonial magic, and the spiritual interpretation of alchemical processes as discussed by Eugenius Philalethes (Thomas Vaughan).</meta>

# NOTES.

### NOTE 14 (page 90).

Believers in the inherent magical virtues of certain signs should take note of this passage, which indiscriminately denounces all ceremonial magic as a gross and material misconception of Hermetic symbolism. Eugenius Philalethes had, however, transcended—at least intellectually—the common level of practical magic, which, as he probably never attempted it, he may quite possibly have misjudged. No sequence of rites and ceremonies may possess an intrinsic virtue, but they educate and direct the imagination as well as the will; though they are not to be counted as factors in psychic progress, they are not to be denounced as useless in their own sphere.

### NOTE 15 (page 92).

The reunion of <term>Malchuth</term> with the <term>Ilan</term> seems to have been accomplished by the mystics so far as they were individually concerned, and the true method of this reunion is one of the secrets which the modern mystic should set himself to recover.

### NOTE 16 (page 115).

It is needless to say that this story, along with that of the inscription on Mount Horeb, is in all probability mythical. I have been personally unable to discover anything concerning it, and as no mystical interest attaches to either, the reservation of the reader’s judgment will not involve much difficulty or disappointment.

### NOTE 17 (page 136).

In the search for the true process of the spiritual *magnum opus*, the analogical lines of physical alchemy should be rigorously followed, as I have elsewhere stated. The intimate connection of both operations is shown in the passage to which this note is made, and by which it would appear that the process with metals is simply a digression from the process of the mystic medicine. But the medicine itself is pneumatic <note>spiritual or relating to the soul/spirit</note>, and the search for the medicine in its highest sense is the search of the mind after Psyche, of the mind united to Psyche after its own Spirit, and of the hypostatized triplicity after the arch-hypostatic union of the whole man with God.

### NOTE 18 (page 141).

If this spiritual and glorified condition be in any way possible for the individual, it is possible in some way for the human race. If so, it is the end of evolution, and I hope in a forthcoming work—*AZOTH, OR THE STAR IN THE EAST*—to show how this end may be accomplished.

### NOTE 19 (page 142).

At the beginning of *Anthroposophia Theomagica*, this chaos is described as a substance secreted in the very bosom of Nature, and is identified

with the Adamic earth. It is evident from the context that the purification of this chaos described in the passage to which these remarks are attached involved an experimental investigation into the substance of life. Of this life, the chaos is the first envelope—a point which is sufficient to show the extent to which the philosophers claim to have carried their investigations.

### NOTE 20 (page 146).

In this connection, it should be noted that a certain *Nox Corporis* would be involved in the development of the mystic trance, and this night is apparently the entrance to the *Regio Lucis*.

### NOTE 21 (page 151).

This expectation will be commonly identified with the "firm hope of a glorious resurrection," but it also allows for another interpretation. The unceasing yearning of the universal soul of humanity toward a more perfect environment survives the faith in resurrection and does not depend on it, and her whole physical environment is, in a sense, the soul's body. This desire for a better life, which takes a thousand shapes, may be viewed as a consciousness of the tendency and end of evolution; and evolution, as interpreted by mystics, promises that spiritualized perfection of the body which alone the soul expects.
